  \setglossarystyle{list}% %DIF > 
  \setlist[description]{itemsep=0.15ex,parsep=0.15ex,topsep=0pt} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
  \renewcommand*{\glossentry}[2]{% %DIF > 
    \mainmatterglossaryfalse %DIF > 
    \begingroup %DIF > 
      \def\debugglshypernumber####1{% %DIF > 
        \checkmainmatterlocations{####1}% %DIF > 
      }% %DIF > 
      \let\@xp@glshypernumber\debugglshypernumber %DIF > 
      ##2% %DIF > 
    \endgroup %DIF > 
    \ifmainmatterglossary %DIF > 
      \glsxtrifhasfield*{alias}{##1} %DIF > 
      { %DIF > 
        \item %DIF > 
%DIF -------
      }
%DIF 62a198-207
      {% %DIF > 
        \item %DIF > 
        \glsentryitem{##1}% %DIF > 
        \glstarget{##1}{\glossentryname{##1}}% %DIF > 
        \space %DIF > 
        \glossentrydesc{##1}% %DIF > 
      } %DIF > 
    \fi %DIF > 
  }% %DIF > 
} %DIF >

  \ifcsname symfmt@#1\endcsname %DIF > 
    % formatting command: ignore it %DIF > 
  \else %DIF > 
    \ifcsname symgreek@#1\endcsname %DIF > 
      \csname symgreek@#1\endcsname %DIF > 
    \else %DIF > 
      00#1% %DIF > 
    \fi %DIF > 
  \fi %DIF > 
} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
% ============================================================ %DIF > 
% Sort entire key %DIF > 
% ============================================================ %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
\def\sym@sortaux#1-#2\@nil{% %DIF > 
  \ifcsname symfmt@#1\endcsname %DIF > 
    % skip formatting component %DIF > 
  \else %DIF > 
    \sym@sortcomponent{#1}% %DIF > 
    \ifx\relax#2\relax %DIF > 
    \else %DIF > 
      -% %DIF > 
    \fi %DIF > 
  \fi %DIF > 
  \ifx\relax#2\relax %DIF > 
  \else %DIF > 
    \sym@sortaux#2\@nil %DIF > 
  \fi %DIF > 
} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
\newcommand{\mysortkey}[1]{% %DIF > 
  \sym@sortaux#1-\@nil %DIF > 
} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
% ============================================================ %DIF > 
% First actual symbol %DIF > 
% ============================================================ %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
\def\getFirstChar#1#2\@nil{#1} %DIF > 
% Wrapper macro to make syntax cleaner %DIF > 
\newcommand{\firstletter}[1]{\getFirstChar#1\@nil} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
\def\sym@firstaux#1-#2\@nil{% %DIF > 
  \ifcsname symfmt@#1\endcsname %DIF > 
    \sym@firstaux#2\@nil %DIF > 
  \else %DIF > 
    \ifcsname symgreek@#1\endcsname %DIF > 
      #1% %DIF > 
    \else %DIF > 
      \firstletter{#1}% %DIF > 
    \fi %DIF > 
  \fi %DIF > 
} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
\newcommand{\mysymgroup}[1]{% %DIF > 
  \sym@firstaux#1-\@nil %DIF > 
} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
% ============================================================ %DIF > 
% Glossary entry %DIF > 
% ============================================================ %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
\renewcommand{\glsxtrsetaliasnoindex}{% %DIF > 
    \glsxtrindexaliased %DIF > 
    \setkeys{glslink}{noindex}% %DIF > 
} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
\NewDocumentCommand{\newsym}{m m m o}{% %DIF > 
  \ifglsentryexists{sym-#1}{% %DIF > 
    \PackageError{glossary-setup}{Duplicate symbol key `#1' (label `sym-#1') in \string\newsym}{% %DIF > 
      Remove or rename one of the duplicate \string\newsym definitions.\MessageBreak %DIF > 
      The symbol label `sym-#1' has already been defined. %DIF > 
%DIF -------
    }%
%DIF 68a485-504
  }{}% %DIF > 
  \edef\newsym@sortkey{\mysortkey{#1}}% %DIF > 
  \IfNoValueTF{#4}{% %DIF > 
    \newglossaryentry{sym-#1}{ %DIF > 
      name={\ensuremath{#2}}, %DIF > 
      category={symbol}, %DIF > 
      sort={\newsym@sortkey}, %DIF > 
      type={sym-\mysymgroup{#1}}, %DIF > 
      description={#3}}% %DIF > 
  }{% %DIF > 
    \newglossaryentry{sym-#1}{ %DIF > 
      name={\ensuremath{#2}}, %DIF > 
      category={symbol}, %DIF > 
      sort={\newsym@sortkey}, %DIF > 
      type={alias-sym-\mysymgroup{#1}}, %DIF > 
      description={#3}, %DIF > 
      alias={sym-#4}}% %DIF > 
  }% %DIF > 
} %DIF > 
\makeatother %DIF > 
%DIF -------

    \newsym{p-prime-0}{p^{'}_0}{Normalized price coefficient for constant capital and operational costs that do not scale with design}[p-parent] %DIF > 
    \newsym{T-s}{T_s}{Draft of spar}[T-parent] %DIF > 
    \newsym{F-max}{F_\mathrm{max}}{Peak instantaneous force that the generator is capable of producing}[A-A-max-parent] %DIF > 
    \newsym{P-max}{P_\mathrm{max}}{Maximum allowable power at the generator electrical port across all sea states}[A-A-max-parent] %DIF > 
\newsym{overline-P-elec}{\overline{P}_{\mathrm{elec}}}{Annual average electrical power generation} %DIF > 
\newsym{p}{p}{Instantaneous time-domain power at any port} %DIF > 
\newsym{p-pk-VI}{p_{\mathrm{pk},VI}}{Peak power over a wave period at the generator electrical port in a given sea state} %DIF > 
\newsym{F-p}{F_p}{\glsxtrshort{acr-pto} force on a single hydrodynamic degree of freedom} %DIF > 
\newsym{F-heave-mult}{F_{\mathrm{heave},\mathrm{mult}}}{Tuning multiplier for heave forces} %DIF > 
\newsym{xi-f}{\xi_f}{Time domain vertical position of the float} %DIF > 
\newsym{xi-s}{\xi_s}{Time domain vertical position of the spar} %DIF > 
\newsym{xi-lin}{\xi_{\mathrm{lin}}}{Maximum acceptable body displacement to maintain linear hydrodynamic assumptions} %DIF > 
\newsym{xi-slam}{\xi_\mathrm{slam}}{General slamming amplitude limit} %DIF > 
\newsym{h-fs-up}{h_{fs,\mathrm{up}}}{Allowable displacement of the float to move upwards relative to the spar} %DIF > 
\newsym{h-fs-down}{h_{fs,\mathrm{down}}}{Allowable displacement of the float to move downwards relative to the spar} %DIF > 
\newsym{N-harmonics}{N_{\mathrm{harmonics}}}{Number of harmonics used in \glsxtrshort{acr-meem} for all fluid regions} %DIF > 
\newsym{f}{f}{Generic scalar function} %DIF > 
\newsym{D-d-min}{D_{d,\mathrm{min}}}{Minimum permissible damping plate diameter} %DIF > 
\newsym{T-f-2}{T_{f,2}}{Draft of float, measured at inner diameter} %DIF > 
\newsym{h-f}{h_f}{Float height, including both above and below water} %DIF > 
\newsym{h-s}{h_s}{Spar height, including both above and below water} %DIF > 
\newsym{h-1-stiff-d}{h_{1,\mathrm{stiff},d}}{Height of the larger damping plate stiffener} %DIF > 
\newsym{h-d}{h_d}{Damping plate thickness} %DIF > 
\newsym{h-stiff-f}{h_{\mathrm{stiff},f}}{Height of float structural stiffeners} %DIF > 
\newsym{GM}{\overline{GM}}{Distance from center of gravity (G) to metacenter (M)} %DIF > 
\newsym{V-f-struct}{V_{f,\mathrm{struct}}}{Structural volume of the float} %DIF > 
\newsym{V-s-struct}{V_{s,\mathrm{struct}}}{Structural volume of the spar} %DIF > 
\newsym{T-period}{T}{Period of equivalent regular wave} %DIF > 
\newsym{T-struct}{T_\mathrm{struct}}{100-year (design storm) wave period used for structural load cases} %DIF > 
\newsym{BW}{BW}{Half-power bandwidth of the \glsxtrshort{acr-jpd} of a given location} %DIF > 
\newsym{H-s}{H_s}{Significant wave height of irregular waves} %DIF >

\def\DeclareFigureOptions#1#2#3{% %DIF > 
    \if\relax\detokenize{#2}\relax %DIF > 
        \define@key{Gin}{figopts@\detokenize{#1}@twocol}[true]{}% %DIF > 
    \else %DIF > 
        \define@key{Gin}{figopts@\detokenize{#1}@twocol}[true]{\setkeys{Gin}{width=#2}}% %DIF > 
    \fi %DIF > 
    \if\relax\detokenize{#3}\relax %DIF > 
        \define@key{Gin}{figopts@\detokenize{#1}@onecol}[true]{}% %DIF > 
    \else %DIF > 
        \define@key{Gin}{figopts@\detokenize{#1}@onecol}[true]{\setkeys{Gin}{width=#3}}% %DIF > 
    \fi %DIF > 
} %DIF >

\newsym{f-penalty}{f_\mathrm{pen}}{Penalty function to ensure feasibility of a candidate reflection coefficient in \glsxtrshort{acr-qcqp}} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
% candidate points %DIF > 
\newsym{mathcal-C}{\mathcal{C}}{Set of candidate points for analytical \glsxtrshort{acr-qcqp}} %DIF > 
\newsym{mathcal-C-cl}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{cl}}}{Set of candidate points for analytical \glsxtrshort{acr-qcqp} representing the closest point to the origin of each circle} %DIF > 
\newsym{mathcal-C-cl-oval}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{oval}}}{Set of candidate points for analytical \glsxtrshort{acr-qcqp} representing the closest point to the origin of each oval} %DIF > 
\newsym{mathcal-C-int}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{int}}}{Set of candidate points for analytical \glsxtrshort{acr-qcqp} representing the intersections of each circle} %DIF > 
\newsym{mathcal-C-int-oval-circ}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{oval-circ}}}{Set of \glsxtrshort{acr-qcqp} candidate points at oval-circle intersections} %DIF > 
 %DIF > 
% qcqp weights %DIF > 
\newsym{pi-1-mu-nu-beta}{\varpi_{1\mu\nu\beta}}{First transformed weighting term for the $\mu,\nu$ constraint pair in sea state $\beta$} %DIF > 
\newsym{pi-2-mu-nu-beta}{\varpi_{2\mu\nu\beta}}{Second transformed weighting term for the $\mu,\nu$ constraint pair in sea state $\beta$} %DIF > 
 %DIF >

\usetikzlibrary{shapes.geometric}
\newcommand{\Stars}[2][fill=black,draw=black]{
    \begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=-0.35em,#1]
    \foreach \X in {1,...,3}
    {\pgfmathsetmacro{\xfill}{min(1,max(1+#2-\X,0))}
    \path (\X*1.1em,0) 
    node[star,draw,star point height=0.25em,minimum size=1em,inner sep=0pt,
    path picture={\fill (path picture bounding box.south west) 
    rectangle  ([xshift=\xfill*1em]path picture bounding box.north west);}]{};
    }
    \end{tikzpicture}
}
%DIF 179a1915-1919

\cortext[1]{Corresponding author}

\begin{abstract}
Wave energy converters (WECs) require system-level techno-economic analysis to balance power production, cost, and survivability,
yet existing simulation tools are often either too computationally intensive for large-scale optimization
or too narrow in disciplinary scope to \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{support integrated design studies}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{capture realistic viability criteria}\DIFaddend .
This work presents MDOcean, a novel modular WEC simulation framework developed for rapid early-stage design exploration, parametric analysis, and multidisciplinary optimization.
MDOcean integrates \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{analytical and semi-analytical models of }\DIFaddend hydrodynamics, dynamics, structures, and economics within a computationally efficient architecture\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{based on analytical and semi-analytical methods that substantially reduce }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, substantially reducing }\DIFaddend runtime while maintaining \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{near-numerical }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{high }\DIFaddend accuracy. 

The framework includes a mesh-free eigenfunction-based linear hydrodynamic solver, a quasi-linearized frequency-domain dynamics engine capable of modeling drag and saturation nonlinearities, a structural sizing module incorporating \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{realistic yield, ultimate}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{yield}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{buckling, storm, and fatigue }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{fatigue, ultimate, and buckling }\DIFaddend design criteria, and a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{simplified }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{simple }\DIFaddend cost model for techno-economic assessment.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Particular emphasis is placed on the quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral dynamics formulation , which extends prior frequency-domain constraint-handling approaches through a unified describing-function and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The }\acrlong{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{optimal control formulation receives particular emphasis, unifying describing functions with an }\DIFaddend analytical quadratically-constrained quadratic program\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(QCQP) framework}\DIFdelend .
This formulation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{efficiently treats nonlinearities and constraints while preserving compatibility with optimization workflows and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{preserves the efficiency and intuition of standard }\DIFaddend frequency-domain \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{analysis techniques commonly used in WEC design}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{techniques while handling nonlinearities and dynamic constraints}\DIFaddend .

Validation and benchmarking demonstrate that MDOcean's \resultsAOR[simRuntime] runtime is orders of magnitude faster than leading WEC simulation tools while maintaining agreement with higher-fidelity baselines to within a few percent in most cases.
Beyond computational performance, the framework also provides insight into limiting behaviors, scaling laws, subsystem interactions, and key tradeoffs governing WEC design and techno-economic performance.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{MDOcean is released as }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Limiting assumptions include regular waves and point absorber archetype.
The MDOcean software is released }\DIFaddend open-source \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{software to support accelerated }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{to accelerate }\DIFaddend WEC research, design, and optimization \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{toward the clean energy transition.
}\DIFaddend \end{abstract}

\begin{graphicalabstract}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/from-matlab/graphical_abstract_modeling.pdf}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/graphical_abstract_modeling.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/graphical_abstract_modeling.pdf}
}\DIFaddend \end{graphicalabstract}

\begin{highlights}
\item Presents MDOcean, an open-source semi-analytical wave energy converter simulation.
\item Integrates hydrodynamics, powertrain, structures, \& economics for coupling intuition.

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \item Quasi-linear frequency domain dynamics model captures nonlinearities such as generator force saturation and drag.
    \item Derives analytical constrained optimal control solution for underactuated wave-to-wire multi-port model respecting effort, flow, and power limits.
    \item Analytical stiffened-plate structural model assesses stress and stability failure modes under fatigue and storm survival loadcases.
\else
    \item Derives analytical frequency-domain constrained optimal control with nonlinearities.
\fi

\item Achieves \resultsAOR[simRuntime] runtime, 10-1000x faster than existing tools.
\item Verifies <10\% power \& amplitude error for standard reference model 3 design.

\end{highlights}

\begin{keywords}
marine renewable energy \sep
semi-analytical hydrodynamics \sep
linearized pseudo-spectral optimal control \sep
structural survivability \sep
techno-economic modeling \sep
model validation \sep
computational benchmarking
\end{keywords}

 \DIFaddend \newpage \section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}
\subsection{Wave Energy Overview}
%(Why WECs, in renewable energy context):

% stronger opening sentence
The global climate crisis requires a transition to carbon-free energy sources such as ocean wave energy.
Ocean waves have higher consistency, predictability, and energy density than other renewable energy sources such as wind and solar, while the temporal complementarity of waves with other resources can improve grid resilience and capacity adequacy, decrease energy prices, and decrease requirements for energy storage and balancing power \citep{akdemir_opportunities_2023,bhattacharya_timing_2021,pennock_temporal_2022}.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Wave energy converters (WECs)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-wec}\DIFaddend , the devices that harness and convert this energy, could provide both electricity for the grid at large scale and power for smaller offshore technologies like aquaculture, desalination, and marine sensing \citep{livecchi_powering_2019}.
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
Despite the potential advantages of \glspl{acr-wec} , a significant decrease in the cost per unit energy production is required before the technology can be deployed at large scales. %\hl{(Add a more specific number to show that the LCOE needs to come down an order of magnitude).} Meanwhile, a lack of design convergence (...)
Thus, a design process that emphasizes techno-economic viability from an early stage is necessary.
\else
    However, \glspl{acr-wec} require substantial cost reduction before large-scale deployment, motivating a design process that emphasizes techno-economic viability from an early stage.
\fi

%(Why multidisciplinary):
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    However, the presence of strong interdisciplinary coupling complicates the application of typical design and optimization techniques to \glspl{acr-wec} .
    The powertrain, controller, bulk dimensions, hydrodynamic shape, and structural thicknesses must be selected to balance the complex tradeoff between power production and cost while ensuring survivability and practical feasibility.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Concentrating and automating the design decision-making efforts of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Accurately capturing all major relationships and requirements that drive }\DIFaddend structural, control, hydrodynamic\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{s}\DIFdelend  , and electrical \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{engineers while accurately capturing all major relationships and requirements is challenging, not to mention }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{design is technically challenging and }\DIFaddend computationally prohibitive.
    Nonetheless, leveraging the coupling between subsystems and across technical domains can produce large gains in techno-economic viability.
    Automating this process through optimization allows \gls{acr-wec} designers to more quickly and systematically explore tradeoffs of major design decisions, and could eventually \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{allow standardized }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{standardize }\DIFaddend comparisons between different \gls{acr-wec} architectures.
    Thus, performing a system-level \gls{acr-wec} optimization that simultaneously considers the coupled techno-economic goals, design decisions, and requirements is highly advantageous.
\else
    The strong interdisciplinary coupling of between powertrain, controller, bulk dimensions, hydrodynamic shape, and structural thicknesses complicates conventional \gls{acr-wec} design techniques.
    Systematically exploring these coupled tradeoffs requires a fast multidisciplinary simulation that can be embedded in an optimization loop.
\fi
%\Cref{fig:disciplines} is a non-exhaustive outline of the most common disciplines in a wave energy converter, and the level and direction of coupling between them.

\subsection{\gls{acr-wec} System Modeling: State of the Art and Gaps}
\label{sec:lit}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    Existing models of \glspl{acr-wec} in the literature vary widely in their disciplinary scope, fidelity, and suitability for optimization.
    In the wave energy industry, multidisciplinary modeling is common due to the imperative of considering all design elements, although different disciplines are often modeled separately and sequentially rather than concurrently.
    \citet{trueworthy_wave_2020} provides a critical summary of the \gls{acr-wec} design process and shares survey results from 25 \gls{acr-wec} designers and developers.
    50\% of respondents design all subsystems concurrently.
    The others tend to start with the \gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{shape and }\gls{acr-pto} , then move to the controller and moorings, and finish with the power transportation system.
    \citet{trueworthy_wave_2020} concludes that concurrent design has promise to take advantage of subsystem interactions and is an under-utilized technique worthy of further study, emphasizing the importance of incorporating all design requirements at an early stage.
\else
    Existing \gls{acr-wec} models vary widely in disciplinary scope, fidelity, and suitability for optimization.
In industry, multidisciplinary modeling is common but typically sequential rather than concurrent; a survey of 25 \gls{acr-wec} designers \citep{trueworthy_wave_2020} found that only 50\% design all subsystems concurrently, and identified concurrent design as an under-utilized technique worthy of further study.
\fi

The academic state-of-the-art typically focuses on modeling and optimizing one or a few disciplines at a time, with the most common being hydrodynamics, dynamics, and controls.
Major modeling choices include the representation (or lack thereof) of the \gls{acr-wec} 's hydrodynamics, dynamics, controls, mooring, powertrain, structures, and economic viability, as well as the sea states considered.

% figure of taxonomy
\Cref{fig:model-taxonomy} provides a simplified taxonomy of the various \gls{acr-wec} system modeling approaches used in the literature,
and \Cref{tab:lit} compares the most relevant studies along this taxonomy.


% table summary
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Major modeling choices include the representation (or lack thereof) of the WEC's hydrodynamics, dynamics, controls, mooring, powertrain, structures, and economic viability, as well as the sea states considered.
    }\DIFdelend After examining the overall trends\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:lit}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend , each table entry will be described in more detail.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Point absorbers (PA) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \Acrfull{acr-pa} \DIFaddend seem to be the most common device architecture, though most major device types are represented, and several studies optimize arrays of multiple devices.
    Nearly all model hydrodynamics and power production in some way.
    Hydrodynamic modeling is typically performed with the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{boundary element method (BEM)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-bem}\DIFaddend , occasionally with semi-analytical models like \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{matched eigenfunction expansion method (MEEM)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-meem}\DIFaddend , and rarely with high fidelity \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{computational fluid dynamics (CFD)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-cfd}\DIFaddend , fitting a model to numerical or experimental data (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{FIT}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-fit}\DIFaddend ), or \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{algebraic approximations (ALG)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-alg}\DIFaddend .
    Nonlinear forces such as drag are most often linearized and included as an additional \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{linear damping (LD)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-ld}\DIFaddend , though other methods exist including quadratic representation (Q), \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{describing function (DF)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-df}\DIFaddend , strip-theory integration of Morison's equation (M), and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{CFD}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-cfd}\DIFaddend .
    Dynamic modeling is frequently performed both in the frequency domain (F) and time domain (T), with a handful of studies using extensions of frequency domain techniques (F+) to handle constraints and nonlinearities that are typically only handled in the time domain, and a few using the newer \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{pseudo-spectral method (PS)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-ps}\DIFaddend .
    A range of controllers are considered, including proportional-integral / reactive (PI), proportional / pure damping (P), their extensions to incorporate constraints (P+/PI+), nonlinear structured (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{NL}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-nl}\DIFaddend ), and unstructured (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{UNS}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-uns}\DIFaddend ).

    Optimizations that incorporate powertrain, structures, and economics are less ubiquitous but still common.
    When a discipline is included, its fidelity can vary widely.
    For instance, some studies model the \gls{acr-pto} only with an \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{efficiency (EF)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-eff}\DIFaddend , while others model the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO dynamics(DY)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{dynamics}\DIFaddend .
    Structural models are split relatively evenly between analytical models for stress as a function of dimensions (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{AN}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-an}\DIFaddend ), calculating loads without considering dimension-dependent stress (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{LO}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-lo}\DIFaddend ), and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{finite element analysis (FEA)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-fea}\DIFaddend .
    Cost models most often use a geometric cost proxy (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{GEO}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-geo}\DIFaddend ) such as hull volume or area.
    Some estimate costs of the structural material (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{STR}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-str}\DIFaddend ), power take-off (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-pto}\DIFaddend ), mooring (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{MOOR}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-moor}\DIFaddend ), or include a comprehensive cost model (FULL).
    The use of a mooring model is relatively rare, with four studies modeling the mooring system physical design (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{DE}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-de}\DIFaddend ) and one modeling its dynamic properties (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{DY}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-dyn}\DIFaddend ).
    It is most common to simulate irregular waves (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{IRR}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-irr}\DIFaddend ), though several studies assume regular waves (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{REG}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-reg}\DIFaddend ).
    Others incorporate multiple sea states via a joint probability density matrix (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{IRR}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-irr}\DIFaddend +/\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{REG}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-reg}\DIFaddend +).
    Only a few consider storm sea states (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{STO}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-sto}\DIFaddend ).
\else
    The dominant trends across these studies are: point absorbers as the most common architecture;
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{boundary element method (BEM) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-bem} \DIFaddend hydrodynamics with occasional semi-analytical (\gls{acr-meem} ) treatments;
    drag handled via linear damping or describing functions;
    dynamics in either frequency or time domain (with extensions of either to handle constraints); 
    and limited inclusion of structures, mooring, and cost modeling. 
\fi

% Per-study paragraphs
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \paragraph{Notable large-scale optimization studies}
    More specifically, a few studies \citep{gaudin_single_2021,khanal_multi-objective_2024,edwards_optimisation_2022,garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021} perform \gls{acr-wec} optimization with several disciplines.
    These studies are notable for their large scales of 120 \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend and 14, 15, and 66 design variables respectively.
    \citet{gaudin_single_2021} optimizes an array of 20 submerged cylinders, holding the \gls{acr-wec} design constant but optimizing site selection, array layout, and mooring design while considering cable and pile cost as well as pile load capacity.
    \citet{khanal_multi-objective_2024} optimizes an array of four cylindrical point absorbers, with 4 modeled disciplines of array layout, hydrodynamics, controls, and economics.
    \citet{edwards_optimisation_2022} is a point absorber shape optimization study that minimizes surface area as a cost proxy, accounts for stability constraints, and considers only geometries with power extraction equal to theoretical radiation, amplitude, and steepness limits.
    \citet{garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021}, along with the smaller scale study \citep{garcia-teruel_design_2022} by the same authors, apply multi-objective optimization to trade off power with fatigue load and device volume, respectively.
    \citet{cotten_multi-objective_2022} similarly optimizes fatigue load for an attenuator-style \gls{acr-wec} with many more \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend but fewer (8) design variables.
    A different shape optimization \citep{abdulkadir_control_2024} notably calculates hydrodynamic coefficients via the semi-analytical \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{matched eigenfunction expansion method (MEEM)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-meem}\DIFaddend , rather than from boundary element method as is typical, but does not include disciplines beyond hydrodynamics and dynamics/controls.
    A team at \gls{acr-nrel} attempted local optimization for a terminator shape study but found they could only reasonably optimize 3 of the intended 13 design variables at a time due to the high computational cost of their time-domain simulation \citep{housner_numerical_2024}.
    The previous studies \citet{garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021,garcia-teruel_design_2022,cotten_multi-objective_2022} avoided this issue by simulating primarily in the frequency domain and using the time domain only to address \gls{acr-pto} constraints.

    \paragraph{Structural modeling and optimization}
    Structural optimization of \glspl{acr-wec} faces a similar challenge in the high compute time of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{finite element analysis (FEA)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-fea}\DIFaddend .
    References \citep{coe_survey_2018, ove_arup__partners_ltd_structural_2016,paduano_towards_2024,giannini_wave_2022} provide guidelines for \gls{acr-wec} survivability modeling and design, including the prediction of both extreme loads and structural failure.
    Fatigue modeling methods include spectral analysis, rainflow counting, damage equivalent loads, and Miner's law, augmented with standard stress concentration factors or \gls{acr-fea} for local hotspots \citep{ove_arup__partners_ltd_structural_2016}.
    Strength modeling methods consist primarily of various fidelities of \gls{acr-fea} , although hand and spreadsheet calculations permit sizing to a specified stress early in the design process \citep{ove_arup__partners_ltd_structural_2016}.
    \gls{acr-fea} \DIFadd{of }\glspl{acr-wec} is most often performed with manual design iterations, as in the studies \citep{RM3,mi_multi-scale_2025}.
    These two studies, while not optimization, are extremely multidisciplinary, encompassing mooring design and detailed economic evaluation in addition to the more common hydrodynamics, powertrain, controls, and structures.
    Automating the design process reported there remains a long-term vision for \gls{acr-wec} multidisciplinary design.

    Notably, one study \citep{an_optimal_2024} performs true optimization of \gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{structural }\gls{acr-fea} using the commercial computer aided engineering software Altair Inspire.
    The study examines four design variables, although it keeps the power calculations entirely separate from the structural optimization, neglecting the interdisciplinary coupling.
    Another structural optimization does not consider power at all, but is able to perform a brute-force sweep of 3 design variables by using analytical equations for stress and other failure modes \citep{ambuhl_reliability-based_2014}.
    The new Project SEA Stack modeling suite adds hydrodynamics to Project Chrono, a multiphysics software with the ability to perform finite element analysis using beam, shell, or solid elements, although it appears that so far the structural modeling capabilities have only been used for offshore wind turbines rather than \glspl{acr-wec} \citep{ogden_hydrochrono_2023}.
    Finally, several studies consider structures indirectly by examining the load on the device rather than the stress, e.g.\ \citep{nguyen_theoretical_2024, ferri_balancing_2014}, or equivalently assuming that stress scales with load and does not depend on any other dimensional design variable, e.g.\ \citep{garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021, cotten_multi-objective_2022}.
    Like the earlier \gls{acr-meem} study, \citet{nguyen_theoretical_2024} uses the equivalent analytical hydrodynamics in elliptical coordinates for an oscillating surge \gls{acr-wec} .

    \paragraph{Controls and powertrain co-design}
    The study \citep{ferri_balancing_2014} is notable because in addition to structural loads, it compares various control schemes and simulates second-order drivetrain dynamics.
    The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{control co-design (CCD) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-ccd} \DIFaddend methodology \citep{garcia-sanz_control_2019} emphasizes the importance of considering drivetrain dynamics and generator efficiency due to their strong effect on electrical power generation \citep{coe_useful_2023}.
    A number of recent studies \citep{rosati_control_2023,son_performance_2016,anderson_re-imagining_2024,devin_high-dimensional_2024,grasberger_control_2024} simultaneously optimize the \gls{acr-pto} with the controller.
    \citet{rosati_control_2023} optimizes an \gls{acr-owc} and is notable for its comparatively detailed cost modeling and its optimization of both economic and power variability metrics.
    \citet{mccabe_multidisciplinary_2022} also optimized power variation, although that study's use of regular waves makes the variation formulation less useful.
    Meanwhile, one point absorber \gls{acr-pto} optimization \citep{son_performance_2016} is additionally notable for its use of the \gls{acr-meem} method.
    \citet{gaebele_tpl_2025} is relevant because it optimizes the dimensional scale and \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{of the }\gls{acr-rm3} \gls{acr-wec} directly using a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{control co-design }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-ccd} \DIFaddend approach.
    It also efficiently incorporates many time-domain constraints (maximum force, speed, average power, \gls{acr-rms} torque, and stroke) while maintaining frequency-domain hydrodynamics via the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{pseudo-spectral (PS) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-ps} \DIFaddend modeling method, which \citep{devin_high-dimensional_2024,grasberger_control_2024} also use.

    Other \gls{acr-ccd} \DIFadd{papers optimize }\gls{acr-wec} dimensions without modeling the \gls{acr-pto} .
    \citet{herber_dynamic_2014} performs a direct transcription co-optimization of cylinder draft and radius with control.
    \citet{lin_fast_2025} introduces a new \gls{acr-ccd} formulation for constrained control that is even faster than \gls{acr-ps} .
    The method leverages the analytical \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Pontryagin Maximum Principle (PMP) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pmp} \DIFaddend and performs a case study to optimize a single geometric variable.
    A different \gls{acr-pmp} formulation \citep{abdulkadir_optimal_2024} incorporates \gls{acr-pto}\DIFadd{-constrained } array dynamics, although it has not yet been used for design optimization.
\else
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Across these studies , the most directly relevant work falls into three groups.
First, several }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Existing studies therefore tend to sacrifice disciplinary breadth and/or search space
    in order to maintain computational tractability.
}

    \DIFadd{High-fidelity multidisciplinary models are generally too expensive for }\DIFaddend large-scale optimization\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{studies \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{gaudin_single_2021,khanal_multi-objective_2024,edwards_optimisation_2022,garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021,garcia-teruel_design_2022,cotten_multi-objective_2022} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
address one to four disciplines but invariably exclude either structures or powertrain.
    Second, structural optimization of WECs is hampered by }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
    For example, }\linelabel{lne:struct-refs-start}\DIFaddend the cost of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{FEA \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{coe_survey_2018,ove_arup__partners_ltd_structural_2016,paduano_towards_2024,giannini_wave_2022}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
; the few WEC studies that perform structural optimization \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{an_optimal_2024,ambuhl_reliability-based_2014} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
decouple it from powercalculations.
    Third, recent control co-design studies
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{rosati_control_2023,son_performance_2016,gaebele_tpl_2025,devin_high-dimensional_2024,grasberger_control_2024,herber_dynamic_2014,lin_fast_2025,abdulkadir_optimal_2024} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-fea} \DIFadd{means that most structural studies analyze rather than optimize
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{coe_survey_2018,ove_arup__partners_ltd_structural_2016,paduano_towards_2024,giannini_wave_2022}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

    \DIFadd{Meanwhile, optimization-oriented models often omit structures, powertrain dynamics, or economic assessment.
    The few }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{structural optimizations \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{an_optimal_2024,ambuhl_reliability-based_2014} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
do not calculate power}\linelabel{lne:struct-refs-end}\DIFadd{.
    Large-scale optimizations invariably exclude either structures or powertrain include
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{gaudin_single_2021,khanal_multi-objective_2024,edwards_optimisation_2022,garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021,garcia-teruel_design_2022,cotten_multi-objective_2022}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    }\gls{acr-ccd} \DIFadd{studies
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{rosati_control_2023,son_performance_2016,gaebele_tpl_2025,devin_high-dimensional_2024,grasberger_control_2024,herber_dynamic_2014,lin_fast_2025,abdulkadir_optimal_2024,gaebele_tpl_2025}
    }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend efficiently handle constraints via \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-ps} \DIFaddend methods or analytical \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Pontryagin Maximum Principle}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pmp}\DIFaddend ,
    but none integrate structures or full economic costs.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The directly comparable RM3 study \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{gaebele_tpl_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
optimizes }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{gaebele_tpl_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
is directly comparable since it optimizes }\gls{acr-rm3} \DIFaddend dimensional scale and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO with control co-design but excludes structures}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto}\DIFaddend .
\fi

\paragraph{Software tools and gaps}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    Beyond models used in individual studies, it is also helpful to analyze the capabilities and gaps of available simulation tools.
    The popular time-domain hydrodynamics tool WEC-Sim \citep{ruehl_wec-simwec-sim_2024} can become multidisciplinary through the use of integrations like \gls{acr-nemoh} /Capytaine (hydrodynamic coefficients), \gls{acr-pto}\DIFadd{-Sim } (electric and hydraulic components), WEC-Sim Applications (optimal control and other useful tools), and MoorDyn (mooring).
    However, as \citep{housner_numerical_2024} reveals, it is too slow to use for large-scale optimization.
    Meanwhile, the newer pseudospectral tool WecOptTool \citep{coe_initial_2020} is faster and suitable for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{control co-design}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-ccd}\DIFaddend , but its disciplines are currently limited to hydrodynamics (via Capytaine) and control, with the ability to easily add custom dynamics like \gls{acr-pto} and mooring as needed, and it still can take considerable time for large optimizations.

    A 2023 marine energy software assessment \citep{ruehl_next-generation_2023} identified that industry-wide needs broadly include using state-of-the-art computation; overcoming limitations of existing software; ensuring appropriate language, architecture, and license of new software; accessing data; and ensuring usability and accuracy of free open source software.
    Specifically, relevant gaps include tools for powertrain and controls modeling, co-design, and optimization; high-fidelity models with faster runtime and low/mid-fidelity models with higher accuracy; inter-operabilty of models across disciplines and fidelities; and data for verification and validation \citep{ruehl_next-generation_2023}.
    This lack of a multidisciplinary \gls{acr-wec} simulation platform that is sufficiently fast for optimization helps explain why no optimization study in \Cref{tab:lit} simultaneously optimizes the device geometry, structures, and powertrain.
\else
    The two leading open-source \gls{acr-wec} simulation tools illustrate the gap.
    WEC-Sim \citep{ruehl_wec-simwec-sim_2024} is a time-domain simulator extensible to multidisciplinary studies via integrations
    (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{NEMOH}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrshort{acr-nemoh}\DIFaddend /Capytaine for hydrodynamics, PTO-Sim for powertrain, MoorDyn for mooring),
    but is too slow for large-scale optimization \citep{housner_numerical_2024}.
    WecOptTool \citep{coe_initial_2020} is a faster \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{pseudospectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-ps} \DIFaddend tool suitable for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{control co-design }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-ccd} \DIFaddend but currently limited to hydrodynamics and control.
    A 2023 marine energy software assessment \citep{ruehl_next-generation_2023} explicitly identifies the absence of a fast multidisciplinary optimization platform as an industry-wide gap, which explains why no study in \Cref{tab:lit} simultaneously optimizes geometry, structures, and powertrain.
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Existing studies therefore tend to sacrifice either disciplinary breadth or computational tractability: high-fidelity multidisciplinary models are generally too expensive for large-scale optimization, while optimization-oriented models often omit structures, powertrain dynamics, or economic assessment.
    }\DIFdelend This tradeoff motivates the development of a computationally efficient framework capable of concurrently capturing the major techno-economic couplings governing \gls{acr-wec} performance.
\fi

\subsection{Relevance of Semi-Analytical Modeling}   
An optimization study can use any kind of simulation model to assess the design's performance, including simple algebraic models, theory-intensive semi-analytical models, and standard numerical models.
\Cref{tab:model-types} summarizes the tradeoffs.

\begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \caption{Comparison of model types}
    \label{tab:model-types}
\begin{tabular}{cM{0.14\linewidth}cM{0.13\linewidth}}
         \textbf{Model type}&  \textbf{Expertise required}&  \textbf{Accuracy}&  \textbf{Computation cost}\\ \hline
         \textbf{Algebraic}&  Low&  Low&  Low\\
         \textbf{Semi-analytical}&  High&  Med&  Low\\
         \textbf{Numerical}&  Med&  Med/High&  High\\
    \end{tabular}
    \end{table}

For early-stage \gls{acr-wec} design optimization, where the model must be run many thousands of times, semi-analytical models offer the most beneficial combination of speed and accuracy.

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    Semi-analytical models can be orders of magnitude faster than numerical models, often with minimal sacrifices in accuracy.
    Additionally, semi-analytical models typically vary smoothly with their inputs and can make it easier to obtain accurate gradients, a feature that is often not the case for fully numerical models that may contain non-differentiable artifacts.
    Accurate gradients can also speed up convergence of the optimization and allow it to navigate high-dimension design spaces that would be intractable with heuristic algorithms.

    Unfortunately, semi-analytical models are less likely to be available commercially or open-source as numerical models are, and developing them from scratch requires substantial background in mathematics, numerical methods, and the technical domain of the model (for example, hydrodynamics or constrained optimal control).
    Often, semi-analytical models remain in the realm of theoretical academics, and ``application'' studies that use them to perform design/optimization usually consider just one discipline and have low practical realism.
    This is especially true if the models are closed-source, preventing others besides the model authors from using them.
    In the context of \glspl{acr-wec} , low practical realism usually means studies are limited to mechanical power production, without considering cost and realistic constraints, or perhaps using volume or surface area as a cost proxy.
    The design studies with high practical realism tend to use numerical models instead.
    As the literature review in \Cref{tab:lit} revealed, the high computational cost of the numerical models means that often a study is either multidisciplinary without optimization \citep{RM3,mi_multi-scale_2025}, or performs optimization for a single discipline, such as controls, powertrain, or structures, not all at once.

    Of the few studies that perform multidisciplinary optimization, those that use numerical models take many hours to run, especially when using heuristic optimizers, preventing designers from iterating quickly.
    For example, the \gls{acr-bem}\DIFadd{-based 4-}\gls{acr-wec} array optimization in the study \citep{khanal_multi-objective_2024} took 36 hours to run on a high-end workstation for only a single frequency.
    This type of model is too slow for use in realistic large-scale optimizations.
    On the other extreme, \citet{mccabe_multidisciplinary_2022} uses an algebraic hydrodynamic model that runs quickly but lacks sufficient accuracy to draw meaningful design conclusions.
    The present paper introduces a set of interoperable semi-analytical models that are designed to have the speed and robustness required for multidisciplinary optimization.
\else
    The directly comparable studies above either use slow numerical models that limit optimization scope (e.g.\ a \gls{acr-bem}\DIFadd{-based 4-}\gls{acr-wec} array optimization that took 36 hours per frequency on a high-end workstation \citep{khanal_multi-objective_2024}) or fast algebraic models that lack accuracy for meaningful design conclusions (e.g.\ \citep{mccabe_multidisciplinary_2022}).
    Semi-analytical models offer a useful intermediate that has not yet been combined with multidisciplinary scope in an open-source WEC tool, which is the gap MDOcean addresses.
    %DIF > \hl{Merge this section with the previous one, and just say that I can explain all the stuff in 1.2 by the type of model they use.}
\fi

\subsection{Paper Contributions and Roadmap}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    To address the gaps just described, the authors of this paper create a fast, multidisciplinary, and open-source \gls{acr-wec} simulation software.
    The developed software, called MDOcean \citep{mccabe_mdocean_2024}, implements relevant semi-analytical models from various fields, integrates them in a scalable framework for \gls{acr-wec} simulation and concurrent optimization, and validates them in a \gls{acr-wec} system context.
    The modeling framework articulated here is scalable to more disciplines, and enables systematic \gls{acr-wec} design optimization.
\else
    This paper presents MDOcean \citep{mccabe_mdocean_2024}, an open-source semi-analytical \gls{acr-wec} simulation framework addressing the gap identified above.
    \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{MDOcean is available at }\url{github.com/symbiotic-engineering/MDOcean}\DIFadd{.}\linelabel{lne:github-link-intro}
    \DIFaddend The work provides the following contributions:
    \begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
        \item \textbf{Integrates semi-analytical models} from hydrodynamics, dynamics, control, structures, and economics in a single \DIFaddbegin \linelabel{lne:open-source}\DIFadd{open-source }\DIFaddend framework suitable for optimization
        \item Formulates an underactuated optimal control problem with coupled nonlinear multi-port dynamics, and introduces the \textbf{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral (QLPS) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend method} to approximate time-domain constraints and nonlinearities
        \item \textbf{Analytically solves} the \gls{acr-qlps} optimal control problem as a univariate \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quadratically-constrained quadratic program (QCQP) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qcqp} \DIFaddend with a geometric interpretation on the complex reflection coefficient plane
        \item \textbf{Validates} against WEC-Sim and the \gls{acr-rm3} reference design across power, structural, and economic outputs
        \item Demonstrates \textbf{computational performance} (\resultsAOR[simRuntime] simulation runtime) enabling optimization studies that are otherwise infeasible
        \item Illustrates the \textbf{mathematical structure and effect magnitude} of subsystem interactions and techno-economic scaling relationships, providing intuition to facilitate early-stage design tradeoffs
    \end{itemize}
\fi
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION

    It develops the assumptions, analysis method, and implementation details of each module based on the appropriate balance of accuracy and speed.
    \Cref{sec:validation-benchmarking} discusses model validation and runtime benchmarking.
    Finally, \Cref{sec:discussion} showcases results from model sweeps and insights from the model structure.

    \Cref{sec:validation-benchmarking} compares MDOcean against WEC-Sim and the \gls{acr-rm3} reference design, demonstrates the runtime gain over established tools, and discusses where the model is reliable.
    \Cref{sec:discussion} presents results from variable sweeps and model-derived insights, along with limitations and future work.
    Extended derivations, validation studies, and implementation details are provided in the appendices to preserve readability of the primary narrative while maintaining reproducibility.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A }\DIFaddend companion paper \citep{mccabe_leveraging_2026} applies MDOcean to a multidisciplinary techno-economic optimization of the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{RM3.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-rm3}\DIFadd{.
}

%\clearpage
\section{Model Structure}
\label{sec:model-structure}

The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{simulation is composed of five coupled modules: geometry, hydrodynamics, dynamics and control, structures, and economics.
The geometry module computes derived properties such as areas, volumes, masses, centers of gravity, hydrostatics, and stability margins from bulk dimensions and structural thicknesses.
The hydrodynamics module evaluates frequency-domain hydrodynamic coefficients using a semi-analytical formulation based on the device geometry.
The dynamics and control module combines hydrodynamic coefficients, mass properties , and generator ratings to compute system response, loads, and power production using a quasi-linear frequency-domain model with nonlinear corrections for drag and powertrain saturation.
Both operational and extreme (storm)conditions are considered.
The structures module evaluates stress and factor-of-safety constraints under yield, ultimate, buckling, and fatigue criteria.
Finally, the economics module estimates capital cost (PTO and structure) and levelized cost of energy using power production and material usage.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{overall scope of }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{design includes the selection of discrete architecture choices, such the type of device and }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{(top row of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:design-scope}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
);
continuous design variables in the physical domain, such as dimensions (second row of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:design-scope}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
);
static analysis to evaluate the properties of these physical designs (blue boxes in third row of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:design-scope}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
);
dynamic analysis to quantify the effect of these static properties on coupled dynamic properties like power production (blue dotted outline of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:design-scope}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
);
and performance analysis to evaluate the viability of the design based on its static and dynamic properties (far right of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:design-scope}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
).
}\linelabel{lne:scope-start}
\DIFadd{MDOcean performs static analysis for a single type of floating body (an }\gls{acr-rm3}\DIFadd{-like two-body point absorber)
as well as dynamic analysis and optimal control for the full coupled system,
assuming that the static properties for other components (e.g. drivetrain, generator) are known or swept inputs.
}\linelabel{lne:scope-end}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Although this work does not perform optimization, the model is developed to support system-level design optimization.
The module coupling structure is illustrated in the XDSM diagram in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:n2}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
Inputs and outputs are organized according to standard MDO conventions \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{lambe_extensions_2012}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, and an optimizer would update design variables $x$ using objective $J$ and constraints $g$ until convergence to $x^*$.

\DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{Multiple-Discipline-Feasible Analysis Architecture}}
\DIFaddend \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Absence of Feedback Coupling}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The lack of variables in the lower left portion of the diagram indicates that there is no feedback coupling between modules, so an external solver to enforce consistency is not necessary.
    This helps decrease the simulation computation time and is appealing for optimization.
    }\paragraph{\DIFdel{Presence of Design Variable and Feed-Forward Coupling}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The fact that the same design variable group is an input to multiple modules indicates the presence of design variable coupling.
    Most heavily coupled are the bulk dimensions,
which are input to the geometry, hydrodynamics, and structural modules.
    Thicknesses and generatorratings are each input to two modules, with thicknesses coupling geometry and structures and generator ratings coupling dynamics and economics.
Moreover, the presence of variables in the upper right of the diagram indicates feed-forward coupling in which the output of one module directly affects subsequent modules.
    }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Analysis of coupling is important because under certain conditions, the optimization can be decomposed into separate problems to be solved in sequence or in parallel without sacrificing performance.
    For example, techniques like block coordinate descent or alternating direction method of multipliers apply if coupling is convex \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{wright_coordinate_2015}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, and monotonicity-based decomposition and constraint elimination apply if coupling is monotonic \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{azarm_monotonicity-based_1988,papalambros_principles_2017}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    The present coupling of WEC disciplinesis non-convex and non-monotonic, so concurrent optimization is required to obtain the system optimal design.
    Non-monotonicity means that perturbing a variable from one module in a certain direction does not necessarily determine the direction of the propagated change in coupling variables , objective, and constraints computed in other modules.
Non-convexity arises from the presence of both positive and negative curvature in the coupled variable space, leading to multiple local optima.
With an objective $J$ of LCOE, for example, while an increase in hydrodynamic damping generally increases power production (beneficial to $J$), it also increases structural loading (detrimental to $g$), and the larger bulk dimensions required to achieve that higher damping may produce higher stresses (detrimental to $g$) and increase the required structural material (detrimental to $J$).
    Thus, it would be sub-optimal to optimize the hydrodynamics module strictly for damping or power production followed by a separate optimization considering structures and economics.
    Furthermore, while even in a unified optimization it may be tempting to calculate the structural thicknesses within the structures module as the minimum required to sustain loads without failure, it is important to keep structural thicknesses as design variables because they also contribute to the hydrostatic constraints computed in the geometry module.
If active, these constraints would make the system-level optimum material thickness larger than what is structurally necessary.
    Using a single optimizer to drive the design of all modules and consider all constraints addresses this non-monotonic coupling. 
    }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{Multiple-Discipline-Feasible Analysis Architecture}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend All modules except the dynamics module are explicit, meaning they require no internal iteration to converge.
    The dynamics module requires iteration to incorporate nonlinearities into the quasi-linear frequency domain model, shown in the \gls{acr-xdsm} diagram as the orange box.
    The dynamics iteration occurs separately for each outer iteration of the optimizer.
    This structure is known as the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{multiple discipline feasible (MDF) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mdf} \DIFaddend architecture.
    In principle, it is possible to remove the dynamics iteration and instead incorporate the nonlinear dynamics as a residual equality constraint within the optimization, which is known as the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{simultaneous analysis and design (SAND) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-sand} \DIFaddend architecture \citep{martins_multidisciplinary_2013}.
    \gls{acr-sand} generally decreases the runtime of the simulation (analysis) but requires more optimization iterations, since it is the optimization rather than the simulation that must converge residuals. 
    The WecOptTool control co-optimization software pursues the \gls{acr-sand} strategy by collocating the dynamics constraints with the pseudo-spectral method \citep{coe_initial_2020}.

    Readers familiar with trajectory optimization should note that the choice of \gls{acr-mdf} \DIFadd{versus }\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{as an }\gls{acr-mdo} architecture parallels that of shooting versus collocation as a transcription method, in the sense that one chooses to enforce governing equations with simulation (\gls{acr-mdf} and shooting) versus optimization (\gls{acr-sand} and collocation) \citep{tedrake_underactuated_2024}.
    In \gls{acr-mdo} , the appropriate decision depends on characteristics of all modules, not merely the module whose governing equations are in question.
    As \Cref{sec:sim-runtime} will demonstrate, the hydrodynamics module, not dynamics and controls, is the dominant computational cost of the simulation.
    This means that even a substantial dynamics speedup represents only a small speedup of the full simulation and is unlikely to outweigh the slowdown of more (hydrodynamics-dominated) simulation evaluations.
    For this reason, \gls{acr-sand} would likely increase the runtime of the full optimization, motivating MDOcean's selection of \gls{acr-mdf} as the more suitable architecture.
    Another advantage of \gls{acr-mdf} \DIFadd{over }\gls{acr-sand} is that the latter invites the possibility of inconsistent dynamics if the optimization terminates unsuccessfully \citep{martins_multidisciplinary_2013} and requires optimizing a dummy constant objective to perform simulation without optimization.
\DIFaddbegin \else
    \DIFadd{In }\gls{acr-mdo}\DIFadd{, when governing equations circularly depend on one another,
    %DIF >  within a module (an ``implicit'' discipline) or between modules (an interdisciplinary ``feedback loop''),
    dependencies are often resolved with numerical iteration.
    Two common architectures for doing so include }\acrfull{acr-mdf} \DIFadd{and }\acrfull{acr-sand}\DIFadd{.
    They enforce governing equations differently: 
    }\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFadd{converges residuals to zero }\emph{\DIFadd{inside the simulation}}\DIFadd{,
    while }\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{incorporates the residuals as equality constraints in an }\emph{\DIFadd{external optimization}} \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{martins_multidisciplinary_2013}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    }\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{reduces the cost of each simulation but can increase the number of simulations required to optimize,
    so the preference is problem-dependent.
    Separately, }\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{has practical limitations:
    it invites the possibility of inconsistent dynamics if the optimization terminates unsuccessfully \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{martins_multidisciplinary_2013}
    }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and requires optimizing a dummy constant objective to perform simulation without optimization.
}\DIFaddend 

    \DIFdelbegin \paragraph{\DIFdel{Control Optimization Architecture}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{In control co-design}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Only one of MDOcean's governing equations requires such iteration:
    the resolution of nonlinearities in the quasi-linear frequency-domain formulation.
    %DIF >  governing equations contains no interdisciplinary feedback loops and the only implicit module is dynamics (to resolve nonlinearities in the quasi-linear frequency-domain formulation).
    This means that the ratio of dynamics simulation cost to total simulation cost and the expected number of iterations required for convergence determine the choice of }\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFadd{and }\gls{acr-sand}\DIFadd{.
    Hydrodynamics, not dynamics, will be shown to dominate computational cost (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:sim-runtime}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), and the dynamics iteration converges more rapidly than the design optimization, so }\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFadd{is chosen.
}\fi

\subsection{\DIFadd{Explicit Optimal Control Architecture}}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{In }\gls{acr-ccd}\DIFaddend , the nested and simultaneous architectures are often contrasted \citep{sundarrajan_towards_2021,herber_nested_2018}.
    The nested architecture, known as ``asymmetric subspace optimization'' in \gls{acr-mdo} literature \citep{chittick_asymmetric_2009}, optimizes control variables in an inner loop for each iteration of the outer design optimization.
    This takes advantage of the fact that the inner optimization is often convex and can be solved quickly.
    On the other hand, the simultaneous architecture uses a single optimizer to optimize design and control variables concurrently, creating a larger (typically non-convex) optimization problem and avoiding the difficulty of inner loop infeasibility that may arise in the nested approach \citep{herber_nested_2018}. 
    We presently distinguish two additional types of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{control co-design }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-ccd} \DIFaddend architectures in which the controller is not numerically optimized and instead obtained through an optimal control law.
    The optimal control law can be either explicitly derived and applied in the simulation (``explicit'' or analytical), or implicitly solved for by numerically zeroing the residual of an analytical optimal control condition (``implicit'' or semi-analytical).

    Each of these four control architectures can coexist with both \gls{acr-mdf} \DIFadd{and }\gls{acr-sand} analysis architectures, shown in \Cref{fig:control-arch}.
    The analysis architecture determines whether it is a solver or an optimization constraint which handles the dynamic residuals to converge the state, while the control architecture determines whether control variables are determined via simulation, residual solve, or simultaneous/nested optimization.
    In the ``implicit'' control diagram, the plural ``residuals'' is used rather than ``residual'' to convey that there exist residuals on the optimal control conditions to determine the control variables, in addition to those on the dynamic equations to determine the state variables.
    While the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{implicit-SAND and simultaneous-SAND }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{implicit-}\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{and simultaneous-}\gls{acr-sand} \DIFaddend block diagrams look nearly identical, the former determines the control variables via residuals of pre-determined optimality conditions expressed as constraints to the optimizer, while the latter determines them to maximize the optimization objective without a-priori knowledge of the optimal control conditions.

    In WecOptTool, the pseudo-spectral optimizer determines the state and control variables for a fixed design without using previously-known optimality conditions.
    Depending on whether a user adds additional design variables to the existing pseudo-spectral optimizer or in a separate loop outside, WecOptTool would be considered either \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{simultaneous-SAND or nested-SAND }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{simultaneous-}\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{or nested-}\gls{acr-sand} \DIFaddend respectively \citep{coe_initial_2020}.

    For MDOcean, we choose the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{explicit-MDF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{explicit-}\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFaddend architecture.
    Like the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{implicit-MDF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{implicit-}\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFaddend architecture, it minimizes computational cost by avoiding the need to include control variables in an optimization and lowering the number of dynamic constraints which the optimizer must consider.
    This is possible due to the semi-analytical dynamics and control module, where the optimal constrained control conditions are derived analytically.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Explicit-MDF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Explicit-}\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFaddend is chosen over \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{implicit-MDF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{implicit-}\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFaddend because it avoids the latter's numerical root-finding and is thus faster, as \Cref{sec:mod-freq-domain} will detail.
    An early version of MDOcean used the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{implicit-MDF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{implicit-}\gls{acr-mdf} \DIFaddend architecture, which is described in \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-numerical} for completeness.
\else
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The model }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Control co-design problems are commonly classified into nested and simultaneous formulations \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{sundarrajan_towards_2021,herber_nested_2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    In the nested approach, control is optimized in an inner loop, while in the simultaneous approach, control and design variables are optimized together.
    Alternatively, the control can be obtained without numerical optimization via an implicit or explicit optimality condition. 
    An explicit optimal control condition is typically the fastest; though it is not available for all cases, its availability here (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) makes it the natural choice.
    }

    \DIFadd{For comparison, the WecOptTool software would be considered either simultaneous-}\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{or nested-}\gls{acr-sand} \DIFadd{depending on whether a user adds additional design variables to the existing pseudo-spectral optimizer or in a separate loop outside, respectively \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{coe_initial_2020}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\fi

%DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%% XDSM and modules %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{\DIFadd{Modular Decomposition and Coupling}}
\DIFadd{The simulation is composed of five coupled modules: geometry, hydrodynamics, dynamics and control, structures, and economics.
The geometry module computes derived properties such as areas, volumes, masses, centers of gravity, hydrostatics, and stability margins from bulk dimensions and structural thicknesses.
The hydrodynamics module evaluates frequency-domain hydrodynamic coefficients using a semi-analytical formulation based on the device geometry.
The dynamics and control module combines hydrodynamic coefficients, mass properties, and generator ratings to compute system response, loads, and power production using a quasi-linear frequency-domain model with approximate drag and saturation nonlinearities.
Both operational and extreme (storm) conditions are considered.
The structures module evaluates stress and factor-of-safety constraints under yield, ultimate, buckling, and fatigue criteria.
Finally, the economics module estimates capital cost (}\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{and structure) and levelized cost of energy using power production and material usage.
}

\DIFadd{Although this work does not perform optimization, the model is developed to support system-level design optimization.
The module coupling structure is illustrated in the }\gls{acr-xdsm} \DIFadd{diagram in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:n2}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
Inputs and outputs are organized according to standard }\gls{acr-mdo} \DIFadd{conventions \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{lambe_extensions_2012}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
,
and an optimizer would update design variables $\gls{sym-vec-x}$ using objective $\gls{sym-J}$ and constraints $\gls{sym-g}$ until convergence to $\gls{sym-vec-x}^*$.
}

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/xdsm.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/xdsm.pdf}
}\caption[Simplified \gls{acr-xdsm} diagram]{\DIFaddFL{Simplified }\gls{acr-xdsm} \DIFaddFL{diagram. Modules are shown along the diagonal. Inputs come from the top and left sides, and outputs go out the bottom and right sides.}}
\label{fig:n2}
\end{figure*}

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \paragraph{\DIFadd{Absence of Feedback Coupling}}
    \DIFadd{The lack of variables in the lower left portion of the diagram indicates that there is no feedback coupling between modules, so an external solver to enforce consistency is not necessary.
    This helps decrease the simulation computation time and is appealing for optimization.
    }\paragraph{\DIFadd{Presence of Design Variable and Feed-Forward Coupling}}
    \DIFadd{The fact that the same design variable group is an input to multiple modules indicates the presence of design variable coupling.
    Most heavily coupled are the bulk dimensions, which are input to the geometry, hydrodynamics, and structural modules.
    Thicknesses and generator ratings are each input to two modules, with thicknesses coupling geometry and structures and generator ratings coupling dynamics and economics.
    Moreover, the presence of variables in the upper right of the diagram indicates feed-forward coupling in which the output of one module directly affects subsequent modules.
    }

    \DIFadd{Analysis of coupling is important because under certain conditions, the optimization can be decomposed into separate problems to be solved in sequence or in parallel without sacrificing performance.
    For example, techniques like block coordinate descent or alternating direction method of multipliers apply if coupling is convex \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{wright_coordinate_2015}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, and monotonicity-based decomposition and constraint elimination apply if coupling is monotonic \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{azarm_monotonicity-based_1988,papalambros_principles_2017}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    The present coupling of }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{disciplines is non-convex and non-monotonic, so concurrent optimization is required to obtain the system optimal design.
    Non-monotonicity means that perturbing a variable from one module in a certain direction does not necessarily determine the direction of the propagated change in coupling variables, objective, and constraints computed in other modules.
    Non-convexity arises from the presence of both positive and negative curvature in the coupled variable space, leading to multiple local optima.
    With an objective $\gls{sym-J}$ of }\gls{acr-lcoe}\DIFadd{, for example, while an increase in hydrodynamic damping generally increases power production (beneficial to $\gls{sym-J}$), it also increases structural loading (detrimental to $\gls{sym-g}$), and the larger bulk dimensions required to achieve that higher damping may produce higher stresses (detrimental to $\gls{sym-g}$) and increase the required structural material (detrimental to $\gls{sym-J}$).
    Thus, it would be sub-optimal to optimize the hydrodynamics module strictly for damping or power production followed by a separate optimization considering structures and economics.
    Furthermore, while even in a unified optimization it may be tempting to calculate the structural thicknesses within the structures module as the minimum required to sustain loads without failure, it is important to keep structural thicknesses as design variables because they also contribute to the hydrostatic constraints computed in the geometry module.
    If active, these constraints would make the system-level optimum material thickness larger than what is structurally necessary.
    Using a single optimizer to drive the design of all modules and consider all constraints addresses this non-monotonic coupling. 
}\else
    \DIFadd{The model }\DIFaddend exhibits strong design-variable coupling but no direct feedback coupling between modules, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{eliminating the need for a system-level consistency iteration }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{as expected from the prior observation that the only equation requiring iteration is within the dynamics module}\DIFaddend .
    This improves computational efficiency and is advantageous for optimization.
    Coupling occurs primarily through shared design variables, particularly bulk dimensions, which affect geometry, hydrodynamics, and structures, as well as generator ratings and structural thicknesses, which couple multiple subsystems.
    Feed-forward coupling is also present through inter-module dependencies.

    This coupling is both non-convex (e.g., larger bulk dimensions raise power but also raise structural loads and material requirements) and non-monotonic, motivating concurrent rather than sequential optimization.
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{MDO Architecture}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{All modules except dynamics are explicit.
    The dynamics module requires an internal fixed-point iteration to resolve nonlinearities in the quasi-linear frequency-domain formulation.
    This leads to a multiple-discipline feasible (MDF) architecture, where dynamics convergence is handled within each simulation call.
    }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{An alternative simultaneous analysis and design (SAND) formulation \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{martins_multidisciplinary_2013} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
would enforce dynamic residuals directly within the optimization, potentially reducing simulation cost but increasing optimization complexity.
    However, since hydrodynamics dominates computational cost in this framework (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:sim-runtime}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), the benefit of removing dynamics iteration is limited.
    For this reason, MDF is adopted.
    }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\paragraph{\DIFdel{Control Co-Design Architecture}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{paragraph}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Control co-design problems are commonly classified into nested and simultaneous formulations \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{sundarrajan_towards_2021,herber_nested_2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    In the nested approach, control is optimized in an inner loop, while in the simultaneous approach control and design variables are optimized together.
    In addition, optimal control-based formulations may determine control either explicitly or implicitly via optimality conditions. 
    }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{These control formulations can be combined with both MDF and SAND analysis architectures.
    MDOcean uses an implicit-MDF architecture: the dynamics module iterates internally to converge the quasi-linear drag and saturation describing functions, while all other modules are explicit.
    The implicit formulation is preferred over explicit alternatives due to the structure of the derived optimal control conditions (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:mod-freq-domain}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
).
    It also avoids introducing control variables into the outer optimization while preserving optimal control behavior, minimizing total runtime given that hydrodynamics dominates computational cost (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:sim-runtime}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
).
    Detailed architecture rationale, coupling analysis, and the relation to MDO literature are provided in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{mccabe_dissertation_2026}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    }%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:control-arch} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
summarizes the relationship between analysis and control architectures, with the explicit-MDF formulation used in this study highlighted.
}\DIFdelend \fi

%DIF > !TeX root =../main.tex
