%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Module Details}
\label{sec:modules}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    The five modules (geometry, hydrodynamics, dynamics and controls, structures, and economics) will now be described one at a time.
    For notation, unbolded unarrowed variables refer to scalars (e.g. $\gls{sym-x}$  or $X$), arrowed variables refer to vectors ($\gls{sym-vec-x}$  or $\vec{X}$), and bold variables refer to matrices ($\gls{sym-mathbf-x}$ or $\gls{sym-mathbf-X}$ ).
    For dynamic (time-varying) quantities, the time domain representation is denoted $\gls{sym-x}(\gls{sym-t})$ , and can also be represented as the real part of a complex signal \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$x(t)=\Re\left[\tilde{x}(t)\right]$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-x}(\gls{sym-t})=\Re\left[\gls{sym-tilde-x}(\gls{sym-t})\right]$}\DIFaddend .
    Assuming the signal is sinusoidal, the complex signal can be expressed as \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\tilde{x}(t)=\hat{X}e^{i\omega t}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-tilde-x}(\gls{sym-t})=\gls{sym-hat-X}\exp(i\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t})$ }\DIFaddend with complex phasor \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{X}=|\hat{X}|e^{i\angle \hat{X}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-X}=|\gls{sym-hat-X}|\exp(i\angle \gls{sym-hat-X})$ }\DIFaddend and angular frequency $\gls{sym-omega}$ .
    Altogether, this gives $\gls{sym-x}(\gls{sym-t})=|\gls{sym-hat-X}|\cos(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\angle\gls{sym-hat-X})$ .
    Dots (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\dot{x}(t)=\Re(\hat{\dot{X}}e^{i\omega t})=\Re(i\omega\hat{X}e^{i\omega t})$) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\dot{x}(\gls{sym-t})=\Re(\hat{\dot{X}}\exp(i\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}))=\Re(i\gls{sym-omega}\gls{sym-hat-X}\exp(i\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t})))$ }\DIFaddend indicate time-derivatives.
    For static quantities, no special notation differentiates complex from real variables, as this will be indicated directly in the text or with $\gls{sym-mathbb-R}$ or $\gls{sym-mathbb-C}$  if clarification is needed.
\else
    The five modules (geometry, hydrodynamics, dynamics and controls, structures, and economics) will now be described one at a time.
    For notation, unbolded unarrowed variables refer to scalars, arrowed variables refer to vectors, and bold variables refer to matrices.
    Time-varying quantities $\gls{sym-x}(\gls{sym-t})$  are represented by complex phasor \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{X}$ such that $x(t)=\Re[\hat{X}e^{i\omega t}]$.
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-X}$ such that $\gls{sym-x}(\gls{sym-t})=\Re[\gls{sym-hat-X}\exp(i\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t})]$.
    }\linelabel{lne:equation-cite-start}\DIFadd{Equations drawn from prior work are cited where they appear; uncited equations are derived in this work, with full derivations in the appendices.}\linelabel{lne:equation-cite-end}
    \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-parameters} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
documents model parameters and hyperparameters.}\linelabel{lne:appendix-parameters}
\DIFaddend \fi
%TC:break Geometry
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection{Geometry}\label{sec:geom}
The geometry module computes submerged volume \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$V_{\text{sub}}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-V-sub}$}\DIFaddend , structural volume \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$V_{\text{struct}}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-V-struct}$}\DIFaddend , and associated hydrostatic properties from bulk dimensions.
\Cref{fig:dims} defines the principal geometric variables, where $\gls{sym-T-draft}$  denotes drafts, $\gls{sym-h}$  heights, and $\gls{sym-D}$  diameters.
Subscripts $f$, $s$, and $d$ refer to the float, spar, and damping plate, respectively.
Key points include the center of buoyancy $\gls{sym-B}$ , center of gravity $\gls{sym-G}$ , the keel $\gls{sym-K-keel}$ , and metacenter $\gls{sym-M-metacenter}$  for the combined system.
Additional structural dimensions and thickness definitions are provided in \Cref{sec:structures}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/from-matlab/dimensions.pdf}
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/dimensions.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/dimensions.pdf}
}\DIFaddendFL \caption{Dimension labeling of system}\label{fig:dims}
\end{figure}

Static pitch stability is enforced by requiring:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GM}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \overline{GM} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-GM} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \overline{KB} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-KB} \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \overline{BM} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-BM} \DIFaddend - \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \overline{KG} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-KG} \DIFaddend > 0,
\end{equation}
\noindent where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\overline{BM} = \pi D_f^4/(64V_{\text{sub}})$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-BM} = \gls{sym-pi} \gls{sym-D-f}^4/(64 \gls{sym-V-sub})$ }\DIFaddend \citep{newman}.

Hydrostatic equilibrium is enforced by matching displaced water mass to total system mass, with any mismatch compensated using ballast with mass \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$m_{bal}$:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-m-bal}$:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{m_{bal} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-m-bal} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\rho_w V_{sub} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-rho-w} \gls{sym-V-sub} \DIFaddend - \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\rho_M V_{struct}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-rho-M} \gls{sym-V-struct}\DIFaddend ,
\end{equation}
where $\gls{sym-rho-w}$ and $\gls{sym-rho-M}$  are water and structural material densities.
To ensure feasible buoyancy, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$m_{bal}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-m-bal}$ }\DIFaddend must be non-negative and physically storable within the hull.
Assuming seawater ballast, this imposes a volume constraint:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vol-constraint}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{V_{struct} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-V-struct} \DIFaddend \le \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{V_{sub} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-V-sub} \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{V_{surf} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-V-surf} \DIFaddend - \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{V_{pto}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-V-pto}\DIFaddend ,
\end{equation}
where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$V_{\text{surf}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-V-surf}$ }\DIFaddend (design-dependent) and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$V_{pto}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-V-pto}$ }\DIFaddend (fixed) are volumes of the above-water structures and \gls{acr-pto} respectively.

%TC:break Hydrodynamic Coefficients
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection{Hydrodynamic Coefficients}\label{sec:hydro}
The first-order hydrodynamic force phasors $\gls{sym-vec-hat-F}$  for interacting floating bodies are expressed in the frequency domain as the sum of wave excitation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{\hat{F}}_{e}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-hat-F-e}$}\DIFaddend , hydrodynamic radiation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{\hat{F}}_{rad}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-hat-F-rad}$}\DIFaddend , and hydrostatic restoring \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{\hat{F}}_{res}$ contributions:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-hat-F-res}$ contributions:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:hydro-forces}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{F}} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-F} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{F}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{e} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-e} \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{F}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{rad} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-rad} \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{F}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{res}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-res}
\DIFaddend \end{equation}
where, under monochromatic forcing,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hydro-forces-expanded}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{\hat{F}}_{e} &= \vec{\gamma}\,\hat{\zeta},&~~
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{\hat{F}}_{rad} &= -\mathbf{A}_h\vec{\hat{\ddot{\xi}}} - \mathbf{B}_h\vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}},&~~
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{\hat{F}}_{res} &= -\mathbf{K}_h\vec{\hat{\xi}}.
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-e} &= \gls{sym-vec-gamma}\,\gls{sym-hat-zeta},&~~
    \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-rad} &= -\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h}\gls{sym-vec-hat-ddot-xi} - \gls{sym-mathbf-B-h}\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi},&~~
    \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-res} &= -\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h}\gls{sym-vec-hat-xi}.
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
Here $\gls{sym-vec-hat-xi}$  is the body displacement phasor vector from the origin at the center of the float at the still waterline.
The hydrodynamic coefficients $\gls{sym-vec-gamma}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-h}$, and $\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h}$  are the wave excitation vector, added mass matrix, radiation damping matrix, and hydrostatic stiffness matrix, respectively.

The displacement vector $\gls{sym-vec-hat-xi}$  contains a state for each \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degree of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dof}\DIFaddend .
We define separate displacement and excitation vectors for the operational ($op$) and storm ($st$) cases.
A two-body heaving point absorber that rigidly locks the bodies in a storm following \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{RM3} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{RM3} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend has vectors:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:define-vectors}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{\xi}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{op} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-xi-op} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         \hat{\xi}_f \\ \hat{\xi}_s
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-hat-xi-f} \\ \gls{sym-hat-xi-s}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend , ~~
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{\xi}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{st}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-xi-st}\DIFaddend =\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}\hat{\xi}_m 
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}\gls{sym-hat-xi-m}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend , ~~
     \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\gamma}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-gamma}\DIFaddend _{op} = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         \gamma_f \\ \gamma_s
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-gamma-f} \\ \gls{sym-gamma-s}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend ,~~
     \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\gamma}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-gamma}\DIFaddend _{st} = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         \gamma_m
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-gamma-m}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend .
\end{equation}
with subscripts for the float ($f$), spar ($s$), their coupling ($c$), and the merged float-spar ($m$).
Operational hydrodynamic matrices are symmetric $2\times 2$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:op-hydro-coeffs}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \mathbf{A}_{h,op} = \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         A_f & A_c \\ A_c & A_s
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix},~ 
%DIFDELCMD <     \mathbf{B}_{h,op} &= \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         B_f & B_c \\ B_c & B_s
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix},\\
%DIFDELCMD <     \mathbf{K}_{h,op} &= \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         K_f & 0\\ 0 & K_s
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-mathbf-A-h-op} = \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-A-f} & \gls{sym-A-c} \\ \gls{sym-A-c} & \gls{sym-A-s}
    \end{bmatrix},~
    \gls{sym-mathbf-B-h-op} &= \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-B-f} & \gls{sym-B-c} \\ \gls{sym-B-c} & \gls{sym-B-s}
    \end{bmatrix},
    \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
        ~
    \else
        \\
    \fi
    \gls{sym-mathbf-K-h-op} &= \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-K-f} & 0\\ 0 & \gls{sym-K-s}
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
while storm (merged-body) coefficients are scalar:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:st-hydro-coeffs}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD < \vec{\gamma}_{st}&=\gamma_{m}=\Sigma~\vec{\gamma}_{op},&
%DIFDELCMD < ~\mathbf{A}_{h,st}&=A_m=\Sigma~\mathbf{A}_{h,op}, \\
%DIFDELCMD < ~\mathbf{B}_{h,st}&=B_m=\Sigma~\mathbf{B}_{h,op},&
%DIFDELCMD < ~\mathbf{K}_{h,st}&=K_m=\Sigma~\mathbf{K}_{h,op}.
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
\gls{sym-vec-gamma}_{st}&=\gls{sym-gamma-m}=\gls{sym-Sigma}~\gls{sym-vec-gamma}_{op},&
~\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h-st}&=\gls{sym-A-m}=\gls{sym-Sigma}~\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h-op}, \\
~\gls{sym-mathbf-B-h-st}&=\gls{sym-B-m}=\gls{sym-Sigma}~\gls{sym-mathbf-B-h-op},&
~\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h-st}&=\gls{sym-K-m}=\gls{sym-Sigma}~\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h-op}.
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
with $\gls{sym-Sigma}$  denoting the sum of all elements in the matrix or vector (``grand sum'').

The remaining task is to compute the float, spar, and coupling coefficients, with operational and storm implications discussed in \Cref{sec:design-load-cases}.
The hydrostatic stiffness terms ($\gls{sym-K-f}$, $\gls{sym-K-s}$ ) are frequency-independent and are computed directly from geometry (see \Cref{eq:gamma-K} in \Cref{sec:appendix-meem-details}), while the remaining coefficients require solving the frequency-domain radiation boundary value problem for the velocity potential.
Traditionally, this is performed using a \gls{acr-bem} solver, which discretizes the body surfaces and solves a large linear system.
In MDOcean, float coefficients are instead computed using the semi-analytical \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Matched Eigenfunction Expansion Method (MEEM)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-meem}\DIFaddend , exploiting cylindrical symmetry for improved efficiency.
Spar and coupling terms are obtained via algebraic approximations and interpolation of pre-computed \gls{acr-bem} data, and could be extended to \gls{acr-meem} in future work.
\Cref{tab:hydro-methods} summarizes the methods used for each coefficient.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Float Coefficients: \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Matched Eigenfunction Expansion Method}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-meem}\DIFaddend }\label{sec:hydro-meem}
\gls{acr-meem} solves the radiation and excitation problems by expanding the velocity potential in eigenfunctions within each fluid region and enforcing matching across region boundaries.
The \gls{acr-meem} radiation solution for two concentric heaving cylinders was first presented by \citet{mavrakos_hydrodynamic_2004} and detailed by \citet{chau_inertia_2012}.
In this approach, the fluid domain is partitioned into regions with separable analytical solutions, and continuity conditions are enforced at region interfaces to determine the unknown expansion coefficients.
The open-source implementation used here, summarized in \Cref{sec:appendix-meem-details}, follows the formulation by \citet{mccabe_open-source_2024}.
Forthcoming companion papers \citet{bimali_matrix_2026,best_openflash_2026} detail the numerical properties and code implementation respectively of an expanded implementation.

The primary advantage of \gls{acr-meem} is computational efficiency.
Timing comparisons (\Cref{sec:sim-runtime}) show more than an order-of-magnitude speedup over Capytaine \gls{acr-bem} at comparable convergence, significantly reducing the cost of multidisciplinary analysis.
Additional benefits include lower memory usage, elimination of meshing, and avoidance of numerical Green\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{’}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{'}\DIFaddend s function approximation.

Limitations include geometric restrictions and numerical overflow (discussed in \Cref{sec:appendix-meem-details}).
As a semi-analytical method, \gls{acr-meem} is restricted to simplified geometries; the formulation used here assumes a dual concentric cylinder (\Cref{fig:meem-geom}).
Consequently, float coefficients neglect the damping plate and approximate the float bottom as flat rather than slanted.
The damping plate enters through the spar coefficients (next subsection).
These approximations have minor impact and could be relaxed in future extensions using more general \gls{acr-meem} formulations \citep{olaya_hydrodynamic_2015,kokkinowrachos_behaviour_1986,bimali_matrix_2026}.

\begin{figure}[b!]
    \centering
     \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/from-matlab/MEEM_geometry.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelFL{MEEM }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/MEEM_geometry.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/MEEM_geometry.pdf}
    }\caption[\gls{acr-meem} geometry]{\gls{acr-meem} \DIFaddendFL geometry.
Dashed lines represent geometry that is neglected in the \gls{acr-meem} calculations}
   \label{fig:meem-geom}
\end{figure}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Other coefficients: approximations}\label{sec:hydro-other}
Because the damping plate affects the spar coefficients more strongly than the float, the former are obtained by scaling existing solutions that include the damping plate rather than using \gls{acr-meem} .

\begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \caption{Method of computing hydrodynamic coefficients in MDOcean}
    \label{tab:hydro-methods}
    \begin{tabular}
    {M{0.25\linewidth}M{0.63\linewidth}}
         Coefficient& Method\\ \hline
         $\gls{sym-A-f}$, $\gls{sym-B-f}$, $\gls{sym-gamma-f}$  & \gls{acr-meem} (\Cref{eq:hydro,eq:gamma-K} in \Cref{sec:appendix-meem-details})\\
         $\gls{sym-A-s}$ & Approximate $\gls{sym-omega}\rightarrow\infty$  solution (\Cref{eq:As} in \Cref{sec:appendix-additional-hydro})\\
         $\gls{sym-A-c}$, $\gls{sym-B-s}$, $\gls{sym-B-c}$, $\gls{sym-gamma-s}$ & Nominal \gls{acr-bem} solution, scaled with $\gls{sym-D-d}$ and $\gls{sym-T-s}$  (\Cref{eq:scale-wamit} in \Cref{sec:appendix-additional-hydro})\\
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}
%\hl{Describe how I did the WAMIT damping plate scaling for the spar excitation.}
%The MEEM implementation currently cannot take the damping plate into account, and the plate is expected to have a large impact on the spar added mass and damping. Therefore, algebraic approximations of the damping plate coefficients are used instead of the MEEM solution.
The spar added mass coefficient $\gls{sym-A-s}$  is approximated as a frequency-independent quantity based on the displaced water volume in the spherical projection of the damping plate, with the formula given in \Cref{sec:appendix-additional-hydro}.

The spar damping ($\gls{sym-B-s}$ ), excitation ($\gls{sym-gamma-s}$ ), and coupling terms ($\gls{sym-A-c}$, $\gls{sym-B-c}$ ) are obtained by interpolating a pre-computed \gls{acr-wamit} \gls{acr-bem} solution over the nondimensional parameter \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$kD_d$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D-d}$ }\DIFaddend (see \Cref{sec:appendix-additional-hydro}).
The excitation term is additionally scaled as \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\exp(-k(T_s - T_{s,nom}))$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\exp(-\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}(\gls{sym-T-s} - \gls{sym-T-s-nom}))$ }\DIFaddend to account for depth-dependent attenuation.

%DIF < \hl{I suspect this imposes a constraint on $D_d$ needing to be similar to the nominal design, see notebook 3/20/26 referencing analysis from 4/4/25.}
%DIF > \hl{I suspect this imposes a constraint on $\gls{sym-D-d}$ needing to be similar to the nominal design, see notebook 3/20/26 referencing analysis from 4/4/25.}

Because float and spar coefficients are computed using different approximations, positive definiteness of the hydrodynamic matrices is not guaranteed (see \Cref{sec:appendix-additional-hydro}).
To ensure physicality, the added mass and damping matrices are required to remain positive definite.
Any violations are corrected by adjusting the coupling terms $\gls{sym-A-c}$ and $\gls{sym-B-c}$ .

\subsection{Dynamics and Control}\label{sec:dynamics}
This subsection introduces the dynamics and control modeling assumptions before detailing the frequency-domain methods and control formulations.

%DIF < %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Modified frequency-domain methods}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:mod-freq-domain}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend %DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%% start old %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    Methods for \gls{acr-wec} dynamics and control simulation in an optimization context were reviewed in \Cref{sec:lit} and include time-stepping (nonlinear, numerical, high computation cost), frequency domain (linear, analytical, low computation cost), pseudo-spectral (nonlinear, numerical, medium computation cost), \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend techniques (approximate nonlinear, semi-analytical, low computational cost), and Pontryagin's maximum principle (nonlinear, analytical, low computational cost).
    In wave energy, both spectral-domain numerical methods and analytical methods have generated recent research interest\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, with studies \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{bacelli_numerical_2015,coe_initial_2020,tan_computationally_2026,nie_optimal_2016,da_silva_stochastic_2023} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and studies \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{abdulkadir_optimal_2024,lin_fast_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
serving as illustrative examples of each case}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{bacelli_numerical_2015,coe_initial_2020,tan_computationally_2026,nie_optimal_2016,da_silva_stochastic_2023} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
study the former and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{abdulkadir_optimal_2024,lin_fast_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
illustrate the latter}\DIFaddend .
    This section describes both the technical concept and the practical rationale for the choice of a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend method, and contextualizes it with respect to other constrained optimal control methods in more detail than was possible in the literature review.

    MDOcean requires the ability to incorporate nonlinearities, at least approximately.
    Inherent nonlinearities such as drag significantly affect the amplitude and power production at resonance.
    Even with linearized intrinsic dynamics, maintaining optimality subject to dynamic constraints also requires a nonlinear controller.
    Dynamic constraints on amplitude, force, and peak power significantly influence the trade-off of power and cost, and amplitude limits are required to obtain a physically meaningful result.
    Besides nonlinearities, MDOcean also requires a low computation cost, since the dynamics will be evaluated many thousands of times during optimization.
    Of the methods mentioned, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Pontryagin's maximum principle (PMP) and quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pmp} \DIFadd{and }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend techniques meet these requirements.
    \gls{acr-pmp} requires an analytically difficult derivation unique to the specific nonlinearities in question because wave energy dynamics have a particular form requiring so-called singular control \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{zou_optimal_2017}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{zou_optimal_2017}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .
    On the other hand, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend techniques are simple and intuitive.
    Therefore, MDOcean pursues a semi-analytical \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral method}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps}\DIFaddend .
\else
    Standard linear frequency-domain \gls{acr-wec} analysis assumes unconstrained linear dynamics, enabling closed-form impedance-matched optimal control.
    However, real \glspl{acr-wec} are subject to (i) dynamic constraints (e.g., generator force, power, and stroke limits) and (ii) nonlinear forces, particularly viscous drag.
    Time-domain simulation handles both naturally but is computationally expensive, especially in an optimization loop.
    Pseudo-spectral methods are nonlinear and handle constraints by collocating the dynamics at specific time-steps, but require an outer numerical optimizer for both simulation and control synthesis.
\fi
MDOcean adopts a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral (QLPS) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend approach that retains the speed of frequency-domain analysis while handling both temporal constraints and drag nonlinearities.
This formulation seeks to preserve the computational advantages and simplicity of classical linear analysis while extending its applicability to practical \gls{acr-wec} operating regimes that include saturation, drag, and dynamic constraints.
\gls{acr-qlps} combines two techniques: (a) describing functions, which quasi-linearize a nonlinear waveform by retaining only its fundamental harmonic,
and (b) constrained spectral optimal control, which solves the optimal control problem as a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quadratically-constrained quadratic program (QCQP) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qcqp} \DIFaddend assuming linear dynamics and spectral-domain constraints.
\Cref{fig:venn-diagram} positions \gls{acr-qlps} relative to existing methods.
Augmenting spectral optimal control with describing functions extends its scope to approximate time-domain constraints and nonlinear dynamics.
Furthermore, because \gls{acr-qlps} assumptions under monochromatic forcing lead to a monochromatic problem, the \gls{acr-qcqp} becomes univariate for systems with a single control \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degree of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dof}\DIFaddend .
The optimal constrained linear controller can then be found analytically through a geometric interpretation that exploits the low dimensionality.
Its nonlinear counterpart can be reconstructed semi-analytically with inverse describing functions.
Avoiding iterative numerical optimization substantially reduces computational cost in large parametric studies and produces insight into the mathematical structure of dynamic tradeoffs to inform \gls{acr-pto} sizing.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=.9\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/control_method_venn_diagram.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/control_method_venn_diagram.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/control_method_venn_diagram.pdf}
    }\caption[Venn diagram of control synthesis methods]{\DIFaddendFL Venn diagram comparing different methods for control synthesis, with MDOcean's \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddendFL approach at the intersection of frequency domain, nonlinear, and constrained optimal control.
    See text for acronym descriptions.}
    \label{fig:venn-diagram}
\end{figure}

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \Cref{fig:venn-diagram} shows a Venn diagram comparing different methods for optimal control synthesis in greater detail.
    Typical frequency domain methods for control design, such as loop shaping, \gls{acr-pid} tuning, and $H_\infty$/$H_2$ control for general applications, along with complex-conjugate impedance matching for power-maximizing applications such as wave energy, cannot take into account constraints and nonlinearities.
    Meanwhile, typical methods for nonlinear control design, such as feedback linearization, sliding mode control, backstepping, and Lyapunov-based control, are performed in the time domain and do not incorporate constraints.
    Latching and declutching control are wave-energy-specific passive nonlinear bang-bang control methods that utilize the frequency-domain concept of phase-matching the fundamental harmonic of the response to the wave excitation, but they are still primarily time-domain methods and do not incorporate constraints \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{hals_comparison_2011}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{hals_comparison_2011}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .
    Methods for constrained optimal control, meanwhile, such as \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{model predictive control (MPC), constrained linear-quadratic regulation (LQR)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mpc}\DIFadd{, constrained }\gls{acr-lqr}\DIFaddend , and trajectory optimization are likewise time-domain and were originally derived for linear control problems, although nonlinear extensions exist including \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{nonlinear MPC (NMPC)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-nmpc}\DIFaddend , constrained iterative \gls{acr-lqr} (iLQR), and differential \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{dynamic programming (DP)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dp}\DIFaddend .
    Other methods for nonlinear constrained optimal control in the time domain include the already-discussed \gls{acr-pmp} and the control barrier function method.
    Methods to incorporate nonlinearities in the frequency domain include harmonic balance synthesis, which retains full nonlinearity in a sinusoidal-steady state analysis so technically falls in the spectral domain rather than the frequency domain; its linearized counterpart, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{describing function (DF)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-df}\DIFaddend -based synthesis \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{atherton_nonlinear_1982,gelb_multiple-input_1968}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{atherton_nonlinear_1982,gelb_multiple-input_1968}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend ; and the extension of the latter to broadband inputs, spectral linearization (also called stochastic linearization, Gaussian closure, and quasi-linear control) \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{roberts_random_2003,ching_quasilinear_2010}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{roberts_random_2003,ching_quasilinear_2010}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .
    These methods are not optimal control methods and do not incorporate constraints, although it is possible to sub-optimally enforce constraints by applying a saturation nonlinearity to the constrained quantity.
    Finally, methods which combine frequency domain synthesis with constrained optimal control include constrained loop shaping (often enforcing performance constraints while minimizing the distance to an optimal loop shape, in this case the complex-conjugate impedance); linear spectral \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{optimal control (OC)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-oc}\DIFaddend , which poses a trajectory optimization problem for a linear system represented with global basis functions in the spectral domain; constrained \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{impedance matching (IM)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrfull{acr-im}\DIFaddend , the narrow-band, frequency-domain equivalent of spectral optimal control; and convex control formulated using \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{linear matrix inequalities (LMIs)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-lmi}\DIFaddend , typically in the context of robust control \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{vanantwerp_tutorial_2000}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{vanantwerp_tutorial_2000}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

    At the intersection of all three categories lies nonlinear spectral \gls{acr-oc} , pseudo-spectral \gls{acr-oc} , and the method used in MDOcean, which we call ``\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral OC}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \acrlong{acr-qlps} \gls{acr-oc}\DIFaddend .''
    The pseudospectral method uses the same global basis function representation as spectral \gls{acr-oc} , but instead of enforcing the dynamics at all points in the trajectory, it does so only at specific collocation points, which enables the representation of time-domain constraints and nonlinearities as opposed to harmonic constraints and nonlinearities \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{elnagar_pseudospectral_1995}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{elnagar_pseudospectral_1995}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .
    Pseudo-spectral methods often employ Legendre or Chebyshev polynomial bases, although in wave energy Fourier bases are more typical.
    In this case, the method is equivalent to a nonlinear trajectory optimization problem that enforces both spectral-domain harmonic balance and time-domain constraints.
    The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend method used in MDOcean borrows the pseudo-spectral concept of incorporating time-domain constraints in the spectral domain, but retains only the fundamental harmonic to collapse to the frequency domain.
    This is exactly analogous to the way that describing functions linearize the harmonic balance method.
    It can also be thought of as the combination of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{describing functions with constrained impedance matching.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-df} \DIFadd{with constrained }\gls{acr-im}\DIFadd{.
}\DIFaddend \fi


%DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%% end old %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFaddbegin 

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Describing Functions}}
\label{sec:describing-functions}
\paragraph{\DIFadd{Concept}}

\linelabel{lne:df-concept-start}\DIFadd{MDOcean's dynamics utilize the sinusoidal-input describing function method, which assumes a certain time-domain nonlinear signal shape and then calculates the fundamental amplitude of that signal for use in a typical frequency-domain simulation, assuming that the signal's higher harmonics are filtered out by the low-pass plant.
}\linelabel{lne:df-concept-end}
\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:desc-fcns} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
illustrates this concept, showing the nonlinear signals for saturation (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:sat}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) and quadratic drag (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:drag-df}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) along with their fundamental amplitude describing function approximations.
}\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
    \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/saturation_desc_fcn.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/saturation_desc_fcn.pdf}
    }\caption{\DIFaddFL{Generator torque saturation}}\label{fig:sat}
\end{subfigure}
\hfill
\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
    \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/drag_desc_fcn.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/drag_desc_fcn.pdf}
    }\caption{\DIFaddFL{Quadratic drag}}\label{fig:drag-df}
\end{subfigure}
\caption[Conceptual demonstration of describing function approximations]{\DIFaddFL{Conceptual demonstration of describing function approximations. Plots show force versus time. Nonlinear signals in red; fundamental amplitudes in blue.}}\label{fig:desc-fcns}
\end{figure}

\DIFadd{Because the fundamental amplitude depends on the response, the frequency domain simulation is only quasi-linear and must either be iterated numerically or solved analytically.
MDOcean uses iteration to handle both the drag force and control force nonlinearities. %DIF >  and an analytical solution for the force saturation nonlinearity.
While the describing function method used here has been known for over half a century, MDOcean represents its first application in a wave energy optimization or open-source simulation, to the authors' knowledge.
MDOcean therefore extends semi-analytical dynamics to wave energy systems, enabling efficient simulation while retaining model fidelity.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Assumptions}}
\newcounter{asmpnumber}
\DIFadd{Describing functions make the following assumptions:
}\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item \assumption{nonlinearities-are-harmonics}
    \linelabel{lne:A1-start}
    \textbf{\DIFadd{Any nonlinearities can be written as the sum of a quasi-linear transformation and harmonic generation}}\DIFadd{.
    This allows a spectral-domain model, rather than time-domain, while preserving nonlinearities.
    Using the nonlinearity classification structure of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{rijlaarsdam_comparative_2017,rijlaarsdam_frequency_2012}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
,
    \asmpref{nonlinearities-are-harmonics} permits gain compression/expansion, harmonic generation, and frequency-mixing,
    including ``Volterra,'' ``special Weiner,'' or ``convergent'' system types.
    Eligible nonlinearities include drag, saturation, and nonlinear potential flow effects.
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{folley_spectral-domain_2016,da_silva_stochastic_2023} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
discuss spectral-domain modeling for WECs.
}

    \item \assumption{system-low-pass}
    \textbf{\DIFadd{The system is low-pass}}\DIFadd{.
    Most physical systems have low-pass behavior due to inertia, and WECs are no exception.
    Since the nonlinearities of \asmpref{nonlinearities-are-harmonics} considered here generate only odd harmonics,
    we define \asmpref{system-low-pass} as applicable when the third harmonic of an incident wave exceeds the system's cutoff frequency.
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{mccabe_force-limited_2024} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
discuss further and demonstrate the assumption graphically, and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet[Figure 3]{mccabe_wec_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
show that RM3's hydrodynamic response is low-pass for frequencies above 1.2 rad/s.
    This gives a maximum wave period of 15.7~s,
    so \asmpref{system-low-pass} applies for the vast majority of incident energy, even in an irregular sea state.
}

    \item \assumption{quasi-linearized}
    \textbf{\DIFadd{The response to higher harmonics can be neglected}}\DIFadd{, resulting in a linearized spectral-domain model.
    This is a direct consequence of \asmpref{nonlinearities-are-harmonics} and \asmpref{system-low-pass}.
    This assumption is called ``best linear approximation,'' ``quasi-linearization,'' ``statistical linearization,'' and ``Gaussian closure'' by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{rijlaarsdam_comparative_2017,ching_quasilinear_2010,roberts_random_2003,nie_optimal_2016} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
respectively.
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{nie_optimal_2016,da_silva_statistical_2020,spanos_efficient_2016,tan_computationally_2026} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
pursue this assumption for WECs.
}

    \item \assumption{stationary-ergodic}
    \textbf{\DIFadd{The input phase does not affect the analysis.}}
    \DIFadd{Our system meets this criteria since a linearized WEC is wide-sense stationary and the long-term average power is an ergodic output.
    Under this assumption, using a deterministic spectrally-equivalent multisine instead of a random-phase stochastic input for the wave elevation does not change the power and other dynamic outputs, so the former can be used for simplicity.
    Most of the spectral linearization references above implicitly assume this,
    since stochastic-steady-state rather than transient outputs are of interest.
}

    \item \assumption{waves-narrow-band}
    \textbf{\DIFadd{The input wave excitation is narrow-band}}\DIFadd{.
    This implies that the deterministic multisine wave input can be replaced by a single sine wave;
    in other words, it approximates an irregular wave spectrum as an equivalent regular wave.
    }\linelabel{lne:sidf-precise-start}\DIFadd{The quasi-linearization method then collapses to the sinusoidal-input describing function method, where a nonlinearity is approximated simply as an amplitude-dependent gain and phase shift applied to a sinusoidal input.}\linelabel{lne:sidf-precise-end}

    \item \assumption{signal-shape-known}
    \textbf{\DIFadd{The signal shape of the nonlinear waveform is known.}}
    \DIFadd{Calculating the SIDF gain and phase shift requires specifying a signal shape and computing its fundamental \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{gelb_multiple-input_1968,atherton_nonlinear_1982}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    We assume a quadratic absolute-value nonlinearity for drag and a saturated sinusoid nonlinearity for control force.
    }\linelabel{lne:A6-end}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Implementation}}
\DIFadd{The drag describing function formula is encoded directly into the simulation (see derivation in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:drag}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
),
while the control force describing function is implemented indirectly using knowledge of the limit cases of describing functions for multiple nonlinear control laws (see discussion in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-force-sat}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
).
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:assumptions} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
summarizes how these assumptions connect to DFs and to increasingly specialized QLPS formulations.
}

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
    \centering
    \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/assumptions.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/assumptions.pdf}
    }\caption{\DIFaddFL{Directed graph of assumptions for DFs and QLPS.
    Assumptions on system dynamics are yellow, assumptions on the optimal control objectives/constraints are green, and methods for dynamic analysis/control synthesis are blue.
    A single arrow implies necessity and all arrows imply sufficience.
    For example, A1 and A2 are each individually necessary but insufficient for A3; A1 and A2 are together sufficient for A3.}}
    \label{fig:assumptions}
\end{figure*}

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Quasi-Linear Pseudo-Spectral Optimal Control}}
\label{sec:qlps}
\paragraph{\DIFadd{Motivation}}
\DIFadd{The describing functions of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:describing-functions} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
can evaluate a quasi-linear approximation of a nonlinear dynamic system but cannot directly synthesize an optimal controller.
For that, we introduce the }\acrfull{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{optimal control method.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Concept}}
\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{combines the dynamic analysis concept of quasi-linearization with the pseudo-spectral concept of collocation to yield a simple yet effective method for constrained nonlinear optimal control synthesis.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Assumptions}}
\DIFadd{The most general form of }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{requires only a single assumption
}\assumption{constraints-fundamental}\DIFadd{:
}\linelabel{lne:A7-start}
\textbf{\DIFadd{the optimization objective and constraints can be written as a (potentially nonlinear) invertible function of the fundamental amplitude of linear dynamic system state variables}}\DIFadd{.
This follows directly from \asmpref{quasi-linearized} (linearized dynamics) and \asmpref{stationary-ergodic} (deterministic rather than stochastic analysis) when the states used to obtain the objective and constraints are the outputs of the dynamic analysis.
This assumption means that the peak value or other time-domain characteristic of a nonlinear signal can be constrained as long as it can be written in terms of the fundamental.
}

\DIFadd{Here we formulate the }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{problem for a linear multiport dynamic system in which the state variables are ``effort'' and ``flow'' phasors related by linear coefficient matrices.
This form is well suited for expressing oscillatory power flow, although standard linear state-space representation yields equivalent mathematics.
}

\DIFadd{Despite the linearized dynamics, the potential nonlinear relationship between the state variables and the objectives and constraints
makes the general }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{problem a nonlinear optimization problem (NLP).
This alone is not a significant computational benefit over the general pseudo-spectral method for nonlinear dynamic systems.
}

\DIFadd{The computational benefits come when we introduce assumptions }\assumption{quadratic-objective} \DIFadd{and }\assumption{quadratic-constraints}\DIFadd{,
which respectively state that }\textbf{\DIFadd{the objectives and constraints can be written as a quadratic function of the state variables}}\DIFadd{.
This is an easy requirement for wave energy, since quadratic metrics of a linear multiport effort-flow model include many quantities of interest: effort magnitude, flow magnitude, average power, reactive power, and linear combinations thereof.
Under this condition, the NLP becomes a QCQP (quadratically constrained quadratic program).
The original WEC pseudo-spectral formulation by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{bacelli_numerical_2015} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
conforms to this assumption and shows that under the additional assumption }\assumption{passive-dynamics-convex} \DIFadd{of }\textbf{\DIFadd{passive dynamics}}\DIFadd{, the QCQP is convex.
}

\DIFadd{Even more computational benefits, and the novel aspect of the }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{approach, appear when two more assumptions are enforced.
}\assumption{no-freq-coupling} \DIFadd{and }\assumption{no-dof-coupling} \DIFadd{require that the system has }\textbf{\DIFadd{no dynamic coupling between different frequencies and different control DOFs}} \DIFadd{respectively.
\asmpref{no-freq-coupling} is satisfied as a result of \asmpref{waves-narrow-band} (narrow-band waves) and \asmpref{system-low-pass} (low-pass WEC dynamics).
\asmpref{no-dof-coupling} is satisfied because the RM3 contains only a single controlled DOF.
\asmpref{no-dof-coupling} permits coupling between the body DOFs as long as there is a single controlled DOF as is present here,
or it permits multiple controlled DOFs only if they are uncoupled when viewed from each control load port at which power is to be optimized
(i.e. an array of non-interacting multi-DOF WECs for which off-diagonal terms of the intrinsic impedance matrix are negligible).
}\linelabel{lne:A12-end}

\DIFadd{Under \asmpref{no-freq-coupling} and \asmpref{no-dof-coupling}, the QCQP for all frequencies and DOFs can be separated into a set of single-order QCQPs,
one for each frequency and DOF,
which can be solved semi-analytically using the geometric properties of circles.
In fact, this order reduction enables a semi-analytical solution even in the presence of certain nonlinear constraints or nonconvexities,
in violation of \asmpref{quadratic-constraints} and \asmpref{passive-dynamics-convex}.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Implementation}}
\DIFaddend \Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-synthesis,fig:mod-freq-domain-evaluation,fig:mod-freq-domain-combined} distinguish three application workflows for the \gls{acr-qlps} method:
control synthesis (the design of a potentially nonlinear controller to obey constraints and maximize performance of the quasi-linearized system),
evaluation (simulation of an arbitrary controller's power performance, where the controller, plant, or both are nonlinear), and both.
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{MDOcean uses QLPS }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{MDOcean uses }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend for both control synthesis and evaluation.
\DIFaddbegin \begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\caption[Flowchart depicting the \gls{acr-qlps} method (M1)]{\DIFaddFL{Flowchart depicting the }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddFL{method (M1). The }\acrlong{acr-df}\DIFaddFL{s for response/constraint, controller, and plant are shown in yellow, pink, and blue respectively.}}
\begin{subfigure}[t]{.9\linewidth}
    \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_synthesis.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_synthesis.pdf}
    }\caption{\DIFaddFL{Control synthesis - applies to (M1.2) only.}}
    \label{fig:mod-freq-domain-synthesis}
\end{subfigure}
\vfill
\begin{subfigure}[t]{.9\linewidth}
    \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_evaluation.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_evaluation.pdf}
    }\caption{\DIFaddFL{Control evaluation - applies to both (M1.1) and (M1.2).}}
    \label{fig:mod-freq-domain-evaluation}
\end{subfigure}
\vfill
\begin{subfigure}[t]{.9\linewidth}
    \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_combined.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_combined.pdf}
    }\caption{\DIFaddFL{Combined synthesis and evaluation.}}
    \label{fig:mod-freq-domain-combined}
\end{subfigure}
\end{figure*}

\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
derives the overall optimal control problem, and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
derives the semi-analytical solution for the single-order QCQP.
}

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Approximate }\gls{acr-qlps}\DIFadd{: Modified Frequency-Domain Methods}}
\label{sec:mod-freq-domain}
\paragraph{\DIFadd{Motivation}}
\DIFadd{Viewing DFs (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:describing-functions}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) and }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{(\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:qlps}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) as part of the broader family of }\acrfull{acr-mfd} \DIFadd{methods provides greater context and facilitates comparison to other work.
}\DIFaddend In prior wave energy studies, related approaches have been used for evaluation, with simplified treatments of constraints (see F+ and PI+ entries in \Cref{tab:lit}).
We refer to these as the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{modified frequency-domain }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddend family of methods, of which \gls{acr-qlps} is the most accurate and optimal.
In contrast, the present work applies \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{QLPS and other modified frequency-domain }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{and other }\gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddend methods to control synthesis, enabling nonlinear controller design within a frequency-domain formulation.
This differs from linearization approaches such as stochastic linearization, which are generally limited to linear controller design.

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure*}[htbp]
%DIFDELCMD < \centering
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Flowchart depicting the QLPS method (M1). The describing functions (DFs) for response/constraint, controller, and plant are shown in yellow, pink, and blue respectively.}}
%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \begin{subfigure}[t]{.9\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_synthesis.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Control synthesis - applies to (M1.2) only.}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:mod-freq-domain-synthesis}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{subfigure}
%DIFDELCMD < \vfill
%DIFDELCMD < \begin{subfigure}[t]{.9\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_evaluation.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Control evaluation - applies to both (M1.1) and (M1.2).}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:mod-freq-domain-evaluation}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{subfigure}
%DIFDELCMD < \vfill
%DIFDELCMD < \begin{subfigure}[t]{.9\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/from-matlab/mod_freq_domain_ctrl_combined.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Combined synthesis and evaluation.}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:mod-freq-domain-combined}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{subfigure}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{figure*}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mfd} \DIFadd{methods besides }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{can be considerably easier to implement and may be of interest to practitioners seeking simpler alternatives to assess the effect of dynamic constraints,
or to provide a path forward when the assumptions of }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{are not fully satisfied but the computational cost of time-domain optimal control is prohibitive.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Concept}}
\DIFaddend When handling dynamic constraints with a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{modified frequency-domain }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{general }\gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddend method, first the system is (quasi-) linearized and the unconstrained optimal controller (and the response signals corresponding to that controller) are determined in the frequency domain.
Then the unconstrained response is checked for constraint violations.
This check can be performed in the frequency domain if the time-domain constraint can be adequately linearized (via describing functions) as a harmonic constraint, as shown in the top of \Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-synthesis}, or the response can be reconstructed in the time domain via the inverse describing function if the constraint is not easily representable via harmonics.
If the response for a given sea state does not violate any constraints, then the unconstrained controller and corresponding power is used for that sea state as the frequency-domain linear optimal controller.
Otherwise, a variety of approaches are possible to address the violated constraint, summarized in \Cref{tab:constraint-approaches} and described below.
Methods M1--M2 can be used for both control synthesis and evaluation, while methods M4--M5 can only be used for evaluation since they do not attempt to find a controller that satisfies the constraint.
Method M3 can, in general, only be used for evaluation.
However, in the special case where the only time-domain constraint is on the control force, the method can be used for control synthesis.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \caption[Constraint satisfaction approaches for \acrlong{acr-mfd} methods]{\DIFaddendFL Constraint satisfaction approaches for \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{modified frequency domain }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddendFL methods. Filled stars indicate better performance.}
\label{tab:constraint-approaches}
\begin{tabular}{M{.03\linewidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}m{0.45\linewidth}l l}
&\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Accuracy} & \textbf{Optimality} \\ \toprule
M1 & Nonlinear optimal (M1.1) or near-optimal (M1.2) controller, quasi-linearized via describing functions& \Stars{3} &\Stars{3} \\ \hline
M2 & Linear optimal controller& \Stars{3} & \Stars{2} \\ \hline
M3 & Saturate or zero the times with constraint violations, neglecting the effect on other signals& \Stars{2} & \Stars{1}\\ \hline
M4 & Saturate or zero entire sea state& \Stars{1} & \Stars{0.5} \\ \hline
M5 &Mark design as infeasible& N/A& \Stars{0} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

% --- M1 ---
\paragraph{M1: \gls{acr-qlps} with nonlinear control}
The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{modified frequency-domain }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddend approach with the highest accuracy and optimality (M1) is to synthesize a new nonlinear optimal (or near-optimal) time-domain controller that obeys the constraint, taking into account the linearized effect of that nonlinear controller on the frequency domain response.
This synthesis can occur in a number of ways.
If a time-domain optimal control law (e.g. those that \citet{abdulkadir_optimal_2024,lin_fast_2025} develop via \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the Pontryagin Maximum Principle (PMP) ) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pmp}\DIFadd{) }\DIFaddend is available for the constraint under consideration (M1.1), it can be used as the nonlinear optimal controller directly, essentially bypassing the use of \gls{acr-qlps} in the control synthesis step (\Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-synthesis}) and using it only for evaluation (\Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-evaluation}).
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    This differs from the standard \gls{acr-pmp} method in that the evaluation of the response and power corresponding to the time-domain \gls{acr-pmp} controller is determined in the frequency domain, rather than via numerical time-integration (as in the method of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{zou_optimal_2017}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{zou_optimal_2017}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend ) or analytical time-integration (as in the method of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{lin_fast_2025}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{lin_fast_2025}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend ).
    This is advantageous if the computational cost or mathematical labor of the numerical or analytical \gls{acr-pmp} evaluation methods, respectively, are of concern.
\fi
For situations without an explicit \gls{acr-pmp} control law (M1.2), a nonlinear near-optimal controller can be constructed by first deriving the optimal linear controller via constrained optimization
(M2) and then adjusting it to be nonlinear using insights from signal saturation and filtering.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This is the approach pursued here for the force limit.
}\DIFdelend M1.2 should be considered the primary formulation of \gls{acr-qlps} , and the other \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{modified frequency-domain }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddend methods (M2)--(M5) in \Cref{tab:constraint-approaches} act as approximations of \gls{acr-qlps} with varying levels of accuracy and optimality.
M1.1 and M1.2 both linearize the nonlinear controller via the describing function method.
In addition to the control nonlinearity, a describing function is also utilized to linearize the plant drag nonlinearity.

% --- M2 ---
\paragraph{M2: \gls{acr-qlps} with linear control}
If a nonlinear controller or the corresponding describing function to linearize it are not available, the most optimal linear controller that obeys the constraint can be used instead (M2).
This approach accurately evaluates the maximum power that a linear controller could produce, although this power will be lower (less optimal) than that of the optimal nonlinear controller.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{MDOcean pursues this approach for amplitude limits in the operational design load case.
}\DIFdelend 

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    The constrained optimization to identify the controller in (M1.2) and (M2) can be performed numerically or analytically by analyzing an impedance-mismatched linear system in the frequency domain and will be discussed in detail in \Cref{sec:optimal-control}.
    The current MDOcean software uses the numerical approach, which requires more computation time but is simpler to implement and equally accurate.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The analytical approachis nonetheless derived in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
(with further details and graphical visualization of the constraints in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and reference \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{mccabe_force-limited_2024}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) and recommended for future implementation}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
derives the analytical approach, while \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{mccabe_force-limited_2024} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
provide details and visualizations}\DIFaddend .
\else
    The constrained optimization used to identify the controller in (M1.2) and (M2) can be performed numerically or analytically by analyzing an impedance-mismatched linear system in the frequency domain;
    the analytical derivation is provided in \Cref{sec:optimal-control}, with additional details in \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution}.
\fi

%DIF <  --- M3, M4, M5 ---
%DIF >  --- M3 ---
\paragraph{M3\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{--M5}\DIFdelend  : \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Constrained frequency-domain estimates}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Saturate or Zero Violations}\DIFaddend }
If neither a numerical nor an analytical implementation is available to identify the optimal constrained linear controller, then the violating portion of the time-domain signals can be saturated or zeroed for the sake of power calculation (M3).
This approach neglects the corresponding effect of the saturation/zeroing on other quantities in the system, which lowers the accuracy, and the controller does not have the opportunity to even approximately consider the constraint, resulting in a power production potentially much lower than the constrained optimal.
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    This simple approach does not require any derivations to implement.
    It has been used in several previous \gls{acr-wec} optimization studies for various constraints \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{garcia-teruel_design_2022,garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021,cotten_multi-objective_2022,mccabe_constrained_2013} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{garcia-teruel_design_2022,garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021,cotten_multi-objective_2022,mccabe_constrained_2013} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend and is evidently the most common \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddend method in the marine energy field.
\else
    This simple approach has been used in several previous \gls{acr-wec} optimization studies for various constraints in the studies \citep{garcia-teruel_design_2022,garcia-teruel_reliability-based_2021,cotten_multi-objective_2022,mccabe_constrained_2013}
    and is the most common \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-mfd} \DIFaddend method in the marine energy field.
\fi
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{It is utilized here for the power limit.
}\DIFdelend 

%DIF >  ---- M4 -- M5 ---
\DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{M4--M5: Penalize Violations}}
\DIFaddend As a last resort, the energy in that sea state can be zeroed (M4),
essentially assuming that the device enters survival mode in those cases to avoid constraint violation, or the entire design can be marked infeasible with a constraint in the optimization (M5), which \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{reference~\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{mccabe_constrained_2013} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{mccabe_constrained_2013} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend pursues for a radiation limit constraint.
These approaches do not attempt to find dynamic solutions which satisfy the limits, and essentially deal with violations by penalizing the optimization through either the objective or constraints.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The latter approach (M5) is actually the only option for evaluation of slamming amplitude limits in the storm design load case.
}%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{This is because the device is already in survival mode (preventing method M4), is not producing power (preventing methods M3 and M4), and is not applying a control input (preventing methods M1 and M2).
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{This is because the device is in survival mode and not applying a control input.
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend 

%Approach (M5) is also pursued here for amplitude limits in the operational design load case, in the interest of consistency and implementation expedience.

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:nonlinearities} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
summarizes the dynamic nonlinearities and limits and the method MDOcean currently uses to handle each.
The describing function for control force is prioritized due to its simpler implementation as well as the force constraint's frequent activity and strong impact on results observed in prior RM3 optimizations \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{mccabe_multidisciplinary_2022,gaebele_tpl_2025,mcgilton_optimal_2024}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{In future work, MDOcean could instead utilize describing functions (M1.2) or the optimal constrained linear controller (M2) for the power and operational amplitude limits respectively to improve accuracy and optimality.
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Method Choice for Each Nonlinearity}}\label{sec:mfd-method-choice}
\DIFaddend \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \caption{Dynamic nonlinearities and approach for each}
    \label{tab:nonlinearities}
\DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.7\linewidth}}
%DIFDELCMD <          %%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \begin{tabular}{L{0.25\linewidth}L{0.7\linewidth}}
         \DIFaddendFL \textbf{Dynamic nonlinearity/limit}& \parbox{\linewidth}{\centering\textbf{Method}}\\ \hline
         Drag& Describing function (M1.2) for $\sin(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t})|\sin(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t})|$  with iteration to find effect on response and optimal controller\\
         Force limit& Describing function (M1.2) for $\text{sat}(\sin(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}))$  with iteration to find effect on response and optimal controller\\
        Operational amplitude limit& Optimal linear controller (M2)\\
         Power limit& Average value of \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\text{sat}(\sin(\omega t)+C)$}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\text{sat}(\sin(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t})+\gls{sym-a})$}\DIFaddendFL , neglecting any effect on response and optimal controller (M3)\\
        Storm amplitude limit&Optimizer considers limit violations as infeasible designs (M5)\\
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Describing functions assume a certain time-domain nonlinear signal shape and then calculate the fundamental amplitude of that signal for use in a typical frequency-domain simulation.
}%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{The two signals where describing functions are used are both force signals.
    For a force signal, the fundamental amplitude is the most relevant harmonic because not only is it typically the largest amplitude, but it also undergoes the least amount of filtering by the low-pass plant and therefore contributes the most to power production.
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Because the fundamental amplitude depends on the response, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:nonlinearities} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
summarizes the dynamic nonlinearities and limits and the method MDOcean currently uses to handle each.
}

\DIFadd{For the control force limit, the corresponding describing function (saturated-sine) is simple to implement, and prior }\gls{acr-rm3} \DIFadd{optimizations \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{mccabe_multidisciplinary_2022,gaebele_tpl_2025,mcgilton_optimal_2024} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
show the constraint to be frequently active and impact results strongly, justifying the use of M1.2 (full }\gls{acr-qlps}\DIFadd{).
For amplitude limits in the operational design load case, M1.2 produces the same result as M2 due to }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{frequency domain simulation is only quasi-linear and must either be iterated numerically or solved analytically.
MDOcean uses iteration to handle both the drag force and control force nonlinearities. %DIF <  and an analytical solution for the force saturation nonlinearity.
The former encodes the describing function formula directly into the simulation (see derivation in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:drag}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), while the latter implements it indirectly using knowledge of the limit cases of describing functions for multiple nonlinear control laws (see discussion in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-force-sat}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{low-pass assumption that makes the constrained quantity a pure sinusoid, so we use the simpler M2 without loss of optimality}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{This is possible because the describing function quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral method is used for both controller synthesis and evaluation, so the time-domain nonlinear optimal controller that is output from the right of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-synthesis} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
is fed into the top left of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-evaluation}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, resulting in a chaining of the inverse control describing function with the forward control describing function, which cancel out.
Unless intentionally made different to study robustness, the linearized plant model is identical for the synthesis and evaluation steps.
    In this case, two workflows essentially become one, with no further computation required after the optimal control step.
    Reconstruction of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For a power limit, under cases where the power limit is }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{nonlinear controller is not necessary for the evaluation of the power corresponding to that controller.
The power can be calculated directly from the frequency-domain response and the describing function of the nonlinear controller.
The time-domain representation of the nonlinear controller is only necessary for hardware implementation, or if the synthesized controller is to be evaluated with a different method such as time-stepping. 
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Because the same describing function is applied in both synthesis and evaluation, the inverse and forward describing functions cancel; the time-domain nonlinear controller need not be reconstructed for power evaluation, only for hardware implementation.
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{While the describing function method used here has been known for over half a century, MDOcean represents its first application in a wave energy optimization or open-source simulation, to the authors' knowledge.
The RAFT open-source Python package for offshore wind turbines uses the drag describing function with iteration but does not include describing functions for saturation \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{hall_open-source_2022}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
MDOcean therefore extends semi-analytical dynamics to wave energy systems, enabling efficient simulation while retaining model fidelity.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{only constraint, applying M2 is straightforward, and applying M1.2 is possible but more cumbersome:
assuming that the optimal power signal is a saturated sinusoid implies that the corresponding force signal is the quotient of the truncated power sinusoid and the amplitude sinusoid, which requires deriving an additional describing function.
When a power limit is applied simultaneously with a force limit, both methods break down.
M2 would be incompatible with the previous choice of M1.2 for the co-occurring force limit, and M1.2 creates ambiguity about the nonlinear signal shapes,
since it is not possible for the force and power signals to both be saturated sinusoids while assuming sinusoidal amplitude.
To avoid this additional complexity, the power limit uses M3, although future work should consider the use of M1.2 or M2 to improve accuracy and optimality.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For slamming amplitude limits in the storm design load case, approach M5 is actually the only option. 
This is because the device is already in survival mode (preventing method M4), is not producing power (preventing methods M3 and M4), and is not applying a control input (preventing methods M1 and M2).
}

\DIFaddend %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFaddbegin \FloatBarrier
\DIFaddend \subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Equation of Motion}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Linearized Dynamics}\DIFaddend }\label{sec:eom}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    Before applying the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quasi-linearized pseudo-spectral }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qlps} \DIFaddend method described above, we first present the equations of motion for a standard linear \gls{acr-wec} model, starting in the time domain and quickly moving to the frequency domain.
    Specifically, we combine the bi-conjugate network model of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{coe_co-design_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{coe_co-design_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend with the underactuated \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{multi-degree of freedom model of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{faedo_principle_2022} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{multi-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFadd{model of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{faedo_principle_2022} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend to arrive at a formulation suitable for the impedance matching of a multibody \gls{acr-wec} subject to wave forcing, powertrain kinematics and dynamics, and drag.
\else
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The MDOcean dynamic model combines the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Before applying the }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{method described above,
    we first present the dynamics for a standard linear }\gls{acr-wec}\DIFadd{,
    combining the }\DIFaddend bi-conjugate network model of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{coe_co-design_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{coe_co-design_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend with the underactuated \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{multi-degree of freedom model of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{faedo_principle_2022} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{multi-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFadd{model of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{faedo_principle_2022} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend to express the equations of motion for a multibody \gls{acr-wec} subject to wave forcing, powertrain kinematics and dynamics, and drag.
    \DIFaddbegin \linelabel{lne:single-freq}\DIFadd{Note that \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:eom,sec:optimal-control,sec:drag} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
consider a single monochromatic sea state; \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:energy} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
performs annual aggregation.
}\DIFaddend \fi

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The float and spar are each modeled as a floating rigid body, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Multiport Structure}}
\DIFadd{The dynamics portion of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:design-scope} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
represents this system as a multiport circuit, with effort 
variables $\gls{sym-vec-e}$ and flow variables $\gls{sym-vec-q}$ at each port.
Ports include the wave input, body mechanical, }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{mechanical, generator mechanical, and generator electrical.
Between each set of ports are the intrinsic body dynamics, PTO kinematics, drivetrain dynamics, }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the two bodies are kinematically coupled to transmit force between them.
Forces include wave excitation $\vec{F}_e$, hydrodynamic radiation $\vec{F}_{rad}$, hydrostatic restoring $\vec{F}_{res}$, drag $\vec{F}_d$, and power takeoff $\vec{F}_p$.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{The MDOcean dynamic model assumes regular waves for reasons to be detailed in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:irregular-waves}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, so the system experiences monochromatic wave forcing.
    Combined with the linearity of the model, this implies that all signals are sinusoids.
    Therefore, the time-domain radiation force is a simple product rather than a convolution integral.
    With these considerations, the equation of motion can be expressed in the time domain as:
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Under the regular-wave assumption justified in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:irregular-waves}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, all signals are sinusoidal
    and the radiation force reduces to a simple product rather than a convolution integral, yielding the time-domain equation of motion:
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
%DIF <  todo: this is time domain so gamma is not allowed to be complex
\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:eom}%%%
    \mathbf{M}\ddot{\vec\xi}(t)
    = \vec{F}_{e}(t) + \vec{F}_{rad}(t) + \vec{F}_{res}(t) + \vec{F}_{d}(t) + \vec{F}_{p}(t)
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{where, in the frequency domain under monochromatic forcing,
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:eom-forces}%%%
\begin{aligned}
    \vec{F}_{e}(t)   &= \Re\!\left(\vec{\gamma}\,\tilde{\zeta}(t)\right),
    & \vec{F}_{rad}(t) &= -\mathbf{A}_h\ddot{\vec{\xi}}(t) - \mathbf{B}_h\dot{\vec{\xi}}(t), \\
    \vec{F}_{res}(t) &= -\mathbf{K}_h\vec{\xi}(t),
    & \vec{F}_{d}(t)   &= -\mathbf{B}_d\dot{\vec{\xi}}(t) + \Re\!\left(\vec{\gamma}_d\,\tilde{\zeta}(t)\right), \\
    \vec{F}_{p}(t)   &= -\mathbf{B}_p\dot{\vec{\xi}}(t) - \mathbf{K}_p\vec{\xi}(t).
\end{aligned}
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{for both operational and storm conditions, where $\vec{\xi}(t)$ (body position) and $\zeta(t)$ (wave elevation) were defined in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:hydro}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
The mass matrix is:
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\mathbf{M_{op}}=\begin{bmatrix}m_f & 0 \\ 0 & m_s\end{bmatrix}, \quad  \mathbf{M_{storm}}=\begin{bmatrix}m_f + m_s \end{bmatrix}
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{For each force component, a linear or quasi-linear model is adopted, with coefficients $\vec{\gamma}$, $\mathbf{A_h}$, $\mathbf{B_h}$, $\mathbf{K_h}$ from the hydrodynamics module and $\mathbf{B_d}$, $\vec{\gamma}_d$, $\mathbf{B_p}$, and $\mathbf{K_p}$ to be derived in the following sections.
    The excitation coefficients $\vec{\gamma}$ and $\vec{\gamma}_d$ are complex, and all other coefficients are real.
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Hydrodynamic coefficients $\vec{\gamma}$, $\mathbf{A_h}$, $\mathbf{B_h}$, $\mathbf{K_h}$ come from \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:hydro}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
;
    drag coefficients $\mathbf{B_d}$, $\vec{\gamma}_d$ are derived in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:drag}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
;
    PTO coefficients $\mathbf{B_p}$, $\mathbf{K_p}$ are derived in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-pto-model,sec:optimal-control}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    The excitation coefficients $\vec{\gamma}$ and $\vec{\gamma}_d$ are complex;
    all others are real.
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{In the frequency domain, the complex transfer function from real regular wave height $H$ to the vector of complex body velocity phasors $\vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}}$ is then: 
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:eom-freq-domain}%%%
    \frac{\vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}}}{H} = \frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbf{Z}_i+\mathbf{Z}_p\right]^{-1}(\vec{\gamma}+\vec{\gamma}_d)
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{where the inertial, hydrodynamic radiation, hydrostatic restoring, and drag damping terms have been combined into a single intrinsic impedance matrix $\mathbf{Z}_{i}$ for compactness:
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:intrinsic-impedance}%%%
\mathbf{Z}_i = -i\omega(\mathbf{M}+\mathbf{A}_h)+(\mathbf{B}_h+\mathbf{B}_d)+\frac{1}{i\omega}(\mathbf{K}_h)
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The PTO kinematics and dynamicsare yet to be specified, currently represented by generic impedance matrix $\mathbf{Z}_p = \mathbf{B}_p+\frac{\mathbf{K}_p}{i\omega}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{generator dynamics respectively.
The overall relationship between the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{mechanical port and generator electrical port is collectively referred to as the PTO dynamics}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Equations \eqref{eq:eom-freq-domain}-\eqref{eq:intrinsic-impedance} are represented as a multiport circuit (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-intrinsic}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
), with effort variables $\vec{e}$ and flow variables $\vec{q}$ at each port.
Three matrix forms describe }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-multiport} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
derives three matrix forms to describe various aspects of }\DIFaddend the port relationships:
impedance ($\gls{sym-mathbf-Z}$ ) groups all ports together \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and is used for the intrinsic body dynamics}\DIFaddend ;
