cascade (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{a}$/ABCD}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-a}$/}\gls{acr-abcd}\DIFaddend ) transmits efforts and flows sequentially between port groups and is used for the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO dynamics(\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-pto-model}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{dynamics}\DIFaddend ;
hybrid ($\gls{sym-mathbf-h}$ ) mixes the two and is used for the \gls{acr-pto} kinematics.
Full conventions \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, conversion formulas , and the cascade inversion rule }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and conversion formulas }\DIFaddend are given in \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-multiport}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, following \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{reveyrand_multiport_2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-multiport-conventions}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[htbp]
%DIFDELCMD < \centering
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/circuit_intrinsic.pdf}
}%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Multiport circuit for two hydrodynamic degrees of freedom with intrinsic and powertrain impedances $\mathbf{Z}_i$ and $\mathbf{Z}_p$}}
%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:multiport-circuit-intrinsic}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{figure}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Excitation forces are represented as dependent voltage sources (diamonds) driven by a single wave elevation source (circle), and the intrinsic and powertrain dynamics are each represented as a two-port impedance matrix.
The dependent sources and intrinsic impedance matrix are inherently linked through the Haskind relation, which relates excitation coefficient magnitudes $|\gamma_i|$ to diagonal elements of the radiation damping $B_{h,ii}$.
This bounds the maximum absorbed power for any geometry}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Floating Body Intrinsic Dynamics}}
\DIFadd{The float and spar are each modeled as a floating rigid body, and the two bodies are kinematically coupled to transmit force between them.
Forces include wave excitation, hydrodynamic radiation, hydrostatic restoring, drag, and power takeoff.
The excitation, radiation, and restoring forces are linear; the drag and }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{forces are generally nonlinear but represented linearly in the multiport model assuming they have been quasi-linearized.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-body-dynamics} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
provides the equations and derivations for the intrinsic body dynamics, which encompasses the body inertia and all external forces except the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{forces}\DIFaddend .

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{PTO Model}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:pto-dynamics-overview}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The PTO model specifies the generic powertrain impedance matrix $\mathbf{Z}_p$ in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
It }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{Model}}
\label{sec:pto-model-overview}
\DIFadd{The }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{model }\DIFaddend is a linear multiport effort-flow model that is valid for multiple \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degree of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend devices across various energy domains.
It consists of \gls{acr-pto} kinematics
(a hybrid matrix relating body \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom to the PTO }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFadd{to the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFaddend port, accommodating underactuation)
and \gls{acr-pto} dynamics
(cascade matrices for a mechanical drivetrain and permanent magnet generator,
relating the \gls{acr-pto} port to the generator electrical port,
with generator voltage $\gls{sym-hat-V}$ and current $\gls{sym-hat-I}$  as the effort and flow variables).
The Thévenin equivalent circuit method is used to find the effective intrinsic impedance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$Z_{s,th}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Z-s-th}$ }\DIFaddend and open-circuit source voltage \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{V}_{s,th}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}$ }\DIFaddend viewed from the generator electrical port, as required for impedance matching.
Details and circuit diagrams are provided in \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-pto-dynamics}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-pto-kinematics,sec:appendix-combine-intrinsic-kinematics,sec:appendix-pto-dynamics}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Power Production}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Power Production}}
\DIFaddend \label{sec:power}
The instantaneous time-domain power at any port is simply the product of the port effort and flow variables, $\gls{sym-p}(\gls{sym-t}) = \gls{sym-e-t}~\gls{sym-q-t}$ .
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    Substituting \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\{e,q\}=\{F_{p},\dot{\xi}\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\{\gls{sym-e},\gls{sym-q}\}=\{\gls{sym-F-p},\gls{sym-dot-xi}\}$ }\DIFaddend yields the floating body mechanical power,
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\{e,q\}=\{F_{PTO},\dot{X}_{PTO}\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\{\gls{sym-e},\gls{sym-q}\}=\{\gls{sym-F-PTO},\gls{sym-dot-X-PTO}\}$ }\DIFaddend yields the mechanical power at the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO port,
    $\{e,q\}=\{\tau,\Omega\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{port,
    $\{\gls{sym-e},\gls{sym-q}\}=\{\gls{sym-tau},\gls{sym-Omega}\}$ }\DIFaddend yields the generator mechanical power,
    and $\{\gls{sym-e},\gls{sym-q}\}=\{\gls{sym-V},\gls{sym-I}\}$  yields the generator electrical power.
\else
    The same expression is evaluated at the floating body, \gls{acr-pto} , generator mechanical, and generator electrical ports by substituting the corresponding effort/flow pair $\{\gls{sym-e},\gls{sym-q}\}$ .
\fi

In the frequency domain assuming sinusoidal waveforms, the active power $\gls{sym-P}$ , reactive power $\gls{sym-Q}$ , complex power $\gls{sym-S}$ , and apparent power $|\gls{sym-S}|$  from electrical engineering \citep{saadat_power_1999} are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-PQS}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     P &= \tfrac{1}{2}\Re(\hat{e}\hat{q}^*) = \tfrac{1}{2}\Re(Z)|\hat{q}|^2,\\
%DIFDELCMD <     Q &= \tfrac{1}{2}\Im(\hat{e}\hat{q}^*) = \tfrac{1}{2}\Im(Z)|\hat{q}|^2, \\
%DIFDELCMD <     S &= P + iQ = \tfrac{1}{2}\hat{e}\hat{q}^* = \tfrac{1}{2}Z|\hat{q}|^2, \\
%DIFDELCMD <     |S| &= \tfrac{1}{2}|Z||\hat{q}|^2,
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-P} &= \tfrac{1}{2}\Re (\gls{sym-hat-e}\gls{sym-hat-q}^*) = \tfrac{1}{2}\Re (\gls{sym-Z-term})|\gls{sym-hat-q}|^2,\\
    \gls{sym-Q} &= \tfrac{1}{2}\Im (\gls{sym-hat-e}\gls{sym-hat-q}^*) = \tfrac{1}{2}\Im (\gls{sym-Z-term})|\gls{sym-hat-q}|^2, \\
    \gls{sym-S} &= \gls{sym-P} + i\gls{sym-Q} = \tfrac{1}{2}\gls{sym-hat-e}\gls{sym-hat-q}^* = \tfrac{1}{2}\gls{sym-Z-term}|\gls{sym-hat-q}|^2, \\
    |\gls{sym-S}| &= \tfrac{1}{2}|\gls{sym-Z-term}||\gls{sym-hat-q}|^2,
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
where the port is terminated with complex impedance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$Z$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Z-term}$ }\DIFaddend and $(\,)^*$ denotes the conjugate transpose.

The average and peak powers over a wave period, derived in \Cref{sec:appendix-power-time}, are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-avg-peak}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{p_{avg} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-p-avg} \DIFaddend = \gls{sym-P} , \qquad p_{pk} = \gls{sym-P} + |\gls{sym-S} |.
\end{equation}
The corresponding minimum power is $\gls{sym-p}_{\text{min}} = \gls{sym-P} - |\gls{sym-S}|$ , negative when $|\gls{sym-S}|>\gls{sym-P}$  (i.e., reactive control).

To capture non-dynamic power losses such as those in an electrical drive, a constant efficiency $\gls{sym-eta}$  describes the gain between average generator electrical power and average absorbed electrical power.
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    If the generator cascade matrix is not used (i.e.\ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$[\mathbf{a}]_{\tau\Omega\leftarrow VI} = \mathbf{I}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI} = \gls{sym-mathbf-I}$}\DIFaddend ), this efficiency can also approximate losses in the generator itself.
    Note that this is not strictly equivalent to assuming the generator has constant efficiency, since controllers with reactive terms ($\gls{sym-K-l}\neq 0$ ) spend some time expending energy, which reverses the direction of the generator efficiency.
    The average efficiency is assumed to also apply to the peak powers, though this is not guaranteed in general for the same reason.
\fi
Validation in \Cref{sec:validation-benchmarking} sets $\gls{sym-eta}=80\%$  to match the \gls{acr-rm3} design assumption of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{RM3}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{RM3}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

The full expansion of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$p_{\text{avg},\text{elec}}$ and $p_{pk,\text{elec}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-p-avg-elec}$ and $\gls{sym-p-pk-elec}$ }\DIFaddend in terms of the optimal-control variables $\gls{sym-B-l}$, $\gls{sym-K-l}$ , and the electrical-port current phasor $\gls{sym-hat-I}$  is given in \Cref{sec:appendix-power-elec}.

%DIF <  Additionally, the drivetrain mechanical cascade matrix $[a]_m$ is set to the identity matrix, which represents a static effective gear radius $R=1$:
%DIF >  Additionally, the drivetrain mechanical cascade matrix $[\gls{sym-a}]_{\gls{sym-m}}$ is set to the identity matrix, which represents a static effective gear radius $\gls{sym-R-gear}=1$:
% \begin{equation}
% [a]_m = \begin{bmatrix}
%DIF <  1/R &~ 0 \\
%DIF <  0 &~ R
%DIF >  1/\gls{sym-R-gear} &~ 0 \\
%DIF >  0 &~ \gls{sym-R-gear}
% \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix}
% 1 &~ 0 \\
% 0 &~ 1
% \end{bmatrix}
% \end{equation}
%DIF <  With this $[a]_m$, the control damping and stiffness $B_u$ and $K_u$ equal the PTO damping and stiffness $B_p$ and $K_p$.
%DIF >  With this $[\gls{sym-a}]_{\gls{sym-m}}$, the control damping and stiffness $\gls{sym-B-u}$ and $K_u$ equal the PTO damping and stiffness $\gls{sym-B-p}$ and $\gls{sym-K-p}$.
% This will simplify the impedance matching procedure described below, requiring matching at only one interface.

%DIF < The time-domain mechanical power is $P_{\text{mech}}(t) = \tau(t) \Omega(t) = (B_u \Omega(t) + K_u \theta(t) ) \Omega(t) $, where $\theta(t)$ is the generator rotational position. Assuming sinusoidal waveforms, the time-average and peak mechanical power in a given sea state are then
%DIF > The time-domain mechanical power is $\gls{sym-P}_{\text{mech}}(\gls{sym-t}) = \gls{sym-tau}(\gls{sym-t}) \gls{sym-Omega}(\gls{sym-t}) = (\gls{sym-B-u} \gls{sym-Omega}(\gls{sym-t}) + \gls{sym-K-u} \gls{sym-theta}(\gls{sym-t}) ) \gls{sym-Omega}(\gls{sym-t}) $, where $\gls{sym-theta}(\gls{sym-t})$ is the generator rotational position. Assuming sinusoidal waveforms, the time-average and peak mechanical power in a given sea state are then

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Optimal Control}\label{sec:optimal-control}
This subsection presents the unconstrained and constrained optimal-control solutions used in the model\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{,
starting with the linearized dynamics of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:eom} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and building up in complexity to arrive back at the }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{formulation laid out in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:qlps}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

\DIFaddend \paragraph{\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Linearized, }\DIFaddend Unconstrained}
In the absence of constraints, maximizing power at a given port requires impedance matching at that port due to the maximum power transfer theorem.
We apply matching at the generator electrical port, so the electrical load impedance $\gls{sym-Z-l}$  must be the complex conjugate of the Th\'{e}venin equivalent source impedance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$Z_{s,th}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Z-s-th}$ }\DIFaddend seen from that port.
This is called reactive control, with subscript \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$reac$:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\text{reac}$:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:matched-load}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Z_{l,reac} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-Z-l-reac} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Z_{s,th}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-Z-s-th}\DIFaddend ^*
\end{equation}

However, some \glspl{acr-pto} lack reactive capability (the ability to function as a motor and add energy to the system at some times), such as rectifying hydraulic systems or winches where cable tension must be maintained.
Avoiding reactive power ($\gls{sym-Q}=0$ ) at the electrical port restricts the system to so-called damping control, with subscript \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$damp$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\text{damp}$}\DIFaddend , and requires \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$K_{l,\text{damp}}=0$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-K-l-damp}=0$}\DIFaddend .
The power-maximizing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO damping $B_{l,\text{damp}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{damping $\gls{sym-B-l-damp}$ }\DIFaddend is then:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:damping-control}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{B_{l,damp} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-B-l-damp} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\sqrt{ (B_{l,reac})^2 + (K_{l,reac}/\omega)^2}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\sqrt{ (\gls{sym-B-l-reac})^2 + (\gls{sym-K-l-reac}/\gls{sym-omega})^2}
}\DIFaddend \end{equation}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    When a port other than the electrical port must avoid reactive power, the constrained solution detailed later in this section can be used to enforce $\gls{sym-Q}=0$  at that port.

    Both of these controllers frequently result in excessively large forces and thus structural and powertrain requirements, especially in energetic sea states or in reactive control when $\gls{sym-K-l}$  is far from zero.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$K_{l,\text{reac}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-K-l-reac}$ }\DIFaddend is often a large negative number because cost minimization favors a low mass, and the negative \gls{acr-pto} stiffness reduces the natural frequency to better match the ocean wave frequency with a smaller device mass.
    Control laws \eqref{eq:matched-load} and \eqref{eq:damping-control} provide no mechanism to reduce this torque, so below we formulate a control law with the ability to obey constraints.
\else
    Both controllers frequently produce excessively large forces in energetic sea states or under reactive control, and provide no mechanism to obey limits.
    We therefore formulate a control law that handles constraints below.
\fi

\paragraph{\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Linearized, }\DIFaddend Constrained}
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Now we incorporate constraints on the linearized system, yielding the method we term ``constrained impedance matching.''
}\DIFaddend We first compile expressions for all metrics \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$M$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-underline-M}$ }\DIFaddend which must be constrained.
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    For the time being, we keep these expressions generic so they apply to any port.
    To facilitate the calculations in transmission form, we write the metrics as the quadratic product of the port variable vector with a suitable matrix \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{A}_M$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-M}$}\DIFaddend :
\else
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Each metric is written as }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \linelabel{lne:quadratic-metrics-start}
    \DIFadd{Metrics such as active power, reactive power, port variable magnitude, and linear combinations thereof can be formulated as }\DIFaddend a quadratic product of the \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{complex }\DIFaddend port variable vector with a matrix \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{A}_M$:
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-M}$:
    }\linelabel{lne:quadratic-metrics-end}
\DIFaddend \fi
\begin{equation}\label{eq:metrics}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{M }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-underline-M} \DIFaddend = \frac{1}{4} \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <     \hat{e} \\ \hat{q}
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
    \gls{sym-hat-e} \\ \gls{sym-hat-q}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend ^*
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{A}_M
    }%DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <     \hat{e} \\ \hat{q}
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-M}
    \begin{bmatrix}
    \gls{sym-hat-e} \\ \gls{sym-hat-q}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
The matrices for various metrics are given in \Cref{tab:power-metrics}.
\begin{table}[bhp]
\centering
\DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \caption[Matrices for calculating port metrics]{\DIFaddendFL Matrices for calculating various metrics at a port via \Cref{eq:metrics}}
\label{tab:power-metrics}
\DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.1\linewidth}|ccccc}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelFL{Metric $M$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \begin{tabular}{L{0.1\linewidth}|ccccc}
    \DIFaddFL{Metric $\gls{sym-underline-M}$ }\DIFaddendFL & $\gls{sym-p}_{\text{avg}}=\gls{sym-P}$  & $\gls{sym-Q}$  & $\gls{sym-S}$  & $|\gls{sym-hat-e}|^2$  & $|\gls{sym-hat-q}|^2$  \\ \hline
    Matrix \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\mathbf{A}_M$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-M}$ }\DIFaddendFL &
    $\begin{bmatrix}
        0 & 1 \\
        1 & 0
    \end{bmatrix}$
    &
    $\frac{1}{i}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        0 & -1 \\
        1 & 0
    \end{bmatrix}$
    &
    $\begin{bmatrix}
        0 & 0 \\
        2 & 0
    \end{bmatrix}$
    &
    $\begin{bmatrix}
        1 & 0 \\
        0 & 0
    \end{bmatrix}$
    &
    $\begin{bmatrix}
        0 & 0 \\
        0 & 1
    \end{bmatrix}$
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Note that the apparent power $|\gls{sym-S}|$ , and therefore the peak power from \Cref{eq:power-avg-peak}, cannot be written in the quadratic form of \Cref{eq:metrics} and is instead fourth-order:
\begin{equation}
    |\gls{sym-S} |^2 = \gls{sym-S} \gls{sym-S} ^* 
    = \frac{1}{16}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix} \hat{e} \\ \hat{q} \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix} \gls{sym-hat-e} \\ \gls{sym-hat-q} \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend ^*
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{A}_S
    }%DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix} \hat{e} \\ \hat{q} \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-S}
    \begin{bmatrix} \gls{sym-hat-e} \\ \gls{sym-hat-q} \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix} \hat{e} \\ \hat{q} \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix} \gls{sym-hat-e} \\ \gls{sym-hat-q} \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend ^*
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{A}_S^T
    }%DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix} \hat{e} \\ \hat{q} \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-S}\DIFadd{^{\top}
    }\begin{bmatrix} \gls{sym-hat-e} \\ \gls{sym-hat-q} \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
Peak power constraints use a more complicated approach discussed in \Cref{sec:appendix-mag-S-constraints} and are left out of the formulation below for simplicity.

To implement the constraints, we create an intentional linear impedance mismatch, sacrificing power to satisfy the limits (method M2 in \Cref{tab:constraint-approaches}).
For select constraints where a describing function is available (currently the torque limit), we additionally use nonlinear control to satisfy the limit with less of a power sacrifice (method M1.2 in \Cref{tab:constraint-approaches}).

For the linear impedance mismatch, the average electrical power from \Cref{eq:power-avg-peak} can be maximized subject to quadratic constraints on the effort and flow variables.
This is a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{quadratically-constrained quadratic program (QCQP):
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-qcqp}\DIFadd{:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:opt-problem}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD < & \max_{\vec{x} \in \mathbb{C}^2} & p_{avg,VI} &= \frac{1}{4}\vec{x}^{\,*} \mathbf{A}_P \vec{x} \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & \text{s.t.}
%DIFDELCMD < & \vec{x}^{\,*}\mathbf{Q}_i\vec{x}
%DIFDELCMD <     + 2\Re\!\left\{\vec{a}_i^{\,*}\vec{x}\right\}
%DIFDELCMD <     &\le b_i,
%DIFDELCMD <     \quad \forall i \in [1,\cdots,N] \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & & \vec{c}^{\,*}\vec{x} &= d \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & \text{where} 
%DIFDELCMD < & \vec{x} &= \begin{bmatrix} \hat{V} & \hat{I} \end{bmatrix}^T \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & & \mathbf{Q}_i &\in \mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}, \quad \mathbf{Q}_i = \mathbf{Q}_i^{\,*} \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & & \vec{a}_i &\in \mathbb{C}^2 \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & & b_i &\in \mathbb{R} \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & & \vec{c}^{\,*} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & Z_{s,th} \end{bmatrix} \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD < & & d &= \hat{V}_{s,th}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
& \max_{\gls{sym-vec-x} \in \gls{sym-mathbb-C}^2} & \gls{sym-p-avg-VI} &= \frac{1}{4}\gls{sym-vec-x}^{\,*} \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-P} \gls{sym-vec-x} \\[3pt]
& \text{\gls{sym-s}.\gls{sym-t}.}
& \gls{sym-vec-x}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-x}
    + 2\Re\!\left\{\gls{sym-vec-a-mu}^{\,*}\gls{sym-vec-x}\right\}
    &\le \gls{sym-b-mu},
    \quad \forall \gls{sym-mu} \in [1,\cdots,\gls{sym-N}] \\[3pt]
& & \gls{sym-vec-c}^{\,*}\gls{sym-vec-x} &= d \\[3pt]
& \text{where}
& \gls{sym-vec-x} &= \begin{bmatrix} \gls{sym-hat-V} & \gls{sym-hat-I} \end{bmatrix}^{\top} \\[3pt]
& & \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu} &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-C}^{2\times 2}, \quad \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu} = \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}^{\,*} \\[3pt]
& & \gls{sym-vec-a-mu} &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-C}^2 \\[3pt]
& & \gls{sym-b-mu} &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-R} \\[3pt]
& & \gls{sym-vec-c}^{\,*} &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \gls{sym-Z-s-th} \end{bmatrix} \\[3pt]
& & d &= \gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}

The linear equality constraint \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{c}^{\,*}\vec{x} = d$ enforces the WEC and PTO }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-c}^{\,*}\gls{sym-vec-x} = d$ enforces the }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{and }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFaddend dynamics through a voltage-current relationship at the generator electrical port, while the quadratic inequality constraints encode limits on linear or quadratic combinations of effort and flow at any port.
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    The specific quantities constrained in this case are shown in \Cref{tab:qp-constraints}.
    More precisely, this simulation implements constraints on the maximum allowed generator torque and mechanical power, float and spar body amplitudes, and \gls{acr-pto} amplitude, as well as a positivity constraint on the electrical power.
    In addition to the amplitude limits shown in the first three rows of \Cref{tab:qp-constraints}, a differently-structured quadratic amplitude constraint will be presented in \Cref{sec:design-load-cases} (see \Cref{eq:slam-constraint-quadratic}) representing a coupled amplitude-phase criterion for the bodies to not exit the water or be fully submerged.
\else
    Constraints implemented in MDOcean include generator torque, mechanical power, float and spar amplitudes, \gls{acr-pto} amplitude, and electrical power positivity\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{; quadratic coefficients $\{\mathbf{Q}_i, \vec{a}_i, b_i\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
    Quadratic coefficients $\{\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}, \gls{sym-vec-a-mu}, \gls{sym-b-mu}\}$ }\DIFaddend for each are given in \Cref{tab:qp-constraints} (\Cref{sec:appendix-qp-constraints-table}).
    A differently-structured quadratic amplitude constraint for float and spar slamming/submersion is presented in \Cref{sec:design-load-cases}.
\fi

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:opt-problem} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
has the following solution :
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The solution to \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:opt-problem} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
can be written as:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:constrained-qp-solution}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{x}_{opt} &=
%DIFDELCMD <     \frac{\hat{V}_{s,th}}{2\Re(Z_{s,th})}
%DIFDELCMD <         \begin{bmatrix} Z_{s,th}^* (1+\Gamma_{\text{opt}}) \\ 1-\Gamma_{\text{opt}} \end{bmatrix} \\
%DIFDELCMD <     p_{avg,VI,opt} &= \frac{|\hat{V}_{s,th}|^2}{8\Re(Z_{s,th})}~ (1 - |\Gamma_{\text{opt}}|^2)
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-vec-x-opt} &=
    \frac{\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}}{2\Re (\gls{sym-Z-s-th})}
        \begin{bmatrix} Z_{s,th}^* (1+\gls{sym-Gamma-opt}) \\ 1-\gls{sym-Gamma-opt} \end{bmatrix} \\
    \gls{sym-p-avg-VI-opt} &= \frac{|\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}|^2}{8\Re (\gls{sym-Z-s-th})}~ (1 - |\gls{sym-Gamma-opt}|^2)
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Gamma_{\text{opt}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma-opt}$ }\DIFaddend is a complex scalar with magnitude \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{typically }\DIFaddend between 0 and 1 called the optimal reflection coefficient, with formula given in \Cref{eq:gamma-opt-quadratic,eq:candidate-points,eq:circle-intersection} of \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution}.
The reflection coefficient represents the degree to which the constraints introduce an impedance mismatch.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Gamma_{\text{opt}}=0$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma-opt}=0$ }\DIFaddend corresponds to the impedance-matched solution (no active inequality constraints).

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    As \Cref{sec:mod-freq-domain} describes, the solution for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Gamma_{\text{opt}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma-opt}$ }\DIFaddend can be found numerically or analytically, and we further distinguish that the numerical approach can be accomplished with either direct or indirect optimal control.
    Direct methods solve the optimization problem itself, while indirect methods first derive the optimality conditions and then solve those conditions.
    MDOcean currently implements both the analytical approach, which corresponds to the explicit architecture in \Cref{fig:control-arch}, and the numerical indirect approach, which corresponds to the implicit architecture in \Cref{fig:control-arch}.
    The analytical approach is described in \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution}, and the numerical indirect approach in \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-numerical}.

    The numerical direct approach can correspond to either the nested architecture or the simultaneous architecture of \Cref{fig:control-arch}.
    While the nested architecture solves the quadratic program \Cref{eq:opt-problem}, the simultaneous architecture integrates the \gls{acr-qcqp} into the nonlinear design optimization, which does not take advantage of the efficiency available with the \gls{acr-qcqp} formulation.
    It is unknown whether the numerical direct approach to solving the \gls{acr-qcqp} would be more efficient than the numerical indirect approach, but both are expected to be significantly less efficient than the analytical approach, so the analytical approach is used for all results and benchmarks presented here.
\else
    Both analytical and numerical-indirect solutions for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Gamma_{\text{opt}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma-opt}$ }\DIFaddend are implemented in MDOcean.
    This study focuses on the superior analytical approach described in \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution}, which has a geometric interpretation as a minimum-distance problem on the complex plane of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Gamma_{\text{opt}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma-opt}$ }\DIFaddend and is used for all results presented here.
    \citet[Appendix A.3.3]{mccabe_dissertation_2026} describes the numerical-indirect approach.
\fi

\paragraph{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Nonlinear controller}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Constrained, QLPS with Linear Control (M2)}\DIFaddend }
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The previous section on constrained impedance matching describes how to achieve optimal power transfer for a linear dynamic system subject to quadratic constraints.
Our system is quasi-linear, so the linear constrained impedance matching approach is applied iteratively with updated quasi-linear dynamic coefficients and updated constraint limits to approximate the optimal solution.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-dynamic-limit} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
covers the relationship between quasi-linear constraint limits and time-domain constraint limits for each constraint type.
Then, we implement an iterative loop around the dynamics and control module to update the quasi-linear coefficients (in the case of linear control, the only nonlinear coefficient is drag) and constraint limits until convergence is achieved.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Constrained, QLPS with Nonlinear Control (M1.2)}}
\DIFaddend \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    For the nonlinear controller, the maximum generator torque constraint is considered separately from the others.
    This constraint changes the optimal torque signal from a pure sinusoid to a saturated waveform, approaching a square wave for sufficiently low torque limit.
    As is, this nonlinear signal could not be handled with a frequency-domain transfer function and would require time-domain simulation.
    Conveniently, through the describing function method, the saturated signal can be represented with Fourier analysis as a superposition of harmonics.
    Furthermore, because the plant is a second order oscillator and filters out high frequency inputs, harmonics other than the fundamental contain very little energy and can be safely neglected.
    \Cref{fig:desc-fcns}(a) shows this approximation graphically for a saturated-sine force signal.
\else
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The maximum generator torque constraint is handled separately.
    Saturation makes the optimal torque non-sinusoidal (approaching a square wave for low torque limits), which would normally require }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{To extend to a nonlinear controller, the nonlinear coefficients now include both drag and the PTO coefficients, and there is an additional relationship needed between the quasi-linear torque limit and the }\DIFaddend time-domain \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{simulation.
    The describing function method instead represents the saturated signal by its fundamental Fourier component (\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:desc-fcns}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
a), with higher harmonics filtered out by the second-order plant}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{torque limit}\DIFaddend .
\fi

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{figure}[htbp]
%DIFDELCMD < \centering
%DIFDELCMD < \begin{subfigure}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/saturation_desc_fcn.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Generator torque saturation}}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:sat}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{subfigure}
%DIFDELCMD < \hfill
%DIFDELCMD < \begin{subfigure}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/drag_desc_fcn.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Quadratic drag}}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:drag-df}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{subfigure}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelFL{Conceptual demonstration of describing function approximations. Plots show force versus time. Nonlinear signals in red; fundamental amplitudes in blue.}}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{fig:desc-fcns}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{figure}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend Details of the calculation are provided in \Cref{sec:appendix-force-sat}.
The result is updated effective values for control gains $\gls{sym-B-l}$ and $\gls{sym-K-l}$  that represent the nonlinear saturated controller with a torque that obeys the constraint and is used in place of \Cref{eq:constrained-qp-solution}.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Drag}\label{sec:drag}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    The hydrodynamics model of \Cref{sec:hydro} does not capture all fluid forces on the body due to its linear, irrotational, and inviscid assumptions.
    More specifically, viscous shear stresses (skin friction),
    higher-order nonlinear potential flow effects, and
    steady and unsteady viscous separation phenomena are all neglected.
    In this section, we model the form drag due to viscous flow separation, including steady wake losses, turbulence, and cycle-averaged vortex shedding.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{These effects have been found to be }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
finds that these effects are }\DIFaddend the dominant drag force for heaving \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{WECs \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-wec} \DIFaddend and are important to avoid unrealistically high resonant peaks in the response amplitude which would lead to overestimates of power production.
\else
    The linear, inviscid hydrodynamics model of \Cref{sec:hydro} omits viscous flow separation, which is the dominant drag mechanism for heaving \glspl{acr-wec} \citep{quartier_influence_2021}.
    Without modeling drag, the predicted resonant amplitude is unrealistically high and power is overestimated.
\fi

% Maha question: should we just jump to the result instead of deriving p_d approximation? (5/18/26)
Drag force \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{F}_d$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-F-d}$ }\DIFaddend is proportional to the product of the relative velocity of the \gls{acr-wec} and incident wave, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{v}_{\text{rel}}(y,t)=\vec{\dot{\xi}}(t)-\vec{v}_{\text{wave}}(y,t)$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-v-rel}(\gls{sym-y},\gls{sym-t})=\gls{sym-vec-dot-xi}(\gls{sym-t})-\gls{sym-vec-v-wave}(\gls{sym-y},\gls{sym-t})$}\DIFaddend , with its absolute value.
The resulting quadratic form \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$|\vec{F}_d|\sim|\vec{v}_{\text{rel}}|^2$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$|\gls{sym-vec-F-d}|\sim|\gls{sym-vec-v-rel}|^2$ }\DIFaddend implies a nonlinear input-output response that would normally require time-domain simulation.
To improve computational efficiency and allow solution in the frequency domain, the nonlinearity is quasi-linearized with a describing function, following \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .
The pressure along the bottom surface of each body due to drag \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{p}_d(y,t)$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-p-d}$ }\DIFaddend is modeled with empirical drag coefficient vector $\gls{sym-vec-C-d}$:
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:drag-pressure}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{p}%%%
\DIFdel{_{d}(y,t) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-p-d} \DIFaddend = \frac{1}{2}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\rho_w }%DIFDELCMD < \vec{C}%%%
\DIFdel{_d }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-rho-w} \gls{sym-vec-C-d} \DIFaddend ~\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{v}%%%
\DIFdel{_{rel}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-v-rel} \DIFaddend (\gls{sym-y} ,\gls{sym-t} ) |\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{v}%%%
\DIFdel{_{rel}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-v-rel} \DIFaddend (\gls{sym-y} ,\gls{sym-t} )|
\end{equation}
\Cref{eq:drag-pressure-approx} expands the velocity term then applies the describing function approximation:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:drag-pressure-approx}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \left(\vec{v}_{rel}(y,t)~\cdot\right.\\
%DIFDELCMD <     \left.\left|\vec{v}_{rel}(y,t)\right|\right)
%DIFDELCMD <     &= |\vec{\hat{V}}_{rel}(y)|^2 \cos(\omega t+\angle \vec{\hat{V}}_{rel}(y)) 
%DIFDELCMD <     \left| \cos(\omega t+\angle \vec{\hat{V}}_{rel}(y))\right|\\
%DIFDELCMD <     &\approx~|\vec{\hat{V}}_{rel}(y)|^2\frac{8}{3\pi} \cos(\omega t+\angle \vec{\hat{V}}_{rel}(y))
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \left(\gls{sym-vec-v-rel} (\gls{sym-y},\gls{sym-t}) ~\cdot
    \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \else
        \right.\\
        \left.  
    \fi
    \left|\gls{sym-vec-v-rel} (\gls{sym-y},\gls{sym-t}) \right|\right)
    &= |\gls{sym-vec-hat-v-rel} (\gls{sym-y})|^2 \cos (\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\angle \gls{sym-vec-hat-v-rel} (\gls{sym-y}))
    \left| \cos (\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\angle \gls{sym-vec-hat-v-rel} (\gls{sym-y}))\right|\\
    &\approx~|\gls{sym-vec-hat-v-rel} (\gls{sym-y})|^2\frac{8}{3\gls{sym-pi}} \cos (\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\angle \gls{sym-vec-hat-v-rel} (\gls{sym-y}))
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Computation of the relative velocity uses the following expression for the incident wave velocity phasor in finite depth water evaluated at the body draft, with $\vec{T}=[T_{f,2},T_s]$ collecting the bottom draft of the float and spar:
    }\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:wave-velocity-phasor}%%%
        \vec{\hat{V}}_{wave}(y) = \frac{H}{2} \frac{g k}{\omega} e^{-k\vec{T}} e^{-iky}
    }\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelend The relative velocity uses the standard linear-wave velocity phasor evaluated at body draft; the explicit expression is in \Cref{sec:appendix-drag} (\Cref{eq:wave-velocity-phasor}).
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend 

The time-domain nonlinear waveform and describing function approximation for the drag pressure/force are compared in \Cref{fig:desc-fcns}(b).
The amplitude of the sinusoidal approximation equals $\frac{8}{3\gls{sym-pi}}\approx0.85$  times the peak of the nonlinear waveform.

Integrating the drag pressure over the wetted surface via strip theory (\Cref{sec:appendix-drag}) yields a quasi-linearized drag force consisting of a damping term in phase with the body motion and an excitation term potentially out of phase:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:drag-damping-excitation}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{F}}%%%
\DIFdel{_d }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-d} \DIFaddend = -\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{B}_{d} }%DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-B-d} \gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}
    \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\gamma}%%%
\DIFdel{_{d} }%DIFDELCMD < \hat{\zeta}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-gamma-d} \gls{sym-hat-zeta}
\DIFaddend \end{equation}
The excitation term arises from the use of the relative velocity rather than direct \gls{acr-wec} velocity, which is often overlooked in \gls{acr-wec} models and has important implications for the phase of the drag force.
The damping-excitation grouping of the drag terms, as well as the dependence of the relative velocity on the direction of wave propagation $\gls{sym-y}$ , improves on the approach of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend and is discussed more in \Cref{sec:appendix-drag}.
Explicit expressions for $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}$ and $\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$  in terms of nondimensional \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{strip-theory }\DIFaddend integrals over body geometry are given in \Cref{sec:appendix-drag} (\Cref{eq:drag-coeffs}).

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{The nondimensional integrals are computed numerically, and for applications like optimization where the simulation must be run many times, they are pre-computed for a range of nondimensional input values and accessed via a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{These numerical integrals are precomputed and stored as a }\DIFaddend lookup table to \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{reduce runtime.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{support fast optimization.
}\DIFaddend The resulting magnitude and phase of \gls{acr-wec} motion are obtained by numerically \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{iterating }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{converging }\DIFaddend the state-dependent coefficients \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{B}_{d}$ and $\vec{\gamma}_{d}$.
Further derivation, convergence details , }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}$ and $\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-drag} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
discusses details }\DIFaddend and control implications\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{are discussed in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-drag}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{These integrals are precomputed and stored as a lookup table to support fast optimization.
    The state-dependent coefficients $\mathbf{B}_{d}$ and $\vec{\gamma}_{d}$ are converged by fixed-point iteration, typically in 5-8 iterations; details are in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-drag}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. 
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
}\DIFaddend 

%\subsubsection{Power Saturation}
%Equation~\eqref{eq:mech-power} shows that the power peak to maximum ratio is at minimum two for a damping controller,
%\hl{describe}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Energy Production}
\DIFaddbegin \label{sec:energy}
\DIFaddend The analysis thus far \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{has either been }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{either is }\DIFaddend sea-state agnostic (\Cref{sec:geom}) or \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{applied }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{applies }\DIFaddend separately to each individual sea state (\Cref{sec:hydro,sec:dynamics}).
We now combine the results across sea states to find the long-term energy statistics in a given wave environment.

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    While matrices in \Cref{sec:hydro,sec:dynamics} represent coupled relationships between vector output variables (i.e.\ $\gls{sym-vec-hat-xi}$ ) for systems with multiple bodies or \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof}\DIFaddend , in the current section each output element is treated as a scalar, either representing a single element of an output vector (i.e.\ $\gls{sym-hat-xi-f}$ ) or the sum across all \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend (i.e.\ power in systems with multiple actuated \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof}\DIFaddend ).
    These scalars are assembled into ``matrices'' (i.e.\ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{\hat{\xi}}^{H,T}_f$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-boldsymbol-hat-xi-H-T-f}$}\DIFaddend ) indexed by sea state rather than by \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degree of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dof}\DIFaddend , indicated by the $\gls{sym-H},\gls{sym-T-period}$  superscript.
    Such ``matrices'' are incompatible with matrix multiplication and any matrix operations in this section should be interpreted as elementwise operations across sea states.
    To distinguish from the average and maximum signal values over a wave period denoted with \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$avg$ and $pk$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{avg and pk }\DIFaddend subscripts in \Cref{sec:power}, the long-term average across sea states is denoted with an overbar $\overline{(\cdot)}$ and long-term maximum with subscript \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$max$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\text{max}$}\DIFaddend .
    This intentionally matches the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$max$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\text{max}$ }\DIFaddend subscript used in \Cref{tab:qp-constraints} for upper limits within each sea state because MDOcean currently applies the same limit value for a given constraint across all sea states, though this need not be the case in general.
    An exception is the slamming and submergence constraints, which will be discussed separately in \Cref{sec:design-load-cases}.
\else
    Quantities indexed by sea state are written as ``power matrices'' $(\cdot)^{\gls{sym-H},\gls{sym-T-period}}$  (e.g.\ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{P}_{\text{elec}}^{H,T}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-P-H-T-elec}\DIFaddend ),
    with operations interpreted elementwise across sea states rather than as matrix multiplication.
    Long-term averages across sea states are denoted with an overbar $\overline{(\cdot)}$ and long-term maxima with subscript \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$max$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\text{max}$}\DIFaddend ,
    distinguishing them from the within-period averages and peaks (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$avg$, $pk$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{avg, pk}\DIFaddend ) introduced in \Cref{sec:power}.
    A separate quadratic amplitude limit for slamming and submergence, which varies across sea states, is treated in \Cref{sec:design-load-cases}.
\fi

To find the long-term average power production \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\overline{P}_{\text{elec}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-overline-P-elec} \DIFaddend in a location with a given distribution of sea states, the power matrix \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{\text{elec}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-P-H-T-elec} \DIFaddend for each sea state is weighted by that sea state's probability using a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Joint Probability Density (JPD) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-jpd} \DIFaddend matrix and then summed.
This method is illustrated in \Cref{fig:JPD-multiply}.
The \gls{acr-jpd} used in this analysis represents wave conditions in Humboldt Bay, \gls{acr-ca} and is taken from the study by \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{janzou_system_2020}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{janzou_system_2020}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

\begin{figure}[b!]
\centering
\DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/manual/jpd_mult.pdf}
}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/manual/jpd_mult.pdf}]{figs/manual/jpd_mult.pdf}
}\DIFaddendFL \caption{Power matrix multiplication}
\label{fig:JPD-multiply}
\end{figure}

The annual energy production $\gls{acr-aep}$  in kWh/year for a farm of multiple devices is the product of the number of devices, the average power per device, an efficiency \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{%
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
$\eta_{\text{array}}$
}%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-eta-array}$ }\DIFaddend representing transmission losses and array downtime, and an appropriate unit conversion:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:AEP}
    \gls{acr-aep} = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{N_{WEC} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-N-WEC} \DIFaddend ~ \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \overline{P}%%%
\DIFdel{_{elec} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-overline-P-elec} \DIFaddend ~\gls{sym-eta-array} \frac{8766}{1000}.
\end{equation}

%DIF < Finally, the standard deviation $\sigma$ of power across all sea states $P(\omega)$ is computed, and normalized by the average power $\mu$ to get the coefficient of variation (see equation \eqref{cv}).
%DIF > Finally, the standard deviation $\gls{sym-sigma}$ of power across all sea states $\gls{sym-P}(\gls{sym-omega})$ is computed, and normalized by the average power $\gls{sym-mu}$ to get the coefficient of variation (see equation \eqref{cv}).

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Dynamic Limits and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Design Load Cases}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dlc}\DIFaddend }
\label{sec:design-load-cases}
This study considers two design load cases: (1) cyclic operational loading in sea states where power is produced, and (2) storm loading in sea states where the device enters survival mode\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, where the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{locks }\DIFaddend and stops producing power.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{These correspond roughly with the 1-year and 50-year return periods specified in the wave energy design standard IEC TS 62600-2, and with the fatigue limit state (FLS) and ultimate limit state (ULS) in offshore wind standards.
When the WEC is in survival mode, an external brake locks the PTO, enforcing the float and spar to move together without loading the generator.
}%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{This section details the allowable amplitudes and forces in each case.
    Currently all sea states in the JPD with nonzero probability are considered operational , since the generator force and power limit serve to reduce loads in energetic sea states.
    Future studies could control the transition from operational to survival mode more finely, perhaps with an additional input variable representing an operational incident energy threshold.
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{All nonzero-probability sea states in the JPD are considered operational; the generator force and power limits reduce loads in the most energetic sea states.
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{See \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-dynamic-limit} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
for further discussion and derivation.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{The heave forces in equation \eqref{eq:eom} can be separated into fluid forces, which act on the vertically-oriented wetted surface of the body, the PTO force , which acts at the connection between the float and the spar, and the inertial force, which for dynamic purposes is assumed to act at the center of mass but for structural purposes actually acts distributed over the material.
    The distributed nature of the inertial force means that the float and spar structures experience internal loads that vary not only in time but also in space along their lengths.
Due to force balance in dynamic equilibrium, the maximum magnitude of internal heave force in space at any given time, $F_{\text{heave},f}(t)$, is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Relating Peak Forces and Amplitudes to Fundamental}}
\DIFadd{In the operational load case,
}\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{greater of the absolute value of the PTO force and }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{peak PTO force is found from }\DIFaddend the \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{fundamental assuming that the PTO force signal shape is a saturated sine wave and the PTO force constructively interferes with shaft torque (a conservative assumption).
The peak fluid force and peak displacement are assumed to equal their respective fundamental amplitudes.
This is a reasonable approximation for the displacements due to the low-pass nature of the system, and could be an under- or over-estimate for the }\DIFaddend fluid force.
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Expressing the latter as the difference between the inertial force and the PTO force, this gives:
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{By force balance, the peak internal heave force at any cross-section of each body is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin 

\DIFadd{In the storm case, }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{greater of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{peak is assumed to equal the fundamental amplitude for }\DIFaddend the PTO force\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{and the fluid (inertial minus PTO) force:
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:heave-force-decomp}%%%
\begin{aligned}
    F_{heave,f}(t) &=\max\!\left(|F_p(t)|,~ |m_f\ddot{\xi}_f(t)-F_p(t)|\right) \\
    F_{heave,s}(t) &=\max\!\left(|F_p(t)|,~ |m_s\ddot{\xi}_s(t)-F_p(t)|\right)
\end{aligned}
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{In operational seas, $F_p(t)$ is determined by the generator torque $\tau(t)$ cascaded through the PTO dynamics matrix $\mathbf{a}_{F_p\xi\leftarrow \tau\Omega}$.
In storm seas, $F_p(t)$ represents the brake force, and its fundamental amplitude is obtained by solving the 2-DOF equation of motion for the }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, fluid }\DIFaddend force\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{necessary to enforce zero relative motion between the float and spar.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, and displacements.
The assumption applies here for the PTO force since the storm regime uses a brake not a generator, so there is no saturation nonlinearity.
}\DIFaddend \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Maximizing over time requires more care because nonlinearity-induced harmonics cannot be neglected for peak force analysis like they can for power production.
    The peak of $\tau(t)$ is lower than its fundamental amplitude due to generatorforce saturation at $\tau_{\text{max}}$, so using $\tau_{\text{max}}$ rather than $|\hat{\tau}|$ is both less conservative and more realistic.
Assuming conservatively that the shaft torque constructively interferes with the saturated generator torque,
    for the operational case we use:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Note that the analysis of this section continues to use standard linear wave theory with equivalent regular waves,
    even for the storm condition.
    This is a questionable assumption,
    but it is necessary because optimization-suitable models for nonlinear wave forces do not currently exist.
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:discussion} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
will discuss possible future alternatives to this modeling choice.
}\DIFaddend \else
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Saturation caps the operational generator torque at $\tau_{\text{max}}$, which is a less conservative and more realistic peak than the fundamental amplitude $|\hat{\tau}|$.
    Assuming constructive interference with the shaft torque,
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Both load cases use standard linear wave theory with equivalent regular waves,
    a questionable approximation whose limitations and possible future alternatives are discussed in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:unmodeled-effects}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\DIFaddend \fi
\DIFdelbegin \begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:peak-pto-force}%%%
\max_t |F_{p,op}(t)| \approx \frac{1}{R} \left(\left| Z_{shaft} \hat{\Omega} \right| + \tau_{max} \right).
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The storm case lacks the control saturation nonlinearity and uses the fundamental amplitude $\hat{F}_{p,st}$ directly.
    }\DIFdelend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The fluid force nonlinearities $|m\ddot{\xi}(t)-F_p(t)|$ are harder to handle, since unlike the generator torque, the peak of the drag force exceeds its fundamental.
MDOcean currently uses the fundamental amplitude for both load cases:
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:peak-fluid-force}%%%
\max_t \left|m\ddot{\xi}(t)-F_p(t)\right| \approx \left|-m\omega^2 \hat{\xi} - \hat{F}_p\right|.
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{This approximation can either under- or overestimate the true peak depending on whether harmonics interfere constructively or destructively with the fundamental; further discussion is in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-peak-fluid-force}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{The fundamental amplitudes are also used to calculate the peak float and spar displacements $\max_t |\xi_f(t)|$ and $\max_t |\xi_s(t)|$ in both cases.
    Using the fundamental for the response displacements is more accurate than for the forces because the system dynamics filter out high frequency inputs.
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Peak float and spar displacements likewise use fundamental amplitudes; the second-order plant filters out higher harmonics, making the approximation more accurate for displacements than for forces .
}%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD <

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The surge force for each component is calculated as \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{newman_motions_1963}
}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:surge-force}%%%
   F_{surge} = \frac{H\rho_w \omega^2 A_w}{k} (e^{-kz_{\text{top}}}-e^{-kT})
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{using the respective waterplane areas $A_w$  and drafts $T$ for the float and spar.
The top vertical height $z_{\text{top}}$ is set to zero for the float and $T_{f,2}$ for the spar.
The design surge force is the maximum surge force over all nonzero-probability sea states.
Equation~\eqref{eq:surge-force} assumes a slender cylinder with diameter much smaller than the wavelength and may be less accurate for the float due to diffraction effects.
Incorporating semi-analytical hydrodynamic coefficients for surge is a possible area of future work. 
Maximum acceptable heave %DIF < and surge 
forces will be evaluated with structural factors of safety in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:structures}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
The structures model currently lacks the ability to account for surge forces, so the surge force calculation is not used in the simulation.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The table also indicates the maximum permissible amplitudes .
In operational sea states, the geometric }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Permissible Force and Amplitude Limits}}
\DIFadd{Permissible amplitudes must meet the following criteria:
}\begin{itemize}
\item \DIFadd{Geometric }\DIFaddend clearance between the float and spar \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is enforced }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{tops and bottoms }\DIFaddend to prevent overtravel
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
The permissible heights for upward and downward motion of the float relative to the spar are:
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:h-fs-up-down}%%%
\begin{aligned}
    h_{fs,\text{up}} &= h_s - T_s - (h_f- T_{f,2}) \\
    h_{fs,\text{down}} &= T_s - T_{f,2} - h_d
\end{aligned}
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Additionally, a limit of $h_{fs,\text{clear}}$ (see definition in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:dims}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) is required to ensure }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \item \DIFadd{Geometric }\DIFaddend clearance of the tubular structure connecting the float and spar
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The conditions for linear }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \item \DIFadd{Linear }\DIFaddend wave-body interaction \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{are enforced }\DIFdelend to maintain compatibility with the linear potential flow theory approach of \Cref{sec:hydro}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{.
This maximum amplitude $\xi_{\text{linear}}$ for the float and spar respectively is
}\begin{displaymath}
    \DIFdel{\xi_{f,\text{linear}} = \frac{1}{10}(h-T_{f,2}), \quad \xi_{s,\text{linear}}  = \frac{1}{10}(h-T_s)
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \ifdefined\DISSERTATION
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{which is derived by requiring that the height }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \item \DIFadd{Prevent slamming, where the bottom surface rises out }\DIFaddend of the water \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{column below the body (the relevant dimension determining the hydrodynamic coefficients in MEEM) does not change by more than 10\% from its equilibrium value when the body is at its peak displacement}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{and impacts on reentry
}\item \DIFadd{Prevent submersion, where the top of the }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{becomes fully submerged
}\end{itemize}

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{The values $\gls{sym-h-fs-clear}$, $\gls{sym-h-fs-up}$, $\gls{sym-h-fs-down}$,
    $\gls{sym-xi-f-lin}$, and $\gls{sym-xi-s-lin}$
    are all upper limits and are independent of sea state,
    while $\gls{sym-xi-slam}$ depends on the sea state
    and can separately act as both an upper and lower limit on the oscillation magnitude,
    as shown in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-slam}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .
\else
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{derived by requiring the water column height below the body (relevant for the MEEM hydrodynamic coefficients)to vary by no more than 10\% from equilibrium.
    }%DIFDELCMD < \fi
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{In both operational and storm states, the WEC surface should }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The first two limits are static and independent of sea state;
    the third limit is static but depends on the sea state;
    the last two limits are dynamic (depend on the body motion as well as the sea state).
    The last two limits, together called the free surface constraints, ensure that the }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{surface does }\DIFaddend not transiently pierce the wave free surface \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, since this causes }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{since doing so would cause }\DIFaddend extremely high loads.
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This includes slamming (the bottom surface rises out of the water and impacts on reentry) and submersion (the top of the WEC becomes fully submerged).
    The condition to prevent slamming and submersion in regular waves of height $H$ is derived in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-slam}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Each free surface constraint can separately act as both an upper and lower limit on the oscillation magnitude.
    }\DIFaddend These limits depend on the phase of the response, not just the magnitude, so they do not use the quadratic coefficients of \Cref{tab:qp-constraints} but can nonetheless be expressed in a quadratic form compatible with \Cref{eq:opt-problem}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{:
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:slam-constraint-quadratic}%%%
    \hat{\xi}^*\hat{\xi} - H\Re(e^{iky}\hat{\xi}) < \Delta z^2-\left(\frac{H}{2}\right)^2
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{for relevant vertical body dimension $\Delta z$ and horizontal coordinate $y$.
The values of $\Delta z$ used for float and spar in operational and storm cases and the rationale (avoiding slanted-surface slamming, damping plate surfacing, and related effects) are given in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-slam}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{.
}\fi
\DIFaddend 

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The values $h_{fs,\text{clear}}$, $h_{fs,\text{up}}$, $h_{fs,\text{down}}$, $\xi_{f,\text{linear}}$, and $\xi_{s,\text{linear}}$ are all upper limits and are independent of sea state, while $\xi_{\text{slam}}$ depends on the sea state and can separately act as both an upper and lower limit on the oscillation magnitude, as shown in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-slam}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    }\DIFdelend In early design studies that do not directly compute the hydrodynamic coefficients and associated power production,
    the amplitude limits proposed here could be plugged into Budal's upper bound,
    a common analytical expression that combines an upper bound on the excitation force with the maximum permissible body velocity
    to yield an upper bound on power production \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{zou_practical_2023}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Note that the analysis of this section continues to use standard linear wave theory with equivalent regular waves, even for the storm condition.
    This is a questionable assumption, but it is necessary because optimization-suitable models for nonlinear wave forces do not currently exist.
    \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:discussion} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
will discuss possible future alternatives to this modeling choice.
}%DIFDELCMD < \else
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdel{Both load cases use standard linear wave theory with equivalent regular waves, an approximation whose limitations and possible future alternatives are discussed in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:unmodeled-effects}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{zou_practical_2023}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\DIFaddend \fi

\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Permissible forces are determined based on structural criteria to be explained in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:structures}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}
