introduces the use of describing functions to quantify the fundamental amplitude of non-sinusoidal force waveforms, which can achieve higher powers than the equivalent sinusoidal waveform for the same force limit.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{hall_open-source_2022} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
documents the }\gls{acr-raft} \DIFadd{open-source Python package for offshore wind turbines, which uses the drag describing function with iteration but does not include describing functions for saturation.
}\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{The indirect implementation of describing functions for the force saturation nonlinearity is possible because the describing function }\gls{acr-qlps} \DIFadd{method is used for both controller synthesis and evaluation, so the time-domain nonlinear optimal controller that is output from the right of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-synthesis} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
is fed into the top left of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:mod-freq-domain-evaluation}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
,
    resulting in a chaining of the inverse control describing function with the forward control describing function, which cancel out.
    Unless intentionally made different to study robustness, the linearized plant model is identical for the synthesis and evaluation steps.
    In this case, two workflows essentially become one, with no further computation required after the optimal control step.
    Reconstruction of the nonlinear controller is not necessary for the evaluation of the power corresponding to that controller.
    The power can be calculated directly from the frequency-domain response and the describing function of the nonlinear controller.
    The time-domain representation of the nonlinear controller is only necessary for hardware implementation, or if the synthesized controller is to be evaluated with a different method such as time-stepping}\DIFaddend .
\DIFaddbegin \else
    \DIFadd{Because the same describing function is applied in both synthesis and evaluation, the inverse and forward describing functions cancel;
    the time-domain nonlinear controller need not be reconstructed for power evaluation, only for hardware implementation.
}\fi
\DIFadd{The simulation permits the nonsinusoidal waveform to have a maximum fundamental a factor of $\frac{4}{\gls{sym-pi}}$ above the force limit.
This coincides with the fundamental to peak ratio of a square wave, which is the limiting case both for the bang-singular-bang controller (a piecewise-discontinuous sine-square wave combination that \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{hendrikx_optimal_2017} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
finds as the analytically optimal solution), and for the saturated sine controller (a piecewise-continuous sine-square wave combination that \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{coe_initial_2020} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
finds as numerically optimal).
In fact, the optimization need not specify the exact nonlinear waveform, since under the describing function filtering hypothesis, only the fundamental matters for simulation purposes.
For hardware implementation purposes, the nonlinear controller can be obtained for the saturated-sine controller by the process outlined in the study by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{mccabe_force-limited_2024}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

\DIFadd{The appropriate complex scale factor from the optimal unsaturated controller (damping or reactive respectively) is found as described in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
and will be real for the damping control case and complex in the reactive control case.
}

%DIF > Mathematically, a saturation factor $0< \gls{sym-f}_{sat}\leq 1$ is computed as the ratio between the fundamental amplitude of the saturated torque and the original unsaturated torque signal,
%DIF > \begin{equation}
%DIF >     f_{sat} = \min\left(\alpha\frac{\tau_{max}}{\tau}, 1\right),
%DIF > \end{equation}
%DIF > where $\gls{sym-tau}_{\text{max}}$ is the maximum permissible generator torque and $\gls{sym-alpha}$ is the ratio between the fundamental amplitude of the saturated waveform and the maximum torque $\gls{sym-tau}_{\text{max}}$, using the describing function derived in \citep{mccabe_force-limited_2024},
%DIF > \begin{equation}
%DIF >     \alpha = \frac{2}{\pi} \left(\frac{F_p}{\tau_{max}}\sin^{-1}\frac{\tau_{max}}{F_p} +  \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{\tau_{max}}{F_p}\right)^2} \right).
%DIF > \end{equation}

%DIF > When $\frac{\gls{sym-F-p}}{\gls{sym-tau}_{\text{max}}}$ = 1 (no saturation), then $\gls{sym-alpha}= 1$;
%DIF > when $\frac{\gls{sym-F-p}}{\gls{sym-tau}_{\text{max}}} \rightarrow \infty$ (full saturation), then $\gls{sym-alpha}= \frac{4}{\gls{sym-pi}}$.
%DIF > This makes sense because $\frac{4}{\gls{sym-pi}}$ is the harmonic amplitude of a unit square wave,
%DIF > and in full saturation the saturated signal approaches a square wave.
%DIF > $\gls{sym-alpha}$ is plotted in \Cref{fig:alpha-fit}.

%DIF > The factor $\gls{sym-f-sat}$ describes the ratio of the saturated and unsaturated torque signal.
%DIF > Previous work \citet{mccabe_multidisciplinary_2022} implies that this is also the ratio of the saturated and unsaturated control gains, $\gls{sym-m-sat}$, but this is not the case because the \gls{acr-wec} response also changes with saturation.

%DIF >  %Substituting $\gls{sym-f-sat}$ back into the equation of motion reveals that the control gain saturation multiplier $\gls{sym-m-sat}$ is the solution to a quadratic equation $\gls{sym-a-q} \gls{sym-m-sat}^2 + \gls{sym-b-q} \gls{sym-m-sat} + \gls{sym-c-q} = 0$. If the uncontrolled dynamics have a natural frequency $\gls{sym-omega}_{\gls{sym-n},\gls{sym-u}}$ and damping ratio $\gls{sym-zeta-u}$, and the controlled unsaturated dynamics have natural frequency $\gls{sym-omega-n}$ and damping ratio $\gls{sym-zeta}$, then the quadratic coefficients can be expressed as:

%DIF >  \begin{equation}\label{eq:abc-matrix}
%DIF >      \begin{bmatrix}
%DIF >          a_q \\ b_q \\ c_q
%DIF >      \end{bmatrix}
%DIF >      =
%DIF >      \begingroup % keep the row spacing change local
%DIF >      \setlength\arraycolsep{2pt}
%DIF >      \begin{bmatrix} -4 & \frac{4}{f_{sat}^2} & \frac{1}{f_{sat}^2} & -1 & 0 & 0\\
%DIF >      8 & 0 & 0 & 2 & 8 & 2\\
%DIF >      -4 & -4 & -1 & -1 & -8 & -2 \end{bmatrix}
%DIF >      \endgroup
%DIF >      \begin{bmatrix}
%DIF >   (\theta_1)^2  \\
%DIF >    (\theta_2)^2 \\
%DIF >     (\theta_3)^2 \\
%DIF >     (\theta_4)^2  \\
%DIF >     \theta_1 \theta_2 \\
%DIF >      \theta_3 \theta_4
%DIF >      \end{bmatrix}
%DIF >  \end{equation}
%DIF >  where the basis vector $\vec{\gls{sym-theta}}$ is a dimensionless function of the natural frequencies and damping ratios:
%DIF >  \begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-basis}
%DIF >          \begin{bmatrix}
%DIF >    \theta_1  \\
%DIF >    \theta_2 \\
%DIF >     \theta_3 \\
%DIF >     \theta_4  \\
%DIF >      \end{bmatrix} =     \begin{bmatrix}
%DIF >  ~ \overline{\omega} (\zeta_u - \overline{\omega}_n\zeta)  \\
%DIF >    \overline{\omega} ~\overline{\omega}_n \zeta \\
%DIF >    \overline{\omega}^2 - \overline{\omega}_n^2\\
%DIF >     \overline{\omega}_n^2 - 1  \\
%DIF >      \end{bmatrix}
%DIF >  \end{equation}
%DIF >  and the overlined frequencies indicate nondimensionalization by the uncontrolled natural frequency: $\overline{\gls{sym-omega}} = \frac{\gls{sym-omega}}{\gls{sym-omega}_{\gls{sym-n},\gls{sym-u}}}, \overline{\gls{sym-omega}}_{\gls{sym-n}} = \frac{\gls{sym-omega-n}}{\gls{sym-omega}_{\gls{sym-n},\gls{sym-u}}}$. The quadratic equation is then solved for $\gls{sym-m-sat}$, and of the two roots, the one that is real-valued and between 0 and 1 is used.

%DIF >  The no saturation case ($\gls{sym-f-sat} = 1$) for \eqref{abc-matrix} yields $\gls{sym-m-sat}=1$. This confirms the expected behavior that when no force reduction is required, the powertrain coefficients can remain unchanged.

%DIF >  Equations \eqref{eq:abc-matrix} and \eqref{eq:theta-basis} are an equivalent reformulation of \citet{mccabe_force-limited_2024}, which uses expressions for the complex reflection coefficient $\gls{sym-Gamma}$ common in electrical engineering, rather than the damping ratio and natural frequency more common in mechanical engineering. The equivalence is:
%DIF >  \begin{equation}
%DIF >      \Gamma = \frac{z-1}{z+1}, \quad z = \frac{2\theta_1 + i~ \theta_4}{-2 (\theta_1+\theta_2) + i~(\theta_3+\theta_4)}
%DIF >  \end{equation}
%DIF >  % I am inputting zeta and omega_n corresponding to mechanical dynamcis when calculating m_sat, but I really should be inputting them corresponding to electrical dynamics. Maybe describe that?

%DIF >  The gain saturation multiplier $\gls{sym-m-sat}$ is then used to modify the powertrain coefficients,
%DIF >  \begin{align}
%DIF >      B_{p,sat} &= m_{sat} B_p, & K_{p,sat} &= m_{sat} K_p.
%DIF >  \end{align}
%DIF >  and these new control gains are then plugged into equation \eqref{eq:eom-freq-domain} to find the saturated displacement amplitude $\gls{sym-X}_{sat}$.


\subsection{\DIFadd{Multiport Model Details}}
\label{sec:appendix-multiport}
\DIFadd{To implement the multiport model of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:dynamics}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, we must specify its
conventions, body dynamics, }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{kinematics, }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{dynamics, power relations, and ultimate solution procedure.
}\DIFaddend This section provides a general structure in effort-flow form applicable across multiple energy domains for any number of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof}\DIFaddend , as well as specific equations for a common \gls{acr-pto} structure consisting of a mechanical drivetrain and a permanent magnet generator.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Multiport Circuit Conventions}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:appendix-multiport}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \label{sec:appendix-multiport-conventions}
\DIFaddend 

The intrinsic, powertrain, and Th\'evenin-equivalent representations used throughout \Cref{sec:dynamics} are all instances of multiport circuit models,
with effort variables $\gls{sym-vec-e}$  and flow variables $\gls{sym-vec-q}$  at each port.
Following \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{reveyrand_multiport_2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{reveyrand_multiport_2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend , three matrix forms describe the relationships between port variables:
impedance ($\gls{sym-mathbf-Z}$ ), cascade ($\gls{sym-mathbf-a}$ , also called \gls{acr-abcd} ), and hybrid ($\gls{sym-mathbf-h}$):
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:multiport-matrix-definitions}
    \vec{e} = \gls{sym-mathbf-Z} \,\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{q}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-q}\DIFaddend ,
\qquad
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \vec{e}_2 \\ \vec{q}_2
    \end{bmatrix}
    = [\gls{sym-mathbf-a} ]_{2\leftarrow 1}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \vec{e}_1 \\ \vec{q}_1
    \end{bmatrix},
\qquad
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \vec{e}_1 \\ \vec{q}_2
    \end{bmatrix}
    = [\gls{sym-mathbf-h} ]_{1,2}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix} \vec{q}_1 \\ \vec{e}_2 \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-vec-q}_1 \\ \vec{e}_2
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend .
\end{equation}
These equations apply to both time-domain and phasor representations of each port.
The three forms differ in how the ports are grouped:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Impedance} ($\gls{sym-mathbf-Z}$ )
        groups all ports together, describing the effort required to produce a unit of flow.
        The intrinsic dynamics matrix \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{Z}_i$ and the PTO impedance
        $\mathbf{Z}_p$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}$ and the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{impedance
        $\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-p}$ }\DIFaddend in \Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain,eq:intrinsic-impedance} both use this form.
    \item \textbf{Cascade} ($\gls{sym-mathbf-a}$ )
        divides the ports into two groups and describes how effort and flow transmit from one group to the other.
        The arrow subscript indicates direction:
        $[\gls{sym-mathbf-a}]_{2\leftarrow 1}$  maps port group 1 to port group 2.
        Reversing direction inverts the matrix,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:cascade-inverse}
            [\gls{sym-mathbf-a} ]_{1\leftarrow 2} = [\gls{sym-mathbf-a} ]_{2\leftarrow 1}^{-1},
        \end{equation}
        and sequential multiplication represents serial composition of two-port elements.
        This form is used for the \gls{acr-pto} dynamics (drivetrain followed by generator).
    \item \textbf{Hybrid} ($\gls{sym-mathbf-h}$ )
        mixes the two conventions, expressing one effort and one flow at the two port groups as functions of the complementary variables.
        This form is used for the \gls{acr-pto} kinematics, where it naturally accommodates underactuation.
\end{itemize}

Conversion between the three forms is accomplished using the formulas \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{in reference \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{reveyrand_multiport_2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{proposed by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{reveyrand_multiport_2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .
Some port relationships are physically well-defined in one convention but degenerate in another (for example, an ideal current source has no impedance representation but a well-defined hybrid representation) so the convention is chosen to match the physics of each subsystem.

%DIF <  Verify against \cite{coe_co-design_2025} before submission and edit
%DIF >  Verify against \citet{coe_co-design_2025} before submission and edit
% if the convention differs.
The sign convention adopted here treats both flows as positive when entering the multiport,
matching \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{coe_co-design_2025}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{the suggestion of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{coe_co-design_2025}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

%DIF < %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Time-Domain Expansion of Instantaneous Power}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Body Dynamics}\DIFaddend }
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:appendix-power-time}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \label{sec:appendix-body-dynamics}
\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-intrinsic} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
presents the multiport circuit representation of the floating body intrinsic dynamics.
Forces include wave excitation $\gls{sym-vec-F-e}$, hydrodynamic radiation $\gls{sym-vec-F-rad}$, hydrostatic restoring $\gls{sym-vec-F-res}$, drag $\gls{sym-vec-F-d}$, and power takeoff $\gls{sym-vec-F-p}$.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Equation~\eqref{eq:power-PQS} can be used to rewrite the instantaneous power $p(t)$ at any port as\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{saadat_power_1999}
}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:power-time-PQS}%%%
    p(t) = P + |S|\cos\!\left(2(\omega t + \angle\hat{e}) - \tan^{-1}\!\left(\frac{Q}{P}\right)\right),
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{a DC offset of $P$ plus an oscillation at $2\omega$ with amplitude $|S|$.
The average and peak powers in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:power-avg-peak} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
follow directly:
$p_{\text{avg}} = P$ since the oscillation averages to zero, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
    \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/circuit_intrinsic.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/circuit_intrinsic.pdf}
}\caption[Multiport circuit for two hydrodynamic \glspl{acr-dof}]{\DIFaddFL{Multiport circuit for two hydrodynamic }\glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddFL{with intrinsic and powertrain impedances $\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}$ and $\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-p}$}}
\label{fig:multiport-circuit-intrinsic}
\end{figure}

\DIFadd{In \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-intrinsic}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, excitation forces are represented as dependent voltage sources (diamonds) driven by a single wave elevation source (circle), and the intrinsic and powertrain dynamics are each represented as a two-port impedance matrix.
The dependent sources and intrinsic impedance matrix are inherently linked through the Haskind relation, which relates excitation coefficient magnitudes $|\gls{sym-gamma}|$ to diagonal elements of the radiation damping $\gls{sym-B-h}$.
This bounds the maximum absorbed power for any geometry.
}

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{The MDOcean dynamic model assumes regular waves for reasons to be detailed in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:irregular-waves}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, so the system experiences monochromatic wave forcing.
    Combined with the linearity of the model, this implies that all signals are sinusoids.
    Therefore, the time-domain radiation force is a simple product rather than a convolution integral.
    With these considerations, the equation of motion can be expressed in the time domain as:
}\else
    \DIFadd{Under the regular-wave assumption justified in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:irregular-waves}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, all signals are sinusoidal
    and the radiation force reduces to a simple product rather than a convolution integral, yielding the time-domain equation of motion:
}\fi
%DIF >  todo: this is time domain so gamma is not allowed to be complex
\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:eom}
    \gls{sym-mathbf-M}\ddot{\vec\xi} (\gls{sym-t})
    = \gls{sym-vec-F-e} + \gls{sym-vec-F-rad} + \gls{sym-vec-F-res} + \gls{sym-vec-F-d} + \gls{sym-vec-F-p} (\gls{sym-t})
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{where, in the time domain under monochromatic forcing,
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:eom-forces}
\begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-vec-F-e}   &= \Re\!\left(\gls{sym-vec-gamma}\,\gls{sym-tilde-zeta} (\gls{sym-t})\right),
    & \gls{sym-vec-F-rad} &= -\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h}\gls{sym-ddot-vec-xi} (\gls{sym-t}) - \gls{sym-mathbf-B-h}\gls{sym-dot-vec-xi} (\gls{sym-t}), \\
    \gls{sym-vec-F-res} &= -\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h}\gls{sym-vec-xi} (\gls{sym-t}),
    & \gls{sym-vec-F-d}   &= -\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}\gls{sym-dot-vec-xi} (\gls{sym-t}) + \Re\!\left(\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}\,\gls{sym-tilde-zeta} (\gls{sym-t})\right), \\
    \gls{sym-vec-F-p} (\gls{sym-t})   &= -\gls{sym-mathbf-B-p}\gls{sym-dot-vec-xi} (\gls{sym-t}) - \gls{sym-mathbf-K-p}\gls{sym-vec-xi} (\gls{sym-t}).
\end{aligned}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{for both operational and storm conditions.
$\gls{sym-vec-xi}(\gls{sym-t})$ (body position) and $\gls{sym-zeta}$ (wave elevation) were defined in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:hydro}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
The mass matrix for the operational and storm cases is given by:
}\begin{equation}
    \DIFadd{\gls{sym-M-op}=
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-m-f} & 0 \\ 0 & \gls{sym-m-s}
    \end{bmatrix},
    \quad
    \gls{sym-M-storm}=
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-m-f} + \gls{sym-m-s}
    \end{bmatrix}
}\end{equation}

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{For each force component, a linear or quasi-linear model is adopted, with coefficients $\gls{sym-vec-gamma}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-h}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h}$ from the hydrodynamics module and $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}$, $\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-p}$, and $\gls{sym-mathbf-K-p}$ to be derived in the following sections.
    The excitation coefficients $\gls{sym-vec-gamma}$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$p_{pk} = P + |S|$ at the cosine maximum.
    The minimum $p_{\text{min}} = P - |S|$ is negative when $|S|>P$, corresponding to instants when energy flows from the load back into the source under reactive control}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$ are complex, and all other coefficients are real.
}\else
    \DIFadd{Hydrodynamic coefficients $\gls{sym-vec-gamma}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-h}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h}$ come from \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:hydro}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
;
    drag coefficients $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}$, $\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$ are derived in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:drag}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
;
    }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{coefficients $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-p}$, $\gls{sym-mathbf-K-p}$ are derived in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-pto-kinematics,sec:appendix-combine-intrinsic-kinematics,sec:appendix-pto-dynamics,sec:optimal-control}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
    The excitation coefficients $\gls{sym-vec-gamma}$ and $\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$ are complex;
    all others are real.
}\fi

\DIFadd{In the frequency domain, the complex transfer function from real regular wave height $\gls{sym-H}$ to the vector of complex body velocity phasors $\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}$ is then:
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:eom-freq-domain}
    \frac{\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}}{\gls{sym-H}} = \frac{1}{2}\left[\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}+\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-p}\right]^{-1} (\gls{sym-vec-gamma}+\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d})
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{where the inertial, hydrodynamic radiation, hydrostatic restoring, and drag damping terms have been combined into a single intrinsic impedance matrix $\gls{sym-mathbf-Z}_{i}$ for compactness:
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:intrinsic-impedance}
\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i} = -i\gls{sym-omega} (\gls{sym-mathbf-M}+\gls{sym-mathbf-A-h})+ (\gls{sym-mathbf-B-h}+\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d})+\frac{1}{i\gls{sym-omega}} (\gls{sym-mathbf-K-h})
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{kinematics and dynamics are yet to be specified, currently represented by generic impedance matrix $\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-p} = \gls{sym-mathbf-B-p}+\frac{\gls{sym-mathbf-K-p}}{i\gls{sym-omega}}$}\DIFaddend .

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Electrical-Port Power in Optimal-Control Variables}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{Kinematics and Underactuation}\DIFaddend }
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:appendix-power-elec}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \label{sec:appendix-pto-kinematics}
\DIFadd{In this section and the next, we turn our attention to the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{kinematics and dynamics, to specify the generic impedance matrix $\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-p}$ in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Expressing the average and peak powers at the electrical port in terms of the constrained-control parameters introduced in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
(control damping $B_l$, control stiffness$K_l$, and current phasor $\hat{I}$), and applying the constant electrical efficiency $\eta$, }\begin{displaymath}\DIFdel{%DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:power-elec}%%%
\begin{bmatrix}
p_{avg,elec} \\
p_{pk,elec}
\end{bmatrix}
=
\eta
\begin{bmatrix}
p_{avg,VI} \\
p_{pk,VI}
\end{bmatrix}
=
\eta \tfrac{1}{2} B_l |\hat{I}|^2
\begin{bmatrix}
1 \\
1 + \sqrt{1 + \left(\tfrac{K_l}{B_l\omega}\right)^2}
\end{bmatrix}.
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{A }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{model must capture the dynamics of any elements that transmit, transduce, or condition power between the rigid-body mechanical energy and the end energy product, in this case electricity.
Relevant dynamics include the inertia, stiffness, static friction, mechanical advantage, losses, and electromagnetic coupling of flywheels, shafts, springs, gears, hydraulic circuits, generators, and power electronics.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{penalba_review_2016} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
review }\gls{acr-wec} \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{models and \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{coe_co-design_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
synthesize these into a multiport impedance matching network framework to facilitate }\gls{acr-ccd}\DIFadd{.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{coe_co-design_2025} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
present a }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{model in cascade matrix form for a single }\gls{acr-dof} \DIFadd{system.
}\DIFaddend The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{peak-power form is used as the apparent-power constraint in the QCQP of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
%DIF < %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{\DIFdel{PTO Kinematics and Underactuation}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The PTO }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \DIFaddend kinematics describe the relationship between \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{WEC body displacements $\vec{\hat{\xi}}$ to PTO displacements $\vec{\hat{X}}_{PTO}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{body displacements $\gls{sym-vec-hat-xi}$ to }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{displacements $\gls{sym-vec-hat-X-PTO}$}\DIFaddend ,
determined by linkages or similar mechanisms and not including subsequent \gls{acr-pto} elements such as a mechanical drivetrain or generator.
In general form, the \gls{acr-pto} kinematics can be expressed as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pto-kinematics-motion}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{X}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{PTO} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-X-PTO} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{T}_{kin}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}\DIFaddend ~ \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{\xi}}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-xi}
\DIFaddend \end{equation}
with kinematic transformation matrix \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{T}_{kin}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}$ }\DIFaddend that may depend on the body displacements $\gls{sym-vec-hat-xi}$  if the linkages are nonlinear,
but hereafter is assumed constant for simplicity.
This assumption means that \Cref{eq:pto-kinematics-motion} can be differentiated trivially to yield the same matrix relationship for velocities.
Assuming power conservation across the linkages,
the kinematics describe the relationship between not only motions but also forces:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pto-kinematics-force}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{F}}%%%
\DIFdel{_p }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-p} \DIFaddend = [\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{T}_{kin}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}\DIFaddend ]\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{^T  }%DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{F}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{PTO}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{^\top  }\gls{sym-vec-hat-F-PTO}
\DIFaddend \end{equation}
To avoid confusion, subscript $PTO$ is used for forces in the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend (post-kinematics),
while subscript $\gls{sym-p}$  denotes forces in the hydrodynamic body \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend (pre-kinematics).
\Cref{eq:pto-kinematics-motion,eq:pto-kinematics-force} imply rigid lossless massless linkages,
and any linkage deflection, dissipation, and inertia would be included in the \gls{acr-pto} dynamics rather than kinematics.

Multibody \glspl{acr-wec} are frequently underactuated,
meaning that the \gls{acr-pto} has fewer controlled \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend than the hydrodynamic bodies,
and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{T}_{kin}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}$ }\DIFaddend has more columns than rows.
Underactuated systems receive considerable attention in robotics \citep{tedrake_underactuated_2024}
but have only recently been explored in the context of wave energy conversion \citep{faedo_principle_2022}.
Cascade matrices require an identical number of input and output ports
and thus cannot be used to describe the unbalanced ports that underactuated kinematics create
\citep{reveyrand_multiport_2018}.
Non-square impedance matrices may be used to capture unbalanced port relations in general,
but the impedance is undefined for the specific relations of
\Cref{eq:pto-kinematics-motion,eq:pto-kinematics-force}.
Instead, the kinematics can be packaged into a square hybrid matrix \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$[\mathbf{h}]_{kin}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-h-kin}$
}\DIFaddend which accurately represents the underactuated system:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:h-matrix-kinematics}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         \vec{\hat{F}}_p \\
%DIFDELCMD <         \vec{\hat{\dot{X}}}_{PTO}
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-p} \\
        \gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-X-PTO}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
    = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \underbrace{
%DIFDELCMD <         \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <             \mathbf{0} & \mathbf{T}_{kin}^T \\
%DIFDELCMD <             \mathbf{T}_{kin} & \mathbf{0}
%DIFDELCMD <         \end{bmatrix}}
%DIFDELCMD <         %%%
\DIFdel{_{[\mathbf{h}]_{kin}}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <         \vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}} \\ \vec{\hat{F}}_{PTO}
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \underbrace{
        \begin{bmatrix}
            \mathbf{0}             & \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}^{\top} \\
            \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin} & \mathbf{0}
        \end{bmatrix}}
        \DIFadd{_{\gls{sym-mathbf-h-kin}}
    }\begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi} \\ \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-PTO}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}

Here, we model two hydrodynamic \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend (heave of the float and spar respectively) and a single \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO degree of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend corresponding to the relative heave motion between the float and spar.
The kinematics matrix is therefore:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:T-kin-specific}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{T}_{kin} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin} \DIFaddend = \begin{bmatrix}1 & -1\end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
Thus, even absent any mechanism with ``kinematics'' in the traditional sense,
the kinematics matrix is filled with zeroes, ones, and negative ones to represent which \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degrees of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \glspl{acr-dof} \DIFaddend are controlled.

In the circuit representation with two hydrodynamic and one \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO degree of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \gls{acr-dof}\DIFaddend ,
the hybrid matrix \Cref{eq:h-matrix-kinematics} can be represented as a three-port element,
as shown in \Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-kinematics}.
\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=.7\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/circuit_kinematics.pdf}
}%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/circuit_kinematics.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/circuit_kinematics.pdf}
    }\caption[Multiport circuit with powertrain kinematics and Th\'{e}venin equivalent]{\DIFaddendFL Multiport circuit with powertrain kinematics represented as a hybrid matrix, followed by the Th\'{e}venin equivalent of the intrinsic dynamics and kinematics}
    \label{fig:multiport-circuit-kinematics}
\end{figure}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Combining Intrinsic Dynamics with \gls{acr-pto} Kinematics}
\DIFaddbegin \label{sec:appendix-combine-intrinsic-kinematics}
\DIFaddend Taking the Th\'{e}venin equivalent of the intrinsic dynamics and kinematics at the \gls{acr-pto} port yields a scalar equivalent circuit for each \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO degree of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \gls{acr-dof}\DIFaddend ,
with force sources \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{\hat{F}}_{th}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-hat-F-th}$ }\DIFaddend and mechanical impedances \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{Z}_{i,th}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-Z-i-th}$}\DIFaddend ,
shown in the bottom portion of \Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-kinematics} and computed with the following expressions:
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \begin{equation}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{\hat{F}}_{th} &= (\mathbf{T}_{kin}\mathbf{T}_{kin}^T)^{-1} \mathbf{T}_{kin} \mathbf{Z}_{i}^{-1} \vec{\gamma}\hat{\zeta}
%DIFDELCMD <     = \frac{1}{2} [1,~-1] \mathbf{Z}_{i}^{-1} \vec{\gamma}\hat{\zeta} \\
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{Z}_{i,th} &= (-\mathbf{T}_{kin}\mathbf{Z}_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{T}_{kin}^T)^{-1} = -\frac{\det(\mathbf{Z}_{i})}{\Sigma \mathbf{Z}_{i}} = -\frac{Z_{i,f}Z_{i,s}-Z_{i,c}^2}{Z_{i,f}+Z_{i,s}+2Z_{i,c}}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-vec-hat-F-th} &= (\gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}\gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}^{\top})^{-1}
                               \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin} \gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}^{-1} \gls{sym-vec-gamma} \gls{sym-hat-zeta}
    = \frac{1}{2} [1,~-1] \gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}^{-1} \gls{sym-vec-gamma} \gls{sym-hat-zeta} \\
    \gls{sym-vec-Z-i-th} &= (-\gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}^{-1} \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}^{\top})^{-1}
                          = -\frac{\det(\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i})}{\gls{sym-Sigma} \gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}}
                          = -\frac{\gls{sym-Z-i-f}\gls{sym-Z-i-s}-\gls{sym-Z-i-c}^2}{\gls{sym-Z-i-f}+\gls{sym-Z-i-s}+2\gls{sym-Z-i-c}}
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
where the vector expressions for the general case are shown first,
followed by the scalar result for the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{T}_{kin}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}$ }\DIFaddend in \Cref{eq:T-kin-specific} with 1 controlled \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degree of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dof}\DIFaddend .

Although, as previously mentioned, a true cascade matrix must have the same number of input and output ports, it is possible to formulate a cascade-like matrix that can transform from the \gls{acr-pto} port to the body ports.
However, it cannot transform in the other direction due to underactuation.

Even in the favorable direction, this matrix cannot transform to the body ports directly.
Due to the wave excitation, body velocities can be nonzero even when the \gls{acr-pto} force and velocity are both zero.
Hence, there is no way to express $\gls{sym-hat-dot-xi}$  as a weighted sum of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{F}_{PTO}$ and $\dot{\hat{X}}_{PTO}$.
$\hat{\dot{\xi}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-F-PTO}$ and $\gls{sym-dot-hat-X-PTO}$.
$\gls{sym-hat-dot-xi}$ }\DIFaddend could be expressed as a function of the \gls{acr-pto} variables by substituting
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{Z}_p=\mathbf{T}_{kin}^T (\vec{\hat{F}}_{PTO}/\vec{\hat{\dot{X}}}_{PTO}) \mathbf{T}_{kin}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-p}=\gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}^{\top} (\gls{sym-vec-hat-F-PTO}/\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-X-PTO}) \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}$
}\DIFaddend into \Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain}, but this yields a nonlinear expression of limited utility.
Instead, we can use cascade-like matrices to solve for a modified variable $\gls{sym-vec-dot-xi-Delta}$  defined as the change in the body velocities caused by the \gls{acr-pto} ,
in other words the difference between the actual body velocities $\gls{sym-vec-dot-xi}$
 and their values if the \gls{acr-pto} were disconnected with \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$F_{PTO}=0$, $\vec{\dot{\xi}}_{0}$:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-F-PTO}=0$, $\gls{sym-vec-dot-xi-0}$:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:modified-body-velocity}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\dot{\xi}}%%%
\DIFdel{_\Delta }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-dot-xi-Delta} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\dot{\xi}} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-dot-xi} \DIFaddend - \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\dot{\xi}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{0}
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-dot-xi-0}
    \DIFaddend \quad \textrm{ where } \quad
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\dot{\xi}}%%%
\DIFdel{_{0} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-dot-xi-0} \DIFaddend = \gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i} ^{-1}~\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\gamma}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-gamma}\DIFaddend ~\frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2}
 \end{equation}
The cascade-like matrix from the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO port $\{F_{PTO}, \dot{X}_{PTO}\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{port $\{\gls{sym-F-PTO}, \gls{sym-dot-X-PTO}\}$
}\DIFaddend to the modified body port $\{\gls{sym-F-p}, \gls{sym-dot-xi-Delta}\}$  is then:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:body-transmission-matrix}
    [\gls{sym-mathbf-a} ]\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{_{F_p\xi_\Delta\leftarrow F\dot{X}} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{_{\gls{sym-F-p}\gls{sym-xi-Delta}\leftarrow \gls{sym-F-PTO}\gls{sym-dot-X}} }\DIFaddend =
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD < \mathbf{I}_2 \\
%DIFDELCMD < -\mathbf{Z}_i^{-1}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-mathbf-I}^{2\times2} \\
        -\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-i}^{-1}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{T}_{kin}^T
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-T-kin}\DIFadd{^{\top}
    }\DIFaddend \begin{bmatrix}
        1 & 0
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
noting that this matrix is non-square ($\in \gls{sym-mathbb-C}^{4\times2}$ ) since there are more hydrodynamic than \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{PTO degrees of freedom}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-pto} \glspl{acr-dof}\DIFaddend ,
and that the matrix label drops the $PTO$ subscripts for compactness.
\Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-kinematics} represents this in circuit form,
noting that the excitation source must be duplicated to create $\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi-0}$ .

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{\gls{acr-pto} Dynamics}
\label{sec:appendix-pto-dynamics}
In addition to the \gls{acr-pto} port already discussed, we define two additional ports of relevance:
the generator mechanical port (torque $\gls{sym-tau}$  and rotation speed $\gls{sym-Omega}$ )
and the generator electrical port (voltage $\gls{sym-V}$ and current $\gls{sym-I}$ ).
Assuming a drivetrain consisting of a linear to rotational gear ratio with equivalent radius \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$R$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-R-gear}$ }\DIFaddend followed by a driveshaft with impedance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$Z_{\text{shaft}}$,
the PTO }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Z-shaft}$,
the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFaddend mechanical dynamics are expressed with cascade matrix
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$[\mathbf{a}]_{F\dot{X}\leftarrow \tau \Omega}$:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$[\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-dot-X-leftarrow-tau-Omega}]$:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:mech-transmission-matrix}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD < F_{PTO} \\ \dot{X}_{PTO}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
\gls{sym-F-PTO} \\ \gls{sym-dot-X-PTO}
\end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
=
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD < Z_{shaft}/R & 1/R \\
%DIFDELCMD < R & 0
%DIFDELCMD < \end{bmatrix}}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{_{[\mathbf{a}]_{F\dot{X}\leftarrow \tau \Omega}}
}%DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <  \tau\\ \Omega
%DIFDELCMD < \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}
\gls{sym-Z-shaft}/\gls{sym-R-gear} & 1/\gls{sym-R-gear} \\
\gls{sym-R-gear} & 0
\end{bmatrix}}
\DIFadd{_{[\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-dot-X-leftarrow-tau-Omega}]}
}\begin{bmatrix}
 \gls{sym-tau}\\ \gls{sym-Omega}
\end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}

Meanwhile, a synchronous permanent magnet generator modeled as an ideal gyrator with torque constant $\gls{sym-k-tau}$
 plus a winding impedance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$Z_{w}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Z-w}$ }\DIFaddend has cascade matrix \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$[\mathbf{a}]_{\tau \Omega \leftarrow V I}$:
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$[\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]$:
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:elec-transmission-matrix}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <  \tau\\ \Omega
%DIFDELCMD < \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-tau} \\ \gls{sym-Omega}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
    =
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD < 0 & k_\tau \\
%DIFDELCMD < 1/k_\tau & Z_{w}/k_\tau
%DIFDELCMD < \end{bmatrix}}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{_{[\mathbf{a}]_{\tau \Omega \leftarrow V I}}
}%DIFDELCMD < \begin{bmatrix}
%DIFDELCMD <  V \\ I
%DIFDELCMD < \end{bmatrix}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}
    0 & \gls{sym-k-tau} \\
    1/\gls{sym-k-tau} & \gls{sym-Z-w}/\gls{sym-k-tau}
    \end{bmatrix}}
    \DIFadd{_{[\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]}
    }\begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-V} \\ \gls{sym-I}
    \end{bmatrix}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
with Park-transformed quadrature electrical voltage and current $\gls{sym-V}$ and $\gls{sym-I}$ .

The system operating point is determined by a controller that applies some electrical load to the generator.
The controller can be formulated as an impedance at either the generator mechanical or electrical port.
The former is more convenient for hardware implementation, because modern drives typically expect torque commands,
while the latter is more convenient for electrical power calculations and optimal control.
%Here we formulate both as linear impedances with damping and stiffness terms, where
The mechanical control impedance $\gls{sym-Z-u}$  and electrical load impedance $\gls{sym-Z-l}$  are defined as follows:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:controller-impedances}
    \gls{sym-Z-l} = \frac{\gls{sym-V}}{\gls{sym-I}}  = \gls{sym-B-l} + \frac{\gls{sym-K-l}}{i\gls{sym-omega}}
     ,  \qquad
    \gls{sym-Z-u} = \frac{\gls{sym-tau}}{\gls{sym-Omega}} % = B_u + \frac{K_u}{s}
\end{equation}
Combining \Cref{eq:controller-impedances,eq:elec-transmission-matrix} gives the following expression for $\gls{sym-Z-u}$  as a function of $\gls{sym-Z-l}$:
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:control-impedance}
    \gls{sym-Z-u} =
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{
    \begin{bmatrix}
    1 & 0
    \end{bmatrix}
    [\mathbf{a}]_{\tau \Omega \leftarrow V I}
    \begin{bmatrix}
    Z_l \\ 1
    \end{bmatrix}
}{
    \begin{bmatrix}
    0 & 1
    \end{bmatrix}
    [\mathbf{a}]_{\tau \Omega \leftarrow V I}
    \begin{bmatrix}
    Z_l \\ 1
    \end{bmatrix}
}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{
    \begin{bmatrix}
    1 & 0
    \end{bmatrix}
    [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]
    \begin{bmatrix}
    \gls{sym-Z-l} \\ 1
    \end{bmatrix}
}{
    \begin{bmatrix}
    0 & 1
    \end{bmatrix}
    [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]
    \begin{bmatrix}
    \gls{sym-Z-l} \\ 1
    \end{bmatrix}
}
}\DIFaddend \end{equation}
This equation allows the $\gls{sym-Z-l}$  identified with optimal control to be implemented as a corresponding $\gls{sym-Z-u}$  in hardware.
%Incorporating \Cref{eq:mech-transmission-matrix} yields the Th\'{e}venin equivalent scalar PTO impedance at the hydrodynamic body port:
% \begin{equation}\label{eq:pto-impedance}
% Z_{PTO,th} =
% \frac{
%     \begin{bmatrix}
%     1 & 0
%     \end{bmatrix}
%     [\mathbf{a}]_{F\dot{X}\leftarrow \tau \Omega}
%     [\mathbf{a}]_{\tau \Omega \leftarrow V I}
%     \begin{bmatrix}
%     Z_l \\ 1
%     \end{bmatrix}
% }{
%     \begin{bmatrix}
%     0 & 1
%     \end{bmatrix}
%     [\mathbf{a}]_{F\dot{X}\leftarrow \tau \Omega} [\mathbf{a}]_{\tau \Omega \leftarrow V I}
%     \begin{bmatrix}
%     Z_l \\ 1
%     \end{bmatrix}
% }
% \end{equation}
% which is useful for calculating the mechanical response of each body.

The \gls{acr-pto} dynamics can be represented as a multiport circuit, as shown in \Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-pto}.
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/circuit_pto.pdf}
}%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelFL{PTO }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/circuit_pto.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/circuit_pto.pdf}
}\caption[\gls{acr-pto} dynamics represented as a multiport circuit]{\gls{acr-pto} \DIFaddendFL dynamics represented as a multiport circuit and electrical Th\'{e}venin equivalent}
\label{fig:multiport-circuit-pto}
\end{figure}

The intrinsic dynamics, \gls{acr-pto} kinematics, and \gls{acr-pto} dynamics can be combined into a Th\'{e}venin equivalent source voltage and impedance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{V}_{s,th}$ and $Z_{s,th}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}$ and $\gls{sym-Z-s-th}$ }\DIFaddend as follows:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thevenin-electrical}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Z_{s,th} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-Z-s-th} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{
    \begin{bmatrix}1 & 0\end{bmatrix}
    \mathbf{a}_{VI \leftarrow F\dot{X}}
    \begin{bmatrix}-Z_{i,th} \\ 1\end{bmatrix}}{\begin{bmatrix}0 & 1\end{bmatrix}
    \mathbf{a}_{VI \leftarrow F\dot{X}}
    \begin{bmatrix}-Z_{i,th} \\ 1\end{bmatrix}
    } }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{
        \begin{bmatrix}1 & 0\end{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-mathbf-a-VI-leftarrow-F-dot-X}
        \begin{bmatrix}-\gls{sym-Z-i-th} \\ 1\end{bmatrix}
    }{
        \begin{bmatrix}0 & 1\end{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-mathbf-a-VI-leftarrow-F-dot-X}
        \begin{bmatrix}-\gls{sym-Z-i-th} \\ 1\end{bmatrix}
    } }\DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{B-AZ_{i,th}}{D-CZ_{i,th}}
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{
                \gls{sym-B-a} - \gls{sym-A-a} \gls{sym-Z-i-th}
            }{
                \gls{sym-D-a}-\gls{sym-C-a} \gls{sym-Z-i-th}
            }
    }\DIFaddend \qquad
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{V}%%%
\DIFdel{_{s,th} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-V-s-th} \DIFaddend =
        \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{F}%%%
\DIFdel{_{th}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-F-th}
        \DIFaddend (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A-CZ_{s,th}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-A-a} \DIFadd{- }\gls{sym-C-a} \gls{sym-Z-s-th}\DIFaddend )
\end{equation}
where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$A,B,C,D$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-A-a},\gls{sym-B-a},\gls{sym-C-a},\gls{sym-D-a}$
}\DIFaddend are the elements of
$\gls{sym-mathbf-a-VI-leftarrow-F-dot-X}$ .
This is shown in the bottom portion of \Cref{fig:multiport-circuit-pto}.
The load voltage and current can then be found as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:voltage-current}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{V} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-V} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{V}%%%
\DIFdel{_{s,th}\frac{Z_l}{Z_{s,th}+Z_l}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}\DIFadd{\frac{
                                                    \gls{sym-Z-l}
                                                }{
                                                    \gls{sym-Z-s-th} + \gls{sym-Z-l}
                                                }}\DIFaddend ,
            \qquad \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{I} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-I} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{\hat{V}_{s,th}}{Z_{s,th}+Z_l}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{
                                                \gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}
                                            }{
                                                \gls{sym-Z-s-th}+\gls{sym-Z-l}
                                            }
}\DIFaddend \end{equation}

%DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFaddbegin 

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Time-Domain Expansion of Instantaneous Power}}
\label{sec:appendix-power-time}

\DIFadd{Equation~\eqref{eq:power-PQS} can be used to rewrite the instantaneous power $\gls{sym-p}(\gls{sym-t})$ at any port as \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{saadat_power_1999}
}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:power-time-PQS}
    \gls{sym-p}(\gls{sym-t}) =
    \gls{sym-P} + |\gls{sym-S}|
    \cos\!\left(
                2(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t} + \angle\hat{e})
                - \tan^{-1}\!\left(
                                    \frac{\gls{sym-Q}}{\gls{sym-P}}
                            \right)
        \right),
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{a }\gls{acr-dc} \DIFadd{offset of $\gls{sym-P}$ plus an oscillation at $2\gls{sym-omega}$ with amplitude $|\gls{sym-S}|$.
The average and peak powers in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:power-avg-peak} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
follow directly:
$\gls{sym-p-avg} = \gls{sym-P}$ since the oscillation averages to zero,
and $\gls{sym-p-pk} = \gls{sym-P} + |\gls{sym-S}|$ at the cosine maximum.
The minimum $\gls{sym-p-min} = \gls{sym-P} - |\gls{sym-S}|$ is negative when $|\gls{sym-S}|>\gls{sym-P}$,
corresponding to instants when energy flows from the load back into the source under reactive control.
}

%DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Electrical-Port Power in Optimal-Control Variables}}
\label{sec:appendix-power-elec}

\DIFadd{Expressing the average and peak powers at the electrical port in terms of the constrained-control parameters introduced in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control}
}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
(control damping $\gls{sym-B-l}$, control stiffness $\gls{sym-K-l}$, and current phasor $\gls{sym-hat-I}$),
and applying the constant electrical efficiency $\gls{sym-eta}$,
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:power-elec}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-p-avg-elec} \\
        \gls{sym-p-pk-elec}
    \end{bmatrix}
    =
    \gls{sym-eta}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \gls{sym-p-avg-VI} \\
        \gls{sym-p-pk-VI}
    \end{bmatrix}
    =
    \gls{sym-eta} \tfrac{1}{2} \gls{sym-B-l} |\gls{sym-hat-I}|^2
    \begin{bmatrix}
        1 \\
        1 + \sqrt{1 + \left(
                        \tfrac{\gls{sym-K-l}} {\gls{sym-B-l}\gls{sym-omega}}
                    \right)^2}
    \end{bmatrix}.
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The peak-power form is used as the apparent-power constraint in the }\gls{acr-qcqp} \DIFadd{of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:optimal-control}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

%DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%DIF >  Additionally, the drivetrain mechanical cascade matrix $[\gls{sym-a}]_{\gls{sym-m}}$ is set to the identity matrix, which represents a static effective gear radius $\gls{sym-R-gear}=1$:
%DIF >  \begin{equation}
%DIF >  [a]_m = \begin{bmatrix}
%DIF >  1/\gls{sym-R-gear} &~ 0 \\
%DIF >  0 &~ \gls{sym-R-gear}
%DIF >  \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix}
%DIF >  1 &~ 0 \\
%DIF >  0 &~ 1
%DIF >  \end{bmatrix}
%DIF >  \end{equation}
%DIF >  With this $[\gls{sym-a}]_{\gls{sym-m}}$, the control damping and stiffness $\gls{sym-B-u}$ and $\gls{sym-K-u}$ equal the PTO damping and stiffness $\gls{sym-B-p}$ and $\gls{sym-K-p}$.
%DIF >  This will simplify the impedance matching procedure described below, requiring matching at only one interface.

%DIF > The time-domain mechanical power is $\gls{sym-P}_{\text{mech}}(\gls{sym-t}) = \gls{sym-tau}(\gls{sym-t}) \gls{sym-Omega}(\gls{sym-t}) = (\gls{sym-B-u} \gls{sym-Omega}(\gls{sym-t}) + \gls{sym-K-u} \gls{sym-theta}(\gls{sym-t}) ) \gls{sym-Omega}(\gls{sym-t}) $, where $\gls{sym-theta}(\gls{sym-t})$ is the generator rotational position. Assuming sinusoidal waveforms, the time-average and peak mechanical power in a given sea state are then
%DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\DIFaddend %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{Linear Solution Procedure}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Linear Solution Procedure}}
\DIFaddend \label{sec:appendix-dynamics-solution}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:eom,sec:pto-dynamics-overview} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:eom,sec:pto-model-overview} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend introduce linear models for the rigid body dynamics, \gls{acr-pto} kinematics, and \gls{acr-pto} dynamics
using impedance, hybrid, and cascade matrices respectively.
Here we unify the formulation and present a clear system-level solution procedure for all port variables:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Use \Cref{eq:thevenin-electrical} to assemble the Th\'{e}venin source voltage and impedance
        \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{V}_{s,th}$ and $Z_{s,th}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}$ and $\gls{sym-Z-s-th}$
        }\DIFaddend as seen from the port on which power should be maximized
        (in this case, the electrical generator port
        $\{\gls{sym-V},\gls{sym-I}\}$ ).
    \item Set the load impedance $\gls{sym-Z-l}$ .
        Any load impedance can be simulated,
        but typically it is selected as a function of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$Z_s$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Z-s-th}$ }\DIFaddend to maximize the power at this port,
        possibly subject to constraints on various ports.
        \Cref{sec:optimal-control} frames the load impedance selection as a constrained optimization problem.
    \item From  \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{V}_{s,th}$, $Z_{s,th}$, and $Z_l$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}$, $\gls{sym-Z-s-th}$, and $\gls{sym-Z-l}$}\DIFaddend ,
        calculate the $\{\gls{sym-V},\gls{sym-I}\}$  port variables from \Cref{eq:voltage-current}.
    \item Use cascade matrices \Cref{eq:mech-transmission-matrix,eq:elec-transmission-matrix,eq:body-transmission-matrix}
        to propagate the variables back toward the rigid body,
        yielding the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\{\tau,\Omega\}$, $\{F_{PTO},\dot{X}_{PTO}\}$,
        and $\{F_p, \dot{\xi}_\Delta\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\{\gls{sym-tau},\gls{sym-Omega}\}$, $\{\gls{sym-F-PTO},\gls{sym-dot-X-PTO}\}$,
        and $\{\gls{sym-F-p}, \gls{sym-dot-xi-Delta}\}$ }\DIFaddend port variables.
    \item Transform from $\gls{sym-dot-xi-Delta}$ to $\gls{sym-dot-xi}$  via \Cref{eq:modified-body-velocity}.
\end{enumerate}
Once all the port variables have been calculated,
power at each port can be calculated according to \Cref{sec:power}.
This serves as the solution to a linear subproblem,
which is then re-solved using updated quasi-linear coefficients until drag and force saturation nonlinearities have converged.


\DIFaddbegin \FloatBarrier
\subsection{\DIFadd{Optimal Control Details}}
\label{sec:appendix-optimal-control}
\DIFaddend %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{Indirect Optimal Control Solution Procedure}}
    %DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Indirect Optimal Control Solution Procedure}}
    \DIFaddend \label{sec:appendix-qp-numerical}
    The indirect optimal control approach applies optimality conditions and then numerically solves the resulting equations.
    The optimality conditions depend on the active set of constraints.
    In the most straightforward case where simply one constraint exists, the constraint is active if and only if the unconstrained solution violates that constraint.
    For example, if only a maximum force constraint exists, the constrained solution has a force \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{F}_{p,\text{constr}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-F-p-constr}$ }\DIFaddend that is the minimum of the unconstrained force \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{F}_{p,\text{unconstrained}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-F-p-unconstrained}$ }\DIFaddend and the maximum allowed force fundamental \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$F_{\text{max}}$:
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-F-max}$:
    }\DIFaddend \begin{equation}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{F}%%%
\DIFdel{_{p,constr} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-F-p-constr} \DIFaddend = \min\left(\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{F}%%%
\DIFdel{_{p,unconstrained}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-F-p-unconstrained}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{F_{max}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-F-max}\DIFaddend \right)
    \end{equation}
    The solver guesses a value of $\gls{sym-Gamma}$ , calculates the resulting force \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{F}_{p,\text{guess}}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-F-p-guess}$}\DIFaddend , and then uses \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{F}_{p,\text{guess}}-\hat{F}_{p,\text{constr}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-F-p-guess}-\gls{sym-hat-F-p-constr}$ }\DIFaddend as an error signal to adjust its next guess of $\gls{sym-Gamma}$ , iterating until the error converges to zero.
    This solver-based optimal control performs numerical root-finding, not numerical optimization, and can happen in an inner loop of the drag-linearization solver iteration (where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{F}_{p,\text{unconstrained}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-F-p-unconstrained}$ }\DIFaddend stays constant each control iteration) or simultaneously with the drag-linearization iteration (where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat{F}_{p,\text{unconstrained}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-hat-F-p-unconstrained}$ }\DIFaddend and the system linearization are updated each control iteration).
    MDOcean implements the latter, which simplifies the setup by requiring only one solver.
    Comparing the efficiency of the two approaches is left for future work.

    When multiple constraints exist, the situation is more complex, since it is possible for a given constraint to be violated by the unconstrained solution but not active in the constrained solution, or for multiple constraints to be active simultaneously.
    In the special case where the unconstrained solution violates no more than one constraint, that constraint is active and the solver can proceed as described above.
    When the unconstrained solution violates multiple constraints, the solver must try all subsets of 1-2 of those constraint(s) as the active set, find which of these active sets produces the largest power while satisfying all constraints, and then set the error as the deviation of the guessed solution from the constrained solution for that active set.
    Since the active set cannot be calculated a-priori as in the simpler case, this approach ends up looking more like a numerical optimization than a numerical root-finding, although the optimization is over a finite set of active set possibilities instead of a continuous control domain as it would be in a direct optimal control method.
    The main advantage of the indirect approach is its ability to handle arbitrary nonlinear\footnote{Nonlinear meaning greater than second order in an optimization sense, not in a dynamic sense, because the dynamics are already quasi-linearized here} constraints, although for the quadratic constraints considered here, the analytical approach described in \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-solution} is more efficient.
\fi
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{Analytical Quadratic Program for Constrained Power Maximization}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsubsection{\DIFadd{Analytical }\gls{acr-qp} \DIFadd{for Constrained Power Maximization}}
\DIFaddend \label{sec:appendix-qp-solution}
This section describes the analytical solution process for the constrained optimal control problem \Cref{eq:opt-problem} introduced in \Cref{sec:optimal-control}.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Quadratic Constraint Coefficients}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Quadratic Constraint Coefficients}}
\DIFaddend \label{sec:appendix-qp-constraints-table}

The quadratic coefficients \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\{\mathbf{Q}_i, \vec{a}_i, b_i\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\{\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}, \gls{sym-vec-a-mu}, \gls{sym-b-mu}\}$ }\DIFaddend in \Cref{eq:opt-problem} for each constraint implemented in MDOcean are given in \Cref{tab:qp-constraints}.

\begin{table}[b!]
\centering
\caption{Quadratic constraint coefficients}
\label{tab:qp-constraints}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
    Constraint & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\mathbf{Q}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}$ }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\vec{a}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-vec-a-mu}$ }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$b$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-b-mu}$ }\DIFaddendFL \\ \hline
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$|\hat{\xi}_f| \leq \hat{\xi}_{f,\text{max}}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$|\gls{sym-hat-xi-f}| \leq \gls{sym-xi-f-max}$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\frac{1}{\omega^2}
        [\mathbf{a}_{(F_{p}\dot{\xi})_f\leftarrow VI}]^* 
        \mathbf{A}_{|\hat{q}|^2}
        [\mathbf{a}_{(F_{p}\dot{\xi})_f\leftarrow VI}]$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\frac{1}{\gls{sym-omega}^2}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-p-dot-xi-f-leftarrow-VI}]^*
        \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-hat-q-2}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-p-dot-xi-f-leftarrow-VI}]$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$[0,~ \hat{\xi}_{f,0}]^T$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$[0,~ \gls{sym-hat-xi-f-0}]^\top$ }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\hat{\xi}_{f,\text{max}} - |\hat{\xi}_{f,0}|$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-xi-f-max} - |\gls{sym-hat-xi-f-0}|$ }\DIFaddendFL \\
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$|\hat{\xi}_s| \leq \hat{\xi}_{s,\text{max}}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$|\gls{sym-hat-xi-s}| \leq \gls{sym-xi-s-max}$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\frac{1}{\omega^2}
        [\mathbf{a}_{(F_{p}\dot{\xi})_s\leftarrow VI}]^* 
        \mathbf{A}_{|\hat{q}|^2}
        [\mathbf{a}_{(F_{p}\dot{\xi})_s\leftarrow VI}]$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\frac{1}{\gls{sym-omega}^2}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-p-dot-xi-s-leftarrow-VI}]^*
        \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-hat-q-2}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-p-dot-xi-s-leftarrow-VI}]$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$[0,~ \hat{\xi}_{s,0}]^T$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$[0,~ \gls{sym-hat-xi-s-0}]^\top$ }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\hat{\xi}_{s,\text{max}} - |\hat{\xi}_{s,0}|$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-xi-s-max} - |\gls{sym-hat-xi-s-0}|$ }\DIFaddendFL \\
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$|\hat{X}_{PTO}| \leq \hat{X}_{PTO,\text{max}}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$|\gls{sym-hat-X-PTO}| \leq \gls{sym-X-PTO-max}$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\frac{1}{\omega^2}
        [\mathbf{a}_{F\dot{X}\leftarrow VI}]^* 
        \mathbf{A}_{|\hat{q}|^2}
        [\mathbf{a}_{F\dot{X}\leftarrow VI}]$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\frac{1}{\gls{sym-omega}^2}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-dot-X-leftarrow-VI}]^*
        \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-hat-q-2}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-F-dot-X-leftarrow-VI}]$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        $\gls{sym-vec-zero}$  & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\hat{X}_{PTO,\text{max}}^2$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-X-PTO-max}^2$ }\DIFaddendFL \\
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$|\hat{\tau}| \leq \hat{\tau}_{\text{max}}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$|\gls{sym-hat-tau}| \leq \gls{sym-tau-max}$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$[\mathbf{a}_{\tau\Omega \leftarrow VI}]^* 
        \mathbf{A}_{|\hat{e}|^2}
        [\mathbf{a}_{\tau\Omega\leftarrow VI}]$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$[\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]^*
        \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-hat-e-2}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        $\gls{sym-vec-zero}$  & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$\hat{\tau}_{\text{max}}^2$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-tau-max}^2$ }\DIFaddendFL \\
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$P_{\tau\Omega} \leq P_{\tau\Omega,\text{max}}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-P-tau-Omega} \leq \gls{sym-P-tau-Omega-max}$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$[\mathbf{a}_{\tau\Omega \leftarrow VI}]^* 
        \mathbf{A}_{P}
        [\mathbf{a}_{\tau\Omega\leftarrow VI}]$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$[\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]^*
        \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-P}
        [\gls{sym-mathbf-a-tau-Omega-leftarrow-VI}]$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        $\gls{sym-vec-zero}$  & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$P_{\tau\Omega,\text{max}}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-P-tau-Omega-max}$ }\DIFaddendFL \\
    $\gls{sym-P-VI} \geq 0$  &
        \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$-\mathbf{A}_{P}$ }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$-\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-P}$ }\DIFaddendFL &
        $\gls{sym-vec-zero}$  & $0$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Reduced Dimension Formulation}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Reduced Dimension Formulation}}
\DIFaddend The linear equality constraint $\gls{sym-vec-c}^{\,*}\gls{sym-vec-x} = d$  reduces the optimization problem dimension from $\gls{sym-vec-x} \in \gls{sym-mathbb-C}^2$ to $\gls{sym-mathbb-C}^1$ .
Specifically, the equality constraint requires that the solution be a particular solution plus a scalar multiple of $\gls{sym-vec-n}$ , the null space vector of $\gls{sym-vec-c}^{\,*}$:
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:x-p-plus-nullspace}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{x} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-x} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \underbrace{
%DIFDELCMD <         \frac{\hat{V}_{s,th}}{2\Re( Z_{s,th})}
%DIFDELCMD <         \begin{bmatrix} \ Z_{s,th}^* \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} 
%DIFDELCMD <     }%%%
\DIFdel{_{
        \textrm{particular solution } \vec{x}_p} 
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \underbrace{
        \frac{\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}}{2\Re(\gls{sym-Z-s-th})}
        \begin{bmatrix} \ Z_{s,th}^* \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}
    }\DIFadd{_{
        \textrm{particular solution } \gls{sym-vec-x-p}}
    }\DIFaddend -~ \gls{sym-Gamma} \cdot \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{\hat{V}_{s,th}}{2\Re( Z_{s,th})}
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}}{2\Re(\gls{sym-Z-s-th})}
    }\DIFaddend \underbrace{
        \begin{bmatrix} -\ Z_{s,th}^* \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}
    }_{
        \textrm{null space } \gls{sym-vec-n}}
 \end{equation}
where $\gls{sym-Gamma}$  is a nondimensional complex scalar representing the remaining \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degree of freedom }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend in the optimization after enforcing the equality constraint.
For the particular solution, we have chosen to use the optimal solution in the absence of inequality constraints, which is identical to the solution implied by the impedance-matching condition \Cref{eq:matched-load} and can be derived from the quadratic program as follows:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:x-p}
    \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{x}%%%
\DIFdel{_{p} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-x-p} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{d \mathbf{A}_P\vec{c}}{\vec{c}^{\,*}\mathbf{A}_P\vec{c}}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{d \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-P}\gls{sym-vec-c}}{\gls{sym-vec-c}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-P}\gls{sym-vec-c}}
}\DIFaddend \end{equation}
This selection for $\gls{sym-vec-x-p}$  means that the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{degree of freedom $\Gamma$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-dof} \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma}$ }\DIFaddend represents the deviation of the solution from the impedance-matched solution.
Using $\gls{sym-hat-I-p}$  for brevity to denote the current in the particular solution, \Cref{eq:x-p-plus-nullspace} can be rewritten as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:x-p-plus-nullspace-simplified}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{x} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-x} \DIFaddend = \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{x}%%%
\DIFdel{_p }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-x-p} \DIFaddend - \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\Gamma }%DIFDELCMD < \hat{I}%%%
\DIFdel{_p }%DIFDELCMD < \vec{n}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-Gamma} \gls{sym-hat-I-p} \gls{sym-vec-n}
    \DIFaddend %&= \begin{bmatrix}
    %    1+\Gamma &  0 \\
    %    0 & 1-\Gamma
    %\end{bmatrix}
    %\vec{x}_p
\end{equation}

Inequality constraints
$\gls{sym-vec-x}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-x}
+ 2\Re\!\left\{\gls{sym-vec-a-mu}^{\,*}\gls{sym-vec-x}\right\}
\le \gls{sym-b-mu}$
 further reduce the feasible design space, and the presence of multiple inequality constraints introduces the possibility of \Cref{eq:opt-problem} having no solutions due to infeasibility.
While constrained quadratic programs like \Cref{eq:opt-problem} generally require numerical solutions, the low dimensionality allows an analytical solution with geometric intuition, even if the problem is non-convex (see \Cref{sec:appendix-qp-convexity} for a discussion on convexity).
First, we rewrite the objective and inequality constraints in terms of $\gls{sym-Gamma}$  by plugging \Cref{eq:x-p-plus-nullspace-simplified} into \Cref{eq:opt-problem}:
\begin{subequations}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \label{eq:obj-inequality-gamma}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \begin{align}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{P_{avg,VI}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-p-avg-VI}
\DIFaddend &= \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{|\hat{V}_{s,th}|^2}{8\Re(Z_{s,th})}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{|\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}|^2}{8\Re (\gls{sym-Z-s-th})}}\DIFaddend ~ (1 - |\gls{sym-Gamma} |^2)
\label{eq:obj-gamma}
\\[6pt]
\gls{sym-A-q-mu} |\gls{sym-Gamma} |^2 +&
2\Re\!\left\{
    \gls{sym-B-q-mu}\gls{sym-Gamma} \right\}+\gls{sym-C-q-mu} \le
0
\quad \forall \gls{sym-mu} \label{eq:ineq-gamma}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
where quadratic coefficients $\gls{sym-A-q-mu}$, $\gls{sym-B-q-mu}$, and $\gls{sym-C-q-mu}$  from \Cref{eq:ineq-gamma} are defined as:
\begin{equation}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     A_{q,\mu} &= |\hat{I}_p|^{2},
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{n}^{\,*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mu}\vec{n}& &\in \mathbb{R} \\
%DIFDELCMD <     B_{q,\mu} &= -\hat{I}_p,
%DIFDELCMD <     \left(\vec{x}_p^{\,*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mu} + \vec{a}_{\mu}^{\,*}\right)\vec{n}& &\in \mathbb{C} \\
%DIFDELCMD <     C_{q,\mu} &= 
%DIFDELCMD <         \vec{x}_p^{\,*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mu}\vec{x}_p
%DIFDELCMD <         +
%DIFDELCMD <         2\Re\!\left\{\vec{a}_{\mu}^{\,*}\vec{x}_p\right\}
%DIFDELCMD <         -b_{\mu}&
%DIFDELCMD <     &\in \mathbb{R}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-A-q-mu} &= |\gls{sym-hat-I-p}|^{2},
    \gls{sym-vec-n}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-n}& &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-R} \\
    \gls{sym-B-q-mu} &= -\gls{sym-hat-I-p},
    \left(\gls{sym-vec-x-p}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu} + \gls{sym-vec-a-mu}^{\,*}\right)\gls{sym-vec-n}& &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-C} \\
    \gls{sym-C-q-mu} &=
        \gls{sym-vec-x-p}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-x-p}
        +
        2\Re\!\left\{\gls{sym-vec-a-mu}^{\,*}\gls{sym-vec-x-p}\right\}
        -\gls{sym-b-mu}&
    &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-R}
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
Since \Cref{eq:obj-gamma} shows that power decreases with increasing $|\gls{sym-Gamma}|$ , the power maximization problem \Cref{eq:opt-problem} can be recast as minimizing the norm of $\gls{sym-Gamma}$ :
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:opt-problem-gamma}
\begin{alignat}{2}
& \min_{\gls{sym-Gamma} \in \gls{sym-mathbb-C}}  &  |\gls{sym-Gamma} |^2& \\[3pt]
& \text{s.t.} &
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{S_{\mu}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-S-mu}\DIFaddend |\gls{sym-Gamma} - \gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu} |^2 &\le S_{\mu} r_{\mu}^2,
\quad \forall \mu \label{eq:disc-constraint}\\
& \text{where} &
\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu} &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-C} \nonumber\\
& & r_{\mu}^2 &\in \gls{sym-mathbb-R} \nonumber \\
& & \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{S_{\mu} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-S-mu} \DIFaddend &\in \left\{-1,1\right\} \nonumber
\end{alignat}
\end{subequations}
where the constraint \Cref{eq:disc-constraint} is derived from \Cref{eq:ineq-gamma} by completing the square.
More precisely, when $\gls{sym-A-q-mu} \neq 0$, the $\gls{sym-mu}$ th inequality constraint defines a circle in the complex plane of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Gamma$ centered at $\Gamma_{c,\mu}$ with radius $r_{\mu}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma}$ centered at $\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}$ with radius $\gls{sym-r-mu}$}\DIFaddend .
The constraint requires that $\gls{sym-Gamma}$  lie within this circle when $\gls{sym-A-q-mu} > 0$ ($\gls{sym-S}_{\gls{sym-mu}}=1$ ) or outside this circle when $\gls{sym-A-q-mu} < 0$ ($\gls{sym-S}_{\gls{sym-mu}}=-1$ ).
Parameters \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Gamma_{c,\mu}$, $r_{\mu}$, and $S_{\mu}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}$, $\gls{sym-r-mu}$, and $\gls{sym-S-mu}$ }\DIFaddend are defined as:
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:gamma-disc-params}
\begin{align}
\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu} &= \frac{-\gls{sym-B-q-mu}^{\,*}}{\gls{sym-A-q-mu}}  =
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{
    \hat{I}_p^{\,*}\,
    \vec{n}^{\,*}
    \left(\mathbf{Q}_{\mu}\vec{x}_p + \vec{a}_{\mu}\right)
}{
    |\hat{I}_p|^{2}\,
    \vec{n}^{\,*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mu}\vec{n}
}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{
    \gls{sym-hat-I-p}^{\,*}\,
    \gls{sym-vec-n}^{\,*}
    \left(\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-x-p} + \gls{sym-vec-a-mu}\right)
}{
    |\gls{sym-hat-I-p}|^{2}\,
    \gls{sym-vec-n}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-n}
}
}\DIFaddend \label{eq:gamma-disc-center}
\\[6pt]
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{r_{\mu}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-r-mu}\DIFaddend ^2
&=
\frac{|\gls{sym-B-q-mu}|^2-\gls{sym-A-q-mu}\gls{sym-C-q-mu}}{\gls{sym-A-q-mu}^2}  =
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{
b_{\mu}
-
\vec{x}_p^{\,*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mu}\vec{x}_p
-
2\Re\!\left\{\vec{a}_{\mu}^{\,*}\vec{x}_p\right\}
}{
|\hat{I}_p|^{2}\,
\vec{n}^{\,*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mu}\vec{n}
}
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{
\gls{sym-b-mu}
-
\gls{sym-vec-x-p}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-x-p}
-
2\Re\!\left\{\gls{sym-vec-a-mu}^{\,*}\gls{sym-vec-x-p}\right\}
}{
|\gls{sym-hat-I-p}|^{2}\,
\gls{sym-vec-n}^{\,*}\gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-n}
}
}\DIFaddend +
|\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu} |^2
\label{eq:gamma-disc-radius}
\\
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{S_{\mu} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-S-mu} \DIFaddend &= \textrm{sign} (\gls{sym-A-q-mu} ) = \textrm{sign}\left(\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{n}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-n}\DIFaddend ^{\,*}\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mathbf{Q}_{\mu}}%DIFDELCMD < \vec{n}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-mathbf-Q-mu}\gls{sym-vec-n}\DIFaddend \right)
\label{eq:gamma-disc-sign}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Solution}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Solution}}
\DIFaddend \Cref{eq:opt-problem-gamma} can be interpreted graphically as finding the point closest to the origin that is interior to all constraint circles for which \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$S_{\mu}=1$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-S-mu}=1$ }\DIFaddend and exterior to all circles for which \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$S_{\mu}=-1$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-S-mu}=-1$}\DIFaddend .
The unconstrained optimal solution of \Cref{eq:opt-problem-gamma} is the origin ($\gls{sym-Gamma}=0$ ), and the constrained optimal solution will also be zero in the case where the origin is feasible (no active constraints), which occurs when \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$ S_{\mu} C_{q,\mu} \leq 0 \quad \forall \mu$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$ \gls{sym-S-mu} \gls{sym-C-q-mu} \leq 0 \quad \forall \gls{sym-mu}$}\DIFaddend .
If the origin is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{infeasible}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \textrm{\DIFadd{infeasible}}\DIFaddend , the constrained optimal solution will lie on the boundary of one or more of the circles, specifically either at an intersection of two or more circles or at any circle's point closest to the origin.
We analytically compute all such candidate points and select the one with the lowest $|\gls{sym-Gamma}|$  that satisfies all constraints.
For $\gls{sym-N}$  constraints, there are up to $\gls{sym-N}^2$  candidate points ($\gls{sym-N}^2-\gls{sym-N}$  at intersections and $\gls{sym-N}$  at closest points).
This leads to the following expression for the constrained optimal solution (valid when $\gls{sym-A-q-mu} \neq 0 ~\forall \gls{sym-mu}$):
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma-opt-quadratic}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\Gamma_{opt} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-Gamma-opt} \DIFaddend =
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{cases}
%DIFDELCMD <  0 & \textrm{if } S_{\mu} C_{q,\mu} \leq 0 \quad \forall \mu \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD <  \arg\min\limits_{\Gamma \in \mathcal{F} \bigcap \mathcal{C}} |\Gamma| 
%DIFDELCMD <  & \textrm{otherwise} \\[3pt]
%DIFDELCMD <  \textrm{infeasible} & \textrm{if } \mathcal{F} \bigcap \mathcal{C} = \emptyset
%DIFDELCMD < \end{cases}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{cases}
 0 & \textrm{if } \gls{sym-S-mu} \gls{sym-C-q-mu} \leq 0 \quad \forall \gls{sym-mu} \\[3pt]
 \arg\min\limits_{\gls{sym-Gamma} \in \gls{sym-mathcal-F} \bigcap \gls{sym-mathcal-C}} |\gls{sym-Gamma}|
 & \textrm{otherwise} \\[3pt]
 \textrm{infeasible} & \textrm{if } \gls{sym-mathcal-F} \bigcap \gls{sym-mathcal-C} = \emptyset
\end{cases}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathcal{F} = \bigcap_{\mu} \left\{\Gamma : S_{\mu}|\Gamma - \Gamma_{c,\mu}| \le S_{\mu} r_{\mu} \right\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathcal-F} = \bigcap_{\gls{sym-mu}} \left\{\gls{sym-Gamma}: \gls{sym-S}_{\gls{sym-mu}}|\gls{sym-Gamma} - \gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}| \le \gls{sym-S}_{\gls{sym-mu}} \gls{sym-r-mu} \right\}$ }\DIFaddend is the set of all feasible points and $\gls{sym-mathcal-C}$  is the set of candidate points. $\gls{sym-mathcal-C}$  is the union of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathcal{C}_{int}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathcal-C-int}$}\DIFaddend , the candidates at circle intersections, and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathcal{C}_{cl}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathcal-C-cl}$}\DIFaddend , the candidates at the closest point on each circle to the origin:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:candidate-points}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD < \mathcal{C}_{int} &= \left\{
%DIFDELCMD <     \Gamma_{\mu,\nu}, \quad \forall \mu \neq \nu
%DIFDELCMD < \right\}.
%DIFDELCMD < \\
%DIFDELCMD < \mathcal{C}_{cl} &= \left\{
%DIFDELCMD <     (|\Gamma_{c,\mu}| - r_{\mu})e^{i\angle\,\Gamma_{c,\mu}}, \quad \forall \mu
%DIFDELCMD < \right\}.
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
\gls{sym-mathcal-C-int} &= \left\{
    \gls{sym-Gamma-mu-nu}, \quad \forall \gls{sym-mu} \neq \gls{sym-nu}
\right\}.
\\
\gls{sym-mathcal-C-cl} &= \left\{
    (|\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}| - \gls{sym-r-mu})\exp (i\angle\,\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}), \quad \forall \gls{sym-mu}
\right\}.
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
The intersection points of the $\gls{sym-mu}$th and $\gls{sym-nu}$ th circles are given by:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:circle-intersection}
    \gls{sym-Gamma-mu-nu} =
    \gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu} + \frac{\gls{sym-Gamma-c-nu} - \gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}}{\gls{sym-d-mu-nu}}
     \left(        \gls{sym-a-mu-nu} \pm
        1i \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\sqrt{r_{\mu}^2-a_{\mu\nu}^2}
    }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\sqrt{\gls{sym-r-mu}^2-\gls{sym-a-mu-nu}^2}
    }\DIFaddend \right)
\end{equation}
where $\gls{sym-d-mu-nu} = |\gls{sym-Gamma-c-nu} - \gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}|$  is the distance between the circle centers,
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$a_{\mu\nu} = \frac{1}{2}(r_{\mu}^2-r_{\nu}^2+d_{\mu\nu}^2)/d_{\mu\nu}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-a-mu-nu} = \frac{1}{2}(\gls{sym-r-mu}^2-\gls{sym-r-nu}^2+\gls{sym-d-mu-nu}^2)/\gls{sym-d-mu-nu}$ }\DIFaddend is the distance from
the center of the $\gls{sym-mu}$ th circle to the midpoint of the two intersection points,
and $1i$ is the imaginary unit.
The closest-point candidates \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathcal{C}_{cl}$ match }%DIFDELCMD < \equationautorefname%%%
\DIFdel{~4 of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{mccabe_force-limited_2024}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
,
which provides the solution }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathcal-C-cl}$ match the solution that \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet[\equationautorefname~4]{mccabe_force-limited_2024} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
derive }\DIFaddend for problems with only one constraint.

Importantly, we need not compute the entire feasible space $\gls{sym-mathcal-F}$ , only compute $\gls{sym-mathcal-C}$  and then check whether each candidate point is feasible.
\Cref{fig:qp-solution-geometry} illustrates the various cases for feasibility and optimality for a toy problem with \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$r_{\mu}=4$, $S_{\mu}=1$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-r-mu}=4$, $\gls{sym-S-mu}=1$}\DIFaddend , and various $\gls{sym-Gamma-c-mu}$ .
\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
