Here, the integrals as defined in \Cref{eq:definition-IB-IG} diverge, and the form of \Cref{eq:drag-integrals-IB-IG} does not apply.
Instead, when $\gls{sym-r}\rightarrow \infty$ , we apply an alternative convention that replaces the \gls{acr-wec} speed in \Cref{eq:drag-integrals-IB-IG} with the incident wave speed and uses a primed form of the integrals, $\gls{sym-I-prime} = \gls{sym-I-drag}/\gls{sym-r}$:
 \begin{equation}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \mathbf{B}_{d} &= \frac{8}{3\pi} \rho_w R^2 C_d |\hat{V}_{wave}|
%DIFDELCMD <     \left[ 
%DIFDELCMD <         I_B'(r,\angle\hat{\dot{\xi}},kR) - \alpha^2 I_B'(r,\angle\hat{\dot{\xi}},\alpha k R)
%DIFDELCMD <     \right]
%DIFDELCMD <     \\
%DIFDELCMD <     \vec{\gamma}_{d} &= \frac{8}{3\pi} \rho_w R^2 C_d  |\hat{V}_{wave}| \frac{gk}{\omega} e^{-k\vec{T}}
%DIFDELCMD <     \left[ 
%DIFDELCMD <         I_G'(r,\angle\hat{\dot{\xi}},kR) - \alpha^2 I_G'(r,\angle\hat{\dot{\xi}},\alpha kR)
%DIFDELCMD <     \right]
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-mathbf-B-d} &= \frac{8}{3\gls{sym-pi}} \gls{sym-rho-w} \gls{sym-vec-R}^2 \gls{sym-C-d} |\gls{sym-hat-V-wave}|
    \left[
        \gls{sym-I-B}'(\gls{sym-r},\angle\gls{sym-hat-dot-xi},\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-vec-R}) 
        - \gls{sym-alpha}^2 \gls{sym-I-B}'(\gls{sym-r},\angle\gls{sym-hat-dot-xi},\gls{sym-alpha} \gls{sym-k-wavenumber} \gls{sym-vec-R})
    \right]
    \\
    \gls{sym-vec-gamma-d} &= \frac{8}{3\gls{sym-pi}} \gls{sym-rho-w} \gls{sym-vec-R}^2 \gls{sym-C-d} |\gls{sym-hat-V-wave}|
    \frac{\gls{sym-g}\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}}{\gls{sym-omega}} \exp(-\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-vec-T})
    \left[
        \gls{sym-I-G}'(\gls{sym-r},\angle\gls{sym-hat-dot-xi},\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-vec-R}) 
        - \gls{sym-alpha}^2 \gls{sym-I-G}'(\gls{sym-r},\angle\gls{sym-hat-dot-xi},\gls{sym-alpha} \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-vec-R})
    \right]
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
The relevant limits are then:
\begin{equation}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \lim_{r\rightarrow \infty} I_B'(r,\theta,\kappa) = 0,
%DIFDELCMD <     \qquad
%DIFDELCMD <     \lim_{r\rightarrow \infty} I_G'(r,\theta,\kappa) = 
%DIFDELCMD <     \begin{cases}
%DIFDELCMD <     \frac{\pi J_1((1i-1)\kappa)}{(1i-1)\kappa} & \textrm{if } \kappa \neq 0 \\
%DIFDELCMD <     \frac{\pi}{2} & \textrm{if } \kappa = 0
%DIFDELCMD <     \end{cases}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \lim_{\gls{sym-r}\rightarrow \infty} \gls{sym-I-B}'(\gls{sym-r},\gls{sym-theta},\gls{sym-kappa}) = 0,
    \qquad
    \lim_{\gls{sym-r}\rightarrow \infty} \gls{sym-I-G}'(\gls{sym-r},\gls{sym-theta},\gls{sym-kappa}) =
    \begin{cases}
    \displaystyle  \frac{
                        \gls{sym-pi} \gls{sym-mathrm-J-1}((1i-1)\gls{sym-kappa})
                        }{
                            (1i-1)\gls{sym-kappa}
                        }
                    & \textrm{if } \gls{sym-kappa} \neq 0 \\
    \displaystyle\frac{\gls{sym-pi}}{2}
                    & \textrm{if } \gls{sym-kappa} = 0
    \end{cases}
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Comparison to Other Drag Formulations}
\label{subsec:appendix-drag-others}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The WEC drag model in the study \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
propose a }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{drag model that }\DIFaddend also utilizes describing functions with the relative velocity but groups the drag force into damping and stiffness terms rather than damping and excitation terms.
While this representation yields identical results for a floating \gls{acr-wec} in the frequency domain, it misleadingly implies that a body fixed at the still water line would experience no drag force from incident waves.
In other words, it fails to account for the edge case of $\gls{sym-r} \rightarrow \infty$  described above, where the drag force is purely an excitation force and cannot be expressed as a damping or stiffness.
Furthermore, the drag ``damping'' term in the grouping of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend includes not just forces with a dissipative effect on the body but also the portion of nonlinear drag excitation forces that are in phase with the body velocity.
This creates the confusing possibility for negative apparent damping coefficients, leading to false concerns about violating energy conservation if erroneously interpreted as a true damping coefficient.
The damping-excitation grouping chosen in \Cref{sec:drag} avoids this confusion and guarantees positive values of the damping coefficient $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}$ .

Another improvement over \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend is that the present model considers the fact that the incident wave velocity, and thus the relative velocity, differs across the width of the \gls{acr-wec} in the direction of wave propagation \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$y$.
Reference \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-y}$.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend more simplistically assumes that the wave velocity at all points equals that at the center of the \gls{acr-wec} , which is only accurate when the incident wavelength far exceeds the \gls{acr-wec} width.
This means that \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Ref.~\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend captures only the Froude-Krylov-like component of the drag excitation, neglecting the diffraction-like component.
Early MDOcean simulations that used the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{formulation of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{quartier_influence_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
formulation }\DIFaddend found this approximation to be unacceptable, producing unstable dynamics (negative drag damping coefficients whose absolute value exceeds the radiation damping) at high frequencies and large wave heights in the operational regime.
The strip-theory approach presented in \Cref{sec:drag} avoids this issue.
Comparisons against WEC-Sim confirm that even without strip theory, assuming that the relative velocity is perfectly in phase with the body velocity (i.e. a drag force of purely damping and in-phase excitation, with no stiffness or out-of-phase excitation) yields more accurate results than the drag formulation used in the study \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{quartier_influence_2021}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{quartier_influence_2021}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Iterative Solution}\DIFaddbegin \label{sec:appendix-drag-iterative}
\DIFaddend With the describing function approximation, the nonlinear time-domain drag equation is now a quasi-linear frequency-domain equation, since the state-dependence of the coefficients \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\mathbf{B}_{d}$ and $\vec{\gamma}_{d}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}$ and $\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$ }\DIFaddend prevents true linearity.
The solution for states $|\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}|$ and $\angle \gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}$  for a given controller can be obtained either through numerical iteration or analytical solution of the nonlinear equation \eqref{eq:eom}.
Iteration is chosen here, the same approach used for a frequency-domain drag simulation of floating offshore wind turbines \citep{hall_open-source_2022}.
MDOcean provides users with two options: to employ a typical nonlinear root-finding algorithm, or to simply use the equation of motion \eqref{eq:eom} to obtain subsequent iterates.
The latter is a form of fixed point iteration where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}}_k = g(\vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}}_{k-1})$, where $g(\vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}})$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}_{\gls{sym-k-generic-index}} = \gls{sym-f}(\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}_{\gls{sym-k-generic-index}-1})$, where $\gls{sym-f}(\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi})$ }\DIFaddend is the equation of motion \Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain} and $\gls{sym-k-generic-index}$  is the iteration index.
It is therefore guaranteed to converge as long as the dynamics are contracting at the solution, which \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{chicone_contraction_2006} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{chicone_contraction_2006} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend shows has the following criteria:
\begin{equation}
 \left|
    \frac{\partial}{\partial \gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}} ~\left[
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{g}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-f}\DIFaddend \left(
        \DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \vec{\hat{\dot{\xi}}}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}\DIFaddend ~
    \right)\right]
\right| < 1
\end{equation}

Five to eight iterations on $|\gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}|$ and $\angle \gls{sym-vec-hat-dot-xi}$  are typically required to converge all sea states to within 0.01~m and 3 degrees. % from summer research slide 44 5/30/24
%DIF < In high-frequency sea states where there is substantial phase difference between $\dot{X}_{\text{rel}}$ and $\dot{X}$, $B_d$ occasionally becomes negative.
%DIF > In high-frequency sea states where there is substantial phase difference between $\gls{sym-dot-X-rel}$ and $\gls{sym-dot-X}$, $\gls{sym-B-d}$ occasionally becomes negative.
At present (since MDOcean v1.0.0), the solver used for drag iteration can be simultaneously used for the numerical optimal control procedure described in \Cref{sec:optimal-control}, although since v1.2.0 by default the solver is used only for drag convergence and optimal control is done analytically.
Compared to numerical optimal control nested inside the drag solve, combining the solvers in this way has the advantages of code simplicity and reducing the number of dynamics evaluations required for a given simulation, provided that the number of iterations required to converge the coupled drag-control solve is less than the product of the number of iterations required to converge each solve individually.
However, it has the disadvantage that the error signal couples drag nonlinearities with constraint violations, which can potentially lead to slower convergence.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Optimal Control Condition}
Note that in the quasi-linear formulation, the lack of complete linearity means that the complex-conjugate reactive controller is technically no longer optimal in the unconstrained case.
The optimal load damping $\gls{sym-B-l}$  is derived by setting
\begin{equation}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD < 0 &= \frac{\partial }{\partial B_l} \left[p_{avg,VI}\right]
%DIFDELCMD < = \frac{\partial}{\partial B_l} \left[\frac{1}{2} B_l |\hat{I}|^2\right]
%DIFDELCMD < = \frac{\partial }{\partial B_l} \left[\frac{1}{2} B_l \left|\frac{\hat{V}_{s,th}}{Z_{s,th}+B_l+K_l/s}\right|^2 \right]
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
0 &= \frac{\partial }{\partial \gls{sym-B-l}} \left[\gls{sym-p-avg-VI}\right]
= \frac{\partial}{\partial \gls{sym-B-l}} \left[\frac{1}{2} \gls{sym-B-l} |\gls{sym-hat-I}|^2\right]
= \frac{\partial }{\partial \gls{sym-B-l}} \left[\frac{1}{2} \gls{sym-B-l} \left|\frac{\gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}}{\gls{sym-Z-s-th}+\gls{sym-B-l}+\gls{sym-K-l}/\gls{sym-s}}\right|^2 \right]
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
and the typical simplification \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\frac{\partial \hat{V}_{s,th}}{\partial B_l}=\frac{\partial Z_{s,th}}{\partial B_l}=0$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\frac{\partial \gls{sym-hat-V-s-th}}{\partial \gls{sym-B-l}}=\frac{\partial \gls{sym-Z-s-th}}{\partial \gls{sym-B-l}}=0$ }\DIFaddend must be replaced with the implicit dependence of the Th\'{e}venin equivalent parameters on \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$B_l$ because $B_l$ affects $\hat{\dot\xi}$ and therefore $\mathbf{B}_{d}$ and $\vec{\gamma}_{d}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-B-l}$ because $\gls{sym-B-l}$ affects $\gls{sym-hat-dot-xi}$ and therefore $\gls{sym-mathbf-B-d}$ and $\gls{sym-vec-gamma-d}$ }\DIFaddend via \eqref{eq:drag-coeffs}.
In practice, however, the standard complex conjugate controller was observed to produce nearly identical power as the one incorporating the more complicated dependence, so the standard is used for simplicity.


%DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFaddbegin \FloatBarrier
\subsection{\DIFadd{Dynamic Limit Details}}
\label{sec:appendix-dynamic-limit}

\DIFadd{The operational and storm loadcases described in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:design-load-cases} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
correspond roughly with the 1-year and 50-year return periods specified in the wave energy design standard }\gls{acr-iec} \gls{acr-ts} \DIFadd{62600-2,
and with the }\gls{acr-fls} \DIFadd{and }\gls{acr-uls} \DIFadd{in offshore wind standards.
When the }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{is in survival mode, an external brake locks the }\gls{acr-pto}\DIFadd{, enforcing the float and spar to move together without loading the generator.
}

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{This section details the allowable amplitudes and forces in each case.
    Currently all sea states in the }\gls{acr-jpd} \DIFadd{with nonzero probability are considered operational, since the generator force and power limit serve to reduce loads in energetic sea states.
    Future studies could control the transition from operational to survival mode more finely, perhaps with an additional input variable representing an operational incident energy threshold.
}\else
    \DIFadd{All nonzero-probability sea states in the }\gls{acr-jpd} \DIFadd{are considered operational;
    the generator force and power limits reduce loads in the most energetic sea states.
}\fi

\subsubsection{\DIFadd{Relationship Between Peak and Fundamental Amplitudes}}
\label{sec:appendix-peak-fundamental}
\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:DLCs} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
summarizes the peak forces and amplitudes based on the assumptions of each design load case.
}

\begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \caption[Forces and amplitudes for each design load case]{\DIFaddFL{Forces, amplitudes, and maximum allowable amplitudes in each design load case}}
\label{tab:DLCs}
\begin{tabular}{P{0.28\linewidth}P{0.3\linewidth}P{0.33\linewidth}}
    & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{\DIFaddFL{Design Loadcase}}}\\\cline{2-3}
    \textbf{\DIFaddFL{Variable}}& \textbf{\DIFaddFL{1: Operational}}&\textbf{\DIFaddFL{2: Storm}}\\ \hline
    \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering \vspace{8pt}
    \DIFaddFL{Peak powertrain force, $\max_{\gls{sym-t}} |\gls{sym-F-p}(\gls{sym-t})|$ }\vspace{8pt} }
        & \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
        \DIFaddFL{Given by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:peak-pto-force}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}}
        & \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
        \DIFaddFL{Solve \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
with \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:op-hydro-coeffs} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
coefficients for $\gls{sym-F-p}$}}\\ \hline
    \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
    \DIFaddFL{Peak fluid force, $\max_{\gls{sym-t}} |\gls{sym-m}\gls{sym-ddot-xi}(\gls{sym-t})-\gls{sym-F-p}(\gls{sym-t})|$ }}
        & \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
        \DIFaddFL{Given by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:peak-fluid-force} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
with \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:op-hydro-coeffs} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
coefficients and $\gls{sym-F-p}$ from \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:peak-pto-force}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}}
        & \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
        \DIFaddFL{Given by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:peak-fluid-force} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
with \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:st-hydro-coeffs} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
coefficients and $\gls{sym-F-p}$ from storm case}}\\ \hline
    \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
    \DIFaddFL{Peak float travel, $\max_{\gls{sym-t}} |\gls{sym-xi-f}(\gls{sym-t})|$ }}
        & \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
        \DIFaddFL{Given by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
with \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:op-hydro-coeffs} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
coefficients}}
        & \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
        \DIFaddFL{Given by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
with \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:st-hydro-coeffs} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
coefficients and $\gls{sym-F-p}=0$}}\\ \hline
    \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
    \DIFaddFL{Peak spar travel, $\max_{\gls{sym-t}} |\gls{sym-xi-s}(\gls{sym-t})|$ }}
        & \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering
        \DIFaddFL{Given by \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:eom-freq-domain} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
with \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:op-hydro-coeffs} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
coefficients}}
        &\DIFaddFL{Equals $\gls{sym-xi-f}$ due to brake}\\ \hline
    \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering \vspace{8pt}
    \DIFaddFL{Maximum allowable relative amplitude between float and spar, $|\gls{sym-xi-f}-\gls{sym-xi-s}|_{\text{max}}$ }\vspace{8pt}}
        & \shortstack{\DIFaddFL{$\min(\gls{sym-h}_{fs,\text{clear}},\gls{sym-h}_{fs,\text{up}},$}\\\DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-h}_{fs,\text{down}})$}}
        &\DIFaddFL{N/A}\\  \hline
    \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering \vspace{8pt}
    \DIFaddFL{Maximum allowable float amplitude, $|\gls{sym-xi-f}|_{\text{max}}$ }\vspace{8pt} }
        & \DIFaddFL{$\min(\gls{sym-xi-f-lin}, \gls{sym-xi-f-slam})$
        }& \DIFaddFL{N/A }\\ \hline %DIF > $\gls{sym-X}_{\text{slam}}$\\
    \parbox[m]{\linewidth}{\centering \vspace{8pt}
    \DIFaddFL{Maximum allowable spar amplitude, $|\gls{sym-xi-s}|_{\text{max}}$ }}
        & \DIFaddFL{$\min(\gls{sym-xi-s-lin}, \gls{sym-xi-s-slam})$
        }& \DIFaddFL{N/A %DIF > $\gls{sym-X}_{\text{slam}}$\\
}\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\DIFadd{The surge force for each component is calculated as \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{newman_motions_1963}
}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:surge-force}
   \gls{sym-F-surge} = \frac{\gls{sym-H}\gls{sym-rho-w} \gls{sym-omega}^2 \gls{sym-A-w}}{\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}} (\exp(-\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-z-top})-\exp(-\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-T-draft}))
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{using the respective waterplane areas $\gls{sym-A-w}$  and drafts $\gls{sym-T-draft}$ for the float and spar.
The top vertical height $\gls{sym-z-top}$ is set to zero for the float and $\gls{sym-T-f-2}$ for the spar.
The design surge force is the maximum surge force over all nonzero-probability sea states.
Equation~\eqref{eq:surge-force} assumes a slender cylinder with diameter much smaller than the wavelength and may be less accurate for the float due to diffraction effects.
Incorporating semi-analytical hydrodynamic coefficients for surge is a possible area of future work.
Maximum acceptable heave %DIF > and surge
forces will be evaluated with structural factors of safety in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:structures}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
The structures model currently lacks the ability to account for surge forces, so the surge force calculation is not used in the simulation.
}

\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{The heave forces in equation \eqref{eq:eom} can be separated into fluid forces, which act on the vertically-oriented wetted surface of the body, the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{force, which acts at the connection between the float and the spar, and the inertial force, which for dynamic purposes is assumed to act at the center of mass but for structural purposes actually acts distributed over the material.
    The distributed nature of the inertial force means that the float and spar structures experience internal loads that vary not only in time but also in space along their lengths.
    Due to force balance in dynamic equilibrium, the maximum magnitude of internal heave force in space at any given time, $\gls{sym-F-heave-f}(\gls{sym-t})$, is the greater of the absolute value of the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{force and the fluid force.
    Expressing the latter as the difference between the inertial force and the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{force, this gives:
}\else
    \DIFadd{By force balance, the peak internal heave force at any cross-section of each body is the greater of the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{force and the fluid (inertial minus }\gls{acr-pto}\DIFadd{) force:
}\fi
\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:heave-force-decomp}
\begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-F-heave-f} (\gls{sym-t}) &=\max\!\left(|\gls{sym-F-p} (\gls{sym-t})|,~ |\gls{sym-m-f}\gls{sym-ddot-xi-f} (\gls{sym-t})-\gls{sym-F-p} (\gls{sym-t})|\right) \\
    \gls{sym-F-heave-s} (\gls{sym-t}) &=\max\!\left(|\gls{sym-F-p} (\gls{sym-t})|,~ |\gls{sym-m-s}\gls{sym-ddot-xi-s} (\gls{sym-t})-\gls{sym-F-p} (\gls{sym-t})|\right)
\end{aligned}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{In operational seas, $\gls{sym-F-p}(\gls{sym-t})$ is determined by the generator torque $\gls{sym-tau}(\gls{sym-t})$ cascaded through the }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{dynamics matrix $\gls{sym-mathbf-a}_{\gls{sym-F-p}\gls{sym-dot-xi}\leftarrow \gls{sym-tau}\gls{sym-Omega}}$.
In storm seas, $\gls{sym-F-p}(\gls{sym-t})$ represents the brake force, and its fundamental amplitude is obtained by solving the 2-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFadd{equation of motion for the force necessary to enforce zero relative motion between the float and spar.
}\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{Maximizing over time requires more care because nonlinearity-induced harmonics cannot be neglected for peak force analysis like they can for power production.
    The peak of $\gls{sym-tau}(\gls{sym-t})$ is lower than its fundamental amplitude due to generator force saturation at }\gls{sym-tau-max}\DIFadd{, so using }\gls{sym-tau-max} \DIFadd{rather than $|\gls{sym-hat-tau}|$ is both less conservative and more realistic.
    Assuming conservatively that the shaft torque constructively interferes with the saturated generator torque, for the operational case we use:
}\else
    \DIFadd{Saturation caps the operational generator torque at $\gls{sym-tau-max}$, which is a less conservative and more realistic peak than the fundamental amplitude $|\gls{sym-hat-tau}|$.
    Assuming constructive interference with the shaft torque,
}\fi
\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:peak-pto-force}
\max_{\gls{sym-t}} | \gls{sym-F-p-op} (\gls{sym-t}) |
\approx \frac{1}{\gls{sym-R-gear}}
\left(
\left| \gls{sym-Z-shaft} \gls{sym-hat-Omega} \right|
                         + \gls{sym-tau-max} \right).
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The storm case lacks the control saturation nonlinearity and uses the fundamental amplitude $\gls{sym-hat-F-p-st}$ directly.
}

\DIFadd{The fluid force nonlinearities $|\gls{sym-m}\gls{sym-ddot-xi}(\gls{sym-t})-\gls{sym-F-p}(\gls{sym-t})|$ are harder to handle, since unlike the generator torque, the peak of the drag force exceeds its fundamental.
MDOcean currently uses the fundamental amplitude for both load cases:
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:peak-fluid-force}
\max_{\gls{sym-t}} \left|\gls{sym-m}\gls{sym-ddot-xi}(\gls{sym-t}) - \gls{sym-F-p}(\gls{sym-t})\right| \approx \left|-\gls{sym-m}\gls{sym-omega}^2 \gls{sym-hat-xi} - \gls{sym-hat-F-p}\right|.
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{This approximation can either under- or overestimate the true peak depending on whether harmonics interfere constructively or destructively with the fundamental; further discussion is in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-peak-fluid-force}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{The fundamental amplitudes are also used to calculate the peak float and spar displacements $\max_{\gls{sym-t}} |\gls{sym-xi-f}(\gls{sym-t})|$ and $\max_{\gls{sym-t}} |\gls{sym-xi-s}(\gls{sym-t})|$ in both cases.
    Using the fundamental for the response displacements is more accurate than for the forces because the system dynamics filter out high frequency inputs.
}\else
    \DIFadd{Peak float and spar displacements likewise use fundamental amplitudes; the second-order plant filters out higher harmonics, making the approximation more accurate for displacements than for forces.
}\fi

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Conservatism of the Fundamental-Amplitude Approximation}}
\label{sec:appendix-peak-fluid-force}

\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:peak-fluid-force} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
approximates the peak fluid force by the magnitude of its fundamental harmonic phasor.
This approximation can either under- or overestimate the true peak depending on whether the higher-harmonic content (primarily from the drag describing function) interferes constructively or destructively with the fundamental.
}

\DIFadd{The fundamental amplitudes are also used for the peak float and spar displacements $\max_{\gls{sym-t}}|\gls{sym-xi-f}(\gls{sym-t})|$ and $\max_{\gls{sym-t}}|\gls{sym-xi-s}(\gls{sym-t})|$ in both load cases.
The fundamental is more accurate for displacements than for forces because the second-order plant dynamics filter out high-frequency inputs, attenuating drag-induced harmonics in the displacement response.
}

\DIFadd{A future refinement could explicitly reconstruct the time-domain drag force from the describing function and track its peak directly, at the cost of either time-domain integration or additional Fourier-component bookkeeping in the frequency-domain formulation.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Maximum Permissible Amplitudes}}
\DIFadd{The table also indicates the maximum permissible amplitudes.
In operational sea states, the geometric clearance between the float and spar is enforced to prevent overtravel.
The permissible heights for upward and downward motion of the float relative to the spar are:
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:h-fs-up-down}
\begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-h-fs-up} &= \gls{sym-h-s} - \gls{sym-T-s} - (\gls{sym-h-f}- \gls{sym-T-f-2}) \\
    \gls{sym-h-fs-down} &= \gls{sym-T-s} - \gls{sym-T-f-2} - \gls{sym-h-d}
\end{aligned}
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{Additionally, a limit of $\gls{sym-h-fs-clear}$ (see definition in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{fig:dims}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
) is required to ensure clearance of the tubular structure connecting the float and spar.
}

\DIFadd{The conditions for linear wave-body interaction are enforced to maintain compatibility with the linear potential flow theory approach of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:hydro}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
This maximum amplitude $\gls{sym-xi-lin}$ for the float and spar respectively is
}\begin{equation}
    \DIFadd{\gls{sym-xi-f-lin} = \frac{1}{10} (\gls{sym-h}-\gls{sym-T-f-2}), \quad
    \gls{sym-xi-s-lin}  = \frac{1}{10} (\gls{sym-h}-\gls{sym-T-s})
}\end{equation}
\ifdefined\DISSERTATION
    \DIFadd{which is derived by requiring that the height of the water column below the body (the relevant dimension determining the hydrodynamic coefficients in }\gls{acr-meem}\DIFadd{) does not change by more than 10\% from its equilibrium value when the body is at its peak displacement.
}\else
    \DIFadd{derived by requiring the water column height below the body (relevant for the }\gls{acr-meem} \DIFadd{hydrodynamic coefficients) to vary by no more than 10\% from equilibrium.
}\fi

\DIFadd{In both operational and storm states, the }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{surface should not transiently pierce the wave free surface, since this causes extremely high loads.
This includes slamming (the bottom surface rises out of the water and impacts on reentry) and submersion (the top of the }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{becomes fully submerged).
The condition to prevent slamming and submersion in regular waves of height $\gls{sym-H}$ is derived in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-slam}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
These limits depend on the phase of the response, not just the magnitude, so they do not use the quadratic coefficients of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{tab:qp-constraints} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
but can nonetheless be expressed in a quadratic form compatible with \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:opt-problem}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
:
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:slam-constraint-quadratic}
    \gls{sym-hat-xi}^*\gls{sym-hat-xi} 
    - \gls{sym-H} \Re(
                    \exp (i\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-y})
                    \gls{sym-hat-xi}
                    )
    < \gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}^2
    -\left(
            \frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2}
    \right)^2
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{for relevant vertical body dimension $\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}$ and horizontal coordinate $\gls{sym-y}$.
The values of $\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}$ used for float and spar in operational and storm cases and the rationale
(avoiding slanted-surface slamming, damping plate surfacing, and related effects)
are given in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:appendix-slam}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}


\DIFaddend %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection{Slamming and Submersion Amplitude}
\label{sec:appendix-slam}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend In regular waves of height $\gls{sym-H}$ , the conditions for slamming can be derived by comparing the vertical position of the bottom of the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{WEC, $\xi(t)-\Delta z_{\text{slam}}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-wec}\DIFadd{, $\gls{sym-xi-t}-\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}$}\DIFaddend , with the wave elevation $\gls{sym-zeta-y-t}$ , which depends on the horizontal position coordinate $\gls{sym-y}$ .
To prevent slamming, the criteria
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slamming-time-domain}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \xi(t) - \Delta z_{slam} &< \zeta(y,t) \\ 
%DIFDELCMD <     |\hat{\xi}|\cos(\omega t+\angle \hat{\xi}) - \Delta z_{slam} &< \frac{H}{2}\cos(\omega t - k y)
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-xi-t} - \gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} &< \gls{sym-zeta-y-t} \\
    |\gls{sym-hat-xi}|\cos(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\angle \gls{sym-hat-xi}) - \gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} &< \frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2}\cos(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t} - \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-y})
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
must be true at all times and over all positions where the body could exit the waves, $-\gls{sym-D}/2<\gls{sym-y}<\gls{sym-D}/2$ .
Note that this expression assumes a sinusoidal waveshape but still applies for irregular waves if a wave-by-wave approach \citep{lin_fast_2025} is utilized.
Manipulating \Cref{eq:slamming-time-domain} to ensure the inequality is satisfied for all $\gls{sym-t}$  yields the criterion:
\begin{equation}
|\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi}\DIFaddend |^2 - \gls{sym-H} \cos(\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ky}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-y}\DIFaddend )|\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi}\DIFaddend |\cos\angle\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi} \DIFaddend + \gls{sym-H} \sin(\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ky}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-y}\DIFaddend )|\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi}\DIFaddend |\sin\angle\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi} \DIFaddend < \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\Delta z_{slam}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}\DIFaddend ^2-\left(\frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2} \right)^2
\end{equation}
which is quadratic in $\gls{sym-hat-xi}$  and therefore already in a form suitable for the constrained optimal control procedure described in \Cref{sec:optimal-control,sec:appendix-qp-solution}, once the appropriate worst-case $\gls{sym-y}$  is substituted.
The remainder of this section develops an explicit expression for the allowable amplitudes under various scenarios and the corresponding worst-case $\gls{sym-y}$ .
Completing the square results in:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slamming-squared}
    \left[
        |\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi}\DIFaddend |- \frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2} \cos(\angle\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi} \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ky}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-y}\DIFaddend )
    \right]^2
    <
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\Delta z_{slam}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}\DIFaddend ^2 - (\gls{sym-H} /2)^2 \sin^2(
                                                        \angle\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi} \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{ky}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-y}
                                                        \DIFaddend )
\end{equation}
In general, after taking the square root of \Cref{eq:slamming-squared}, both the positive and negative branches must be used.
This yields a maximum and minimum amplitude criteria to prevent slamming, $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{min},\text{slam}}<|\gls{sym-hat-xi}|<\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$ , with the following values:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slamming-min-max}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \xi_{max,slam} &= \frac{H}{2} \cos\theta + \sqrt{\Delta z_{slam}^2-\left(\frac{H}{2}\sin\theta\right)^2 } \\
%DIFDELCMD <     \xi_{min,slam} &= \frac{H}{2} \cos\theta - \sqrt{\Delta z_{slam}^2-\left(\frac{H}{2}\sin\theta\right)^2 }
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \gls{sym-xi-max-slam} &= \frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2} \cos\gls{sym-theta-surf} + \sqrt{\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}^2-\left(\frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2}\sin\gls{sym-theta-surf}\right)^2 } \\
    \gls{sym-xi-min-slam} &= \frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2} \cos\gls{sym-theta-surf} - \sqrt{\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}^2-\left(\frac{\gls{sym-H}}{2}\sin\gls{sym-theta-surf}\right)^2 }
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta=\angle\hat{\xi} + ky$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}=\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi} + \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-y}$ }\DIFaddend must be evaluated with the value of $\gls{sym-y}$  that creates the minimum value of $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$  and the maximum value of $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{min},\text{slam}}$  to ensure lack of slamming across the entire \gls{acr-wec} surface.
The relevant value of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$ }\DIFaddend differs depending on the wave amplitude.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Case 1: Small Wave Amplitudes}
For the case of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z_{\text{slam}}>H/2$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} >\gls{sym-H}/2$ }\DIFaddend (wave amplitudes smaller than the draft), \Cref{eq:slamming-min-max} reduces to only a maximum amplitude criteria $|\gls{sym-hat-xi}|<\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$ because $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{min},\text{slam}}<0$ .
This aligns with the intuition that a body fixed at the still water line would not exit the water when the wave amplitude is less than the draft.
The value of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ that minimizes $\xi_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$ that minimizes $\gls{sym-xi-max-slam}$ }\DIFaddend over the relevant range is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slamming-limit-small-waves}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\theta }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-theta-surf} \DIFaddend = \gls{sym-pi} - \max\left(
                                            0,~
                                            \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{-kD}{2}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{-\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D}}{2}
                                            }\DIFaddend + |\gls{sym-pi} -\angle \gls{sym-xi} |
                                     \right)
\end{equation}
where the symmetry of \Cref{eq:slamming-min-max} has been used to collapse possible values onto $[0,\gls{sym-pi}]$ .

This expression reduces to the simple limits \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z_{\text{slam}}-H/2$ and $\Delta z_{\text{slam}}+H/2$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} -\gls{sym-H}/2$ and $\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} +\gls{sym-H}/2$
}\DIFaddend for short wavelengths \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$(\theta=\pi)$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$(\gls{sym-theta-surf}=\gls{sym-pi})$ }\DIFaddend and long wavelengths \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$(\theta=0)$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$(\gls{sym-theta-surf}=0)$ }\DIFaddend respectively.
These limits are also the minimum and maximum values of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\xi_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-xi-max-slam}$ }\DIFaddend for any diameter and wave condition where \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z_{\text{slam}}>H/2$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} >\gls{sym-H}/2$}\DIFaddend .
For intermediate wavelengths, the variable \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$ }\DIFaddend accurately accounts for the effect of body diameter and the phase offset of body motion from the waves.
Intuitively, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$ }\DIFaddend represents how high up the free surface the bottom of the \gls{acr-wec} is allowed to get before the edge exits the water,
with \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta=0$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}=0$ }\DIFaddend allowing the body to get all the way to the wave crest (top)
and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta=\pi$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}=\gls{sym-pi}$ }\DIFaddend requiring the float to remain fully below the wave trough (bottom).

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Case 2: Large Wave Amplitudes}
In sufficiently large waves (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$H/2>\Delta z_{\text{slam}}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-H}/2>\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}$}\DIFaddend ), \Cref{eq:slamming-limit-small-waves} does not apply.
Intuitively, when the wave amplitude exceeds the draft, a minimum amplitude of motion in-phase with the incident wave is required to prevent the wave trough from going below the body surface.
The minimum amplitude arises mathematically when $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{min},\text{slam}}>0$ , which occurs exactly for waves meeting the amplitude criterion.
The ``in-phase'' motion requirement is consistent with the observation that in this wave regime, the maximum amplitude in \Cref{eq:slamming-min-max} becomes unsatisfiable (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\xi_{\text{max},\text{slam}}<0$) for WEC }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{max},\text{slam}}<0$) for }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFaddend phases that are more than 90 degrees out of phase with the incident wave (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta \in [\pi/2,3\pi/2]$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf} \in [\gls{sym-pi}/2,3\gls{sym-pi}/2]$}\DIFaddend ).

For evaluating \Cref{eq:slamming-min-max}, the value of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$ }\DIFaddend that produces both minimum $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$ and maximum $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{min},\text{slam}}$  in this regime is:
\begin{equation}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\theta }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-theta-surf} \DIFaddend = \angle\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi} %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi} \DIFaddend + \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{kD}{2}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D}}{2}}\DIFaddend \textrm{sgn}(\gls{sym-pi} -\angle\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \hat{\xi}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-hat-xi}\DIFaddend )
\end{equation}
After wrapping the value onto $[0,2\gls{sym-pi}]$ , it can be mapped to $[0,\gls{sym-pi}]$  without changing the value of \Cref{eq:slamming-min-max} via the identity \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\cos(\theta)=\cos(\pi-|\pi-\theta|)$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\cos(\gls{sym-theta-surf})=\cos(\gls{sym-pi}-|\gls{sym-pi}-\gls{sym-theta-surf}|)$}\DIFaddend .

Notably, in this wave regime it becomes possible for the right hand side of \Cref{eq:slamming-squared} to become negative for certain values of $\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi}$ and $\gls{sym-D}$ .
This means that those combinations of $\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi}$ and $\gls{sym-D}$  are prohibited because slamming would occur regardless of the amplitude $|\gls{sym-hat-xi}|$ .
The following requirement on $\gls{sym-D}$  ensures that there exist some values of $\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi}$  without automatic slamming:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:D-slamming}
    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{kD}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D}
    \DIFaddend <
    2\arcsin\left(
                    \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\frac{\Delta z_{slam}}{H/2}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\frac{\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}}{\gls{sym-H}/2}
            }\DIFaddend \right)
\end{equation}
which has upper bound \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$kD<\pi$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D}<\gls{sym-pi}$}\DIFaddend .
Once \Cref{eq:D-slamming} is satisfied, the set of acceptable $\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi}$  that avoid negative values for both $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$  and the right hand side of \Cref{eq:slamming-squared} are:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slamming-allowed-angles}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{aligned}
%DIFDELCMD <     \angle\hat{\xi} \in &\left[0,~~\arcsin\left(\frac{\Delta z_{slam}}{H/2}\right)-\frac{kD}{2}\right]
%DIFDELCMD <     \cup \left[2\pi-\arcsin\left(\frac{\Delta z_{slam}}{H/2}\right)+\frac{kD}{2}, ~~2\pi\right]
%DIFDELCMD < \end{aligned}%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{aligned}
    \angle\gls{sym-hat-xi} \in &\left[
                                    0,
                                    ~~
                                    \arcsin\left(
                                                \frac{\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}}{\gls{sym-H}/2}
                                           \right)
                                    -\frac{
                                            \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D}
                                            }{2}
                               \right]
    \cup \left[
                2\gls{sym-pi}-\arcsin\left(
                                            \frac{\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}}{\gls{sym-H}/2}
                                     \right)
                +\frac{
                        \gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D}
                      }{2},
                ~~2\gls{sym-pi}
        \right]
\end{aligned}\DIFaddend 
\end{equation}
At the edge-case $\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi}$  on the inner boundaries of this interval,
only a single amplitude is permitted because the values for
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\xi_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$ and $\xi_{\text{min},\text{slam}}$ coincide at $\sqrt{(H/2)^2-\Delta z_{\text{slam}}^2}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{max},\text{slam}}$ and $\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{min},\text{slam}}$
coincide at $\sqrt{(\gls{sym-H}/2)^2-\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} ^2}$}\DIFaddend .
At any lower amplitude, the \gls{acr-wec} would exit the water at the wave trough,
and any higher, the \gls{acr-wec} would raise out of the water at the wave crest.
The permissible amplitude range can be widened by decreasing $\gls{sym-D}$
or moving $\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi}$  towards the closer of $0$ and $2\gls{sym-pi}$ .
The widest permissible amplitude range occurs when the \gls{acr-wec} is perfectly in-phase with the incident wave
($\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi}=0$ )
and is small with respect to the wavelength
(\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$kD\approx0$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-k-wavenumber}\gls{sym-D}\approx0$}\DIFaddend ),
which yields the limits
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$H/2-\Delta z_{\text{slam}} < |\hat{\xi}| < H/2+\Delta z_{\text{slam}}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-H}/2-\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} < |\gls{sym-hat-xi}| < \gls{sym-H}/2+\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}$}\DIFaddend .

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsubsection{Implementation}
\Cref{fig:slam} visualizes the nondimensional maximum and minimum slamming amplitudes
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\xi_{\text{max},\text{slam}}/(H/2)$ and $\xi_{\text{min},\text{slam}}/(H/2)$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-xi-max-slam}/(\gls{sym-H}/2)$ and $\gls{sym-xi-min-slam}/(\gls{sym-H}/2)$
}\DIFaddend as a function of the worst-case phase angle \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$
}\DIFaddend and the draft to wave amplitude ratio \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z_{\text{slam}}/(H/2)$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} /(\gls{sym-H}/2)$}\DIFaddend .
This reveals that besides increasing the draft, decreasing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$ }\DIFaddend can prevent slamming,
for example by decreasing the diameter or adjusting $\angle \gls{sym-xi}$  via control.
If \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z_{\text{slam}}<H/2|\sin\theta|$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} <\gls{sym-H}/2|\sin\gls{sym-theta-surf}|$}\DIFaddend ,
the design is unviable and the slamming limits are undefined,
consistent with the negative right hand side of \Cref{eq:slamming-squared} discussed above.
\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=.8\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/slamming_amplitude.pdf}
    }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/slamming_amplitude.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/slamming_amplitude.pdf}
    }\DIFaddendFL \caption{Nondimensional critical slamming amplitude}
    \label{fig:slam}
\end{figure}

Separately, there exists a symmetrical counterpart to the slamming condition that we will call the submersion condition.
While preventing slamming requires that the bottom of the \gls{acr-wec} remains below the free surface,
preventing submersion requires that the top of the \gls{acr-wec} remains above the free surface.
Analogous to \eqref{eq:slamming-time-domain}, we obtain:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:submersion}
    \gls{sym-xi-t} + \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\Delta z_{sub} }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-dDelta-z-sub} \DIFaddend > \gls{sym-zeta-y-t} \end{equation}

Conceptually, both the slamming requirement \eqref{eq:slamming-time-domain} and the submersion requirement \eqref{eq:submersion} must be applied to both the float and the spar for a total of 4 free surface constraints for each sea state.
However, the limit on $|\gls{sym-hat-xi-f}-\gls{sym-hat-xi-s}|$  described in \Cref{tab:DLCs} already ensures that the top of the spar remains above the top of the float across all dynamic conditions.
Coupled with the fact that the float diameter always exceeds the spar diameter, this means that satisfaction of the float submersion requirement implies automatic satisfaction of the spar submersion requirement.
While this logic works for submersion, it does not apply for slamming because the damping plate diameter may exceed the float diameter.
The remaining three free surface constraints are aggregated into a single constraint for each sea state, using the minimum z-dimension for each body \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z_{\text{surf}}=\min(\Delta z_{\text{sub}}, \Delta z_{\text{slam}})$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-surf}=\min(\gls{sym-dDelta-z-sub}, \gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam} )$}\DIFaddend , where the values for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z_{\text{sub}}$ and $\Delta z_{\text{slam}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-sub} $ and $\gls{sym-dDelta-z-slam}$ }\DIFaddend were given in \Cref{sec:dynamics}.
This reduces the number of free surface constraints per sea state from four to one.

For operational waves, the vertical dimension \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-surf}$ }\DIFaddend in \Cref{eq:slam-constraint-quadratic} is set as follows.
For float slamming, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z = T_{f,1}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-surf} = \gls{sym-T-f-1}$ }\DIFaddend (rather than $\gls{sym-T-f-2}$ ):
this prevents slamming on the slanted underside of the float and additionally maintains constant waterplane area,
avoiding unmodeled nonlinearities in the hydrostatic stiffness and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Froude–Krylov }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Froude-Krylov }\DIFaddend excitation force.
For spar slamming, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z = T_s - h_d$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-surf} = \gls{sym-T-s} - \gls{sym-h-d}$
}\DIFaddend prevents both slamming on the bottom of the spar and surfacing of the damping plate.
For float and spar submersion,
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-surf}$
}\DIFaddend is set to the above-water heights
$\gls{sym-h-f} - \gls{sym-T-f-2}$
and
$\gls{sym-h-s} - \gls{sym-T-s}$
 respectively.

The storm case slamming constraint is not currently applied.
A future extension should set
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\Delta z = T_{f,2}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-dDelta-z-surf} = \gls{sym-T-f-2}$
}\DIFaddend in the storm case to prevent slamming on the (now-merged) bottom surface of the float-spar system.

The amplitude limits
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$h_{fs,\text{clear}}$,
$h_{fs,\text{up}}$,
$h_{fs,\text{down}}$,
$\xi_{f,\text{linear}}$, and
$\xi_{s,\text{linear}}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-h-fs-clear}$,
$\gls{sym-h-fs-up}$,
$\gls{sym-h-fs-down}$,
$\gls{sym-xi-f-lin}$, and
$\gls{sym-xi-s-lin}$
}\DIFaddend are sea-state-independent upper limits, while
$\gls{sym-xi}_{\text{slam}}$
 depends on the sea state and can act as both an upper and lower limit on oscillation magnitude.

Relevant model values for the nominal float design under reactive optimal control are plotted for each sea state in \Cref{fig:slamming-validation}.
Comparing the first two subplots for the phase of the float motion $\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi-f}$  and the slamming angle \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\theta$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-theta-surf}$
}\DIFaddend shows that the slamming angle roughly ``wraps'' the motion angle around a value of $\gls{sym-pi}/2$ , colored white in the figure.
The third plot shows that the minimum slamming and submersion amplitude is not a concern since it is negative for all sea states and thus will never be an active constraint.
The fourth plot shows the maximum amplitude limit and the fifth depicts the actual amplitude, with amplitudes in violation of the limit indicated with an overlaid black hatching.
A significant fraction of sea states violate the limit and will require adjusted control gains computed via constrained optimal control per \Cref{sec:optimal-control,sec:appendix-qp-solution}.
Significant wave heights in excess of 5.5 meters have negative maximum slamming amplitudes (colored blue),
indicating that control adjustments in those waves must not only decrease $|\gls{sym-hat-xi-f}|$
 but also adjust \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\angle\hat{\xi}_f$ to reduce $\theta$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\angle\gls{sym-hat-xi-f}$ to reduce $\gls{sym-theta-surf}$}\DIFaddend .
\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=1.05\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/slamming_model_comparison_float.pdf}
    }%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/slamming_model_comparison_float.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/slamming_model_comparison_float.pdf}
    }\caption[Slamming model results for nominal \gls{acr-rm3} float design]{\DIFaddendFL Slamming Model Results for Nominal \gls{acr-rm3} Float Design in Operational Sea States}
    \label{fig:slamming-validation}
\end{figure}

%DIF < %%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsubsection{\DIFdel{Conservatism of the Fundamental-Amplitude Approximation for Fluid Forces}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsubsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:appendix-peak-fluid-force}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend %DIF > %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFaddbegin \FloatBarrier
\subsection{\DIFadd{Narrow-band Wave Approximation}}
\label{sec:appendix-narrowband}
\DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{sec:irregular-waves} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
introduced the narrow-band wave environment approximation,
in which an irregular sea state is approximated by a regular wave at its energy-equivalent period and wave height.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:peak-fluid-force} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
approximates the peak fluid force by the magnitude of its fundamental harmonic phasor.
This approximation can either under- or overestimate the true peak depending on whether the higher-harmonic content (primarily from the drag describing function) interferes constructively or destructively with the fundamental}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{In addition to enabling the QCQP formulation for the linearized system to be single-frequency and thus solvable analytically,
even for the nonlinear system the regular wave assumption may be desirable to avoid the time-consuming convolution integral or state-space identification that is typically required to simulate nonlinear dynamics subject to irregular wave forcing.
}

\DIFadd{In the absence of dynamic constraints, the regular wave assumption does not significantly affect power production if the ultimate design utilizes a high-order linear controller capable of impedance matching over the bandwidth of each sea state.
If the ultimate design instead utilizes a simple PI controller, the assumption slightly overestimates the power produced in a broadband wave environment.
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{coe_practical_2021} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
used a Bretschneider spectrum and found the irregular wave power generation for a point absorber with PI control to be around 92\% of the perfectly matched power, which is sufficiently close to justify the approximation for early design phases without worrying about the complexity tradeoff of controller order}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{The fundamental amplitudes are also used for the peak float and spar displacements $\max_t|\xi_f(t)|$ and $\max_t|\xi_s(t)|$ in both load cases .
The fundamental is more accurate for displacements than for forces because the second-order plant dynamics filter out high-frequency inputs, attenuating drag-induced harmonics in the displacement response}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \subsection{\DIFadd{Power Matrix Decomposition}}
\label{sec:appendix-power-decomposition}

\DIFadd{The efficiency matrices introduced in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:power-matrix-decomposition} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
are defined as follows.
The wave power density $\gls{sym-mathbf-J-H-T-wave}$ and maximum capture width  $\gls{sym-mathbf-CW-H-T-max}$ are independent of design:
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\mathbf{J}^{H,T}_{wave} = \frac{\gls{sym-rho-w} \gls{sym-g}^2}{64\gls{sym-pi}} \gls{sym-T-e} \gls{sym-H-s}^2, \qquad
\mathbf{CW}^{\gls{sym-H},\gls{sym-T-period}}_{max} = \frac{\gls{sym-g} \gls{sym-T-e}^2}{4\gls{sym-pi}^2}.
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The maximum capture width matrix represents the radiation limit determined by the }\gls{acr-wec} \DIFadd{mode of motion
(heave in this case; for surge or pitch the value would be doubled due to the asymmetry of the outgoing wave field).
}

\DIFadd{The radiation and drag efficiencies are defined relative to a drag-free simulation $\mathbf{\gls{sym-P}}_{\gls{sym-F-PTO}\gls{sym-dot-X},\text{no drag}}$ (computed with $\gls{sym-C-d-f}=\gls{sym-C-d-s}=0$, ideal }\gls{acr-pto}\DIFadd{):
}\begin{equation}
\DIFadd{\gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-rad} = \frac{\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{no drag}}}{\mathbf{J}^{H,T}_{wave}\,\mathbf{CW}^{\gls{sym-H},\gls{sym-T-period}}_{max}}, \qquad
\gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-drag} = \frac{\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{ideal}}}{\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{no drag}}}.
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{$\gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-drag}$ may exceed 1 at frequencies where the drag-free system is so undamped that
the unconstrained controller from \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:matched-load,eq:damping-control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
would induce instability;
in such cases a sub-optimal controller is substituted, which lowers
$\mathbf{\gls{sym-P}}_{\gls{sym-F-PTO}\gls{sym-dot-X},\text{no drag}}$
(the denominator) rather than raising the numerator.
In cases where the unconstrained controller is stable,
$\gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-drag} \leq 1$ is guaranteed}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{A future refinement could explicitly reconstruct the time-domain drag force from the describing function and track its peak directly, at the cost of either time-domain integration or additional Fourier-component bookkeeping in the frequency-domain formulation }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The product of the first four matrices is the theoretical ideal mechanical power:
}\begin{equation}
    \DIFadd{\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{F\dot{X},\text{ideal}} = \mathbf{J}^{H,T}_{wave}
    \,
    \mathbf{CW}^{\gls{sym-H},\gls{sym-T-period}}_{max}
    \,
    \gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-rad}
    \,
    \gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-drag} 
    = \frac{
                |\gls{sym-mathbf-hat-F-H-T-th}|^2
            }{
                8\Re(\gls{sym-mathbf-Z-H-T-i-th})
            }.
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{The remaining two efficiency matrices capture the loss to dynamic constraints and to }\gls{acr-pto} \DIFadd{electrical inefficiency:
}\begin{equation}
    \DIFadd{\gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-limits} = \frac{\gls{sym-mathbf-P-H-T-F-dot-X}}{\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{F\dot{X},\text{ideal}}}, \qquad
    \gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T-elec} = \gls{sym-eta} \frac{\gls{sym-mathbf-P-H-T-IV}}{\gls{sym-mathbf-P-H-T-F-dot-X}}.
}\end{equation}

\DIFadd{Although it would be possible to formulate alternative variants such as $\gls{sym-boldsymbol-eta-H-T}_{\text{drag},\text{var}} = \gls{sym-mathbf-P-H-T-IV}/\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{IV,\text{no drag}}$ that capture the full effect of drag on the saturated system, such matrices would represent independent sensitivities and could not be multiplied together to recover the overall power matrix.
The current decomposition preserves multiplicativity, which is the key advantage of the formulation in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:power-matrix-decomposition}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend .

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFaddbegin \FloatBarrier
\DIFaddend \subsection{Dynamic Validation}
\label{sec:appendix-dynamic-validation}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \subsubsection{WEC-Sim}
The drag describing function and \gls{acr-meem} hydrodynamic coefficients have a minor effect assuming a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{1-DOF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{1-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend system (9.7\% and 2.7\% error on the average power and maximum amplitude respectively) but a major effect on the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{2-DOF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{2-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend system (38.2\% and 28.6\% respectively).
Meanwhile, the error is extremely low in the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{2-DOF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{2-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend system enforcing the same hydrodynamic coefficients as WEC-Sim and with zero drag (0.2\% and 1.9\% in power and amplitude respectively).
This indicates that the larger error in the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{2-DOF-drag-MEEM }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{2-}\gls{acr-dof}\DIFadd{-drag-}\gls{acr-meem} \DIFaddend case is not an error in the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{2-DOF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{2-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend dynamic model itself, but in the way that a \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{2-DOF }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{2-}\gls{acr-dof} \DIFaddend model amplifies errors in drag and hydrodynamic coefficients due to the importance of the phase of motion between each \gls{acr-dof} .
The remainder of this subsection details the four-quadrant breakdown across drag-on/drag-off and identical/different hydrodynamic coefficient configurations.

A WEC-Sim parallel multiple condition run is performed in accelerator mode for 200s with a 100s ramp time using the ode4 solver and a 10~ms timestep, with the default mass properties and body geometry of the WEC-Sim \gls{acr-rm3} \DIFadd{tutorial.} \footnote{This geometry is not identical to the nominal float design used in the Reference Model Report \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\cite{RM3} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{RM3} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
}\DIFaddend and the rest of this paper, with a shorter spar draft, thicker damping plate, wider float frustum diameter, and assumed deep-water depth regime.
For consistency, MDOcean also uses this alternate geometry for WEC-Sim comparisons only.}
Each of the 210 sea states are simulated individually as the equivalent regular wave.
Body to body interactions (nonzero off-diagonal hydrodynamic coefficients) and state-space computations are turned on.
%\Cref{tab:dynamic-validation} and
\Cref{fig:dynamic-validation} compares the results.
The $\gls{sym-C-d}=0$  simulation with identical hydrodynamic coefficients (top left of \Cref{fig:dynamic-validation}) is conducted to verify correct implementation of the linear dynamics and consistency of sign conventions.
The validation obtains an error of less than $0.2\%$ for wave periods below 12 seconds.
This aligns with the perfect matching that is expected in the absence of drag non-linearities, and we attribute the tiny error that remains to the discrete-time nature of WEC-Sim's numerical integration and post-processing.
For wave periods above 12 seconds, MDOcean significantly overpredicts the mechanical power.
An additional validation for this case with the spar held fixed (not shown) maintained the excellent $<0.2\%$ accuracy even for $\gls{sym-T-period}>12 \gls{sym-s}$ , so we attribute the error to resonant instabilities that occur in the multi-body hydrodynamic interactions in the highly underdamped drag-free dynamics.
Turning on drag ($\gls{sym-C-d}=1$ , top right of \Cref{fig:dynamic-validation}) substantially resolves this inaccuracy, with power errors of $<5\%$ for the most common moderate sea states and error exceeding $40\%$ at the most energetic sea states.
While determining the exact source of the error requires further investigation, it is deemed low-impact enough to justify the model's use in an early-stage design setting.

Moving to the bottom left corner of \Cref{fig:dynamic-validation}, we examine the effect of the error in MDOcean's \gls{acr-meem} hydrodynamic coefficients on its dynamic power production.
The error closely aligns with the error in the identical hydro coefficient case, including the large errors at longer wave periods, confirming that the small error in the \gls{acr-meem} hydro coefficients has minimal effect on the overall dynamic response.
Finally, the bottom right corner of \Cref{fig:dynamic-validation} shows the combined effect of drag and hydro coefficient errors, which is the best representation of MDOcean's total dynamic error in the absence of further model improvements.
Power is underpredicted by up to 40\% at sea states with moderate (10-12s) periods and low wave heights, and overpredicted at both shorter and longer wave periods.
This results in a beneficial positive and negative error cancellation that explains the low error in weighted average power described in \Cref{sec:dynamic-validation}.
Meanwhile, MDOcean underpredicts the peak power by around 25\%.
Error scales more closely with wave period than wave height for periods under 12 seconds, while the opposite is true for periods above 12 seconds.

Besides mechanical power, a variety of other signals including amplitudes, phases, and forces are compared between the models.
While most signals show good agreement between the models, interestingly, large errors are observed in the drag forces, although these errors do not cause significant deviations in the overall system response.
\Cref{fig:drag-validation} compares the drag force on the float and spar in each model using identical hydro coefficients and $\gls{sym-C-d}=1$ .

\begin{landscape}
\begingroup
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{c m{1em} | M{.47\linewidth} | M{.47\linewidth} }
  && \multicolumn{2}{c}{Drag Force}\\
         && Magnitude& Phase\\ \hline
    \multirow{2}{*}[-3em]{\rot{Body}} &\rot{Float}&

  }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/wecsim_wcsm_multi_drag_on_meem_off__spar_drag_force_fund.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/wecsim_wcsm_multi_drag_on_meem_off__spar_drag_force_fund.pdf}
  }\DIFaddendFL & \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/wecsim_wcsm_multi_drag_on_meem_off__spar_drag_force_phase.pdf} }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/wecsim_wcsm_multi_drag_on_meem_off__spar_drag_force_phase.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/wecsim_wcsm_multi_drag_on_meem_off__spar_drag_force_phase.pdf} }\DIFaddendFL \\
\end{tabular}
\DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \caption{%%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \caption[Drag force comparison across modeling assumptions]{\DIFaddendFL Drag force comparison showing large errors in drag force, despite low errors in amplitude and power}
\label{fig:drag-validation}
\fillandplacepagenumber
\end{figure}
\endgroup
\end{landscape}
The reason for this discrepancy remains unclear, since the expected error of the describing function approximation due to higher harmonics would increase with frequency, whereas the observed error is worse at mid-range frequencies.
Evidently, amplitude and power results are quite sensitive to the presence of drag, but not very sensitive to the exact magnitude and phase of the drag force.
The WEC-Sim simulation uses drag-only Morison elements with a maximum width of one tenth the incident wavelength to ensure accuracy of strip theory, and uses a Fourier transform to extract the fundamental amplitude and phase of the nonlinear drag waveform.
Future work should investigate the source of this error and whether it can be reduced by adjusting the drag model.

We also validate the describing function approximation for the drag force.
The general application of the describing function technique requires that the fundamental frequency $\gls{sym-omega}$  contains the vast majority of the energy, resulting in body displacements of the form $\gls{sym-xi}(\gls{sym-t}) \sim \cos(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\gls{sym-phi})$ .
\Cref{fig:wecsim-thd} shows the total harmonic distortion of the float displacement for each sea state in the $\gls{sym-C-d}=1$  case, which peaks at 1\% in the worst-case sea state and is below 0.5\% for the majority of sea states.
This confirms that higher harmonics have a negligible impact on the system and that the describing function method is a valid approach for modeling drag in this context.
The particular describing function relation used for drag assumes that the drag force has the form
$\gls{sym-F-d-t} \sim |\cos(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\gls{sym-phi})|
                       \cos(\gls{sym-omega} \gls{sym-t}+\gls{sym-phi})$ .
\Cref{fig:wecsim-drag-waveform} shows the assumed and actual drag force waveform for four representative sea states corresponding to the four corners of the \gls{acr-jpd} .
The match is excellent in all four cases, confirming that the drag force is indeed well approximated by this form, even in high-amplitude, high-frequency conditions where the nonlinearity is expected to be strongest.

\subsubsection{Reference Model Report}
While the original reference model report \citep{RM3} does not document the intermediate values used in dynamics calculations such as response amplitude or hydrodynamic coefficients,
it does record the mechanical power for each sea state.
\Cref{fig:report-power-validation} compares these values against those predicted by WEC-Sim and MDOcean.
\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{\includegraphics[width=1.05\linewidth]{../../../base/pubs/applied-ocean-research-model/figs/from-matlab/wecsim_rpt_multi_drag_on_meem_on__power_mech_unsat.pdf}
    }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{\includegraphics[\FigureOptions{aor:figs/from-matlab/wecsim_rpt_multi_drag_on_meem_on__power_mech_unsat.pdf}]{figs/from-matlab/wecsim_rpt_multi_drag_on_meem_on__power_mech_unsat.pdf}
    }\DIFaddendFL \caption{Comparison of mechanical power for \gls{acr-rm3} report \cite{RM3} and MDOcean}
    \label{fig:report-power-validation}
\end{figure}
Note that the data is independent of \Cref{fig:dynamic-validation} due to the disparate geometry between the reference model report and provided WEC-Sim hydrodynamic coefficients,
and that the power multiplier mentioned in \Cref{sec:dynamic-validation} has not been applied in the figure.
We observe a rather poor match, with MDOcean underestimating power in the low-period sea states and overestimating in the high-period sea states.
The discrepancy is of minimal concern for the reasons described in \Cref{sec:dynamic-validation}.
Nonetheless, researchers and practitioners should be aware of the inconsistency and exercise caution before using the reference model report's power values for technology benchmarking or model validation.


%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{Power Matrix Decomposition}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:appendix-power-decomposition}
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The efficiency matrices introduced in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:power-matrix-decomposition} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
are defined as follows.
The wave power density and maximum capture width are independent of design:
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\mathbf{J}^{H,T}_{wave} = \frac{\rho_w g^2}{64\pi} T_e H_s^2, \qquad
\mathbf{CW}^{H,T}_{max} = \frac{g T_e^2}{4\pi^2}.
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The radiation and drag efficiencies are defined relative to a drag-free simulation $\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{no drag}}$ (computed with $C_{d,f}=C_{d,s}=0$, ideal PTO):
}\begin{displaymath}
\DIFdel{\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{rad} = \frac{\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{no drag}}}{\mathbf{J}^{H,T}_{wave}\,\mathbf{CW}^{H,T}_{max}}, \qquad
\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{drag} = \frac{\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{ideal}}}{\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{no drag}}}.
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{$\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{\text{drag}}$ may exceed 1 at frequencies where the drag-free system is so undamped that the unconstrained controller from \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:matched-load,eq:damping-control} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
would induce instability; in such cases a sub-optimal controller is substituted, which lowers $\mathbf{P}_{F\dot{X},\text{no drag}}$ (the denominator) rather than raising the numerator.
In cases where the unconstrained controller is stable, $\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{\text{drag}} \leq 1$ is guaranteed.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{The product of the first four matrices is the theoretical ideal mechanical power:
}\begin{displaymath}
    \DIFdel{\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{F\dot{X},\text{ideal}} = \mathbf{J}^{H,T}_{wave}\,\mathbf{CW}^{H,T}_{max}\,\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{rad}\,\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{drag} = \frac{|\mathbf{\hat{F}}^{H,T}_{th}|^2}{8\Re(\mathbf{Z}^{H,T}_{i,th})}.
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{The remaining two efficiency matrices capture the loss to dynamic constraints and to PTO electrical inefficiency:
}\begin{displaymath}
    \DIFdel{\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{limits} = \frac{\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{F\dot{X}}}{\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{F\dot{X},\text{ideal}}}, \qquad
    \mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{elec} = \eta \frac{\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{IV}}{\mathbf{P}^{H,T}_{F\dot{X}}}.
}\end{displaymath}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdel{Although it would be possible to formulate alternative variants such as $\mathbf{\eta}^{H,T}_{\text{drag},\text{var}} = \mathbf{P}_{IV}/\mathbf{P}_{IV,\text{no drag}}$ that capture the full effect of drag on the saturated system, such matrices would represent independent sensitivities and could not be multiplied together to recover the overall power matrix.
The current decomposition preserves multiplicativity, which is the key advantage of the formulation in \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\Cref{eq:power-matrix-decomposition}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%DIF < %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\subsection{Power-Law Scaling with Number of Devices}
\label{sec:appendix-econ-power-law}

The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{per-WEC }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{per-}\gls{acr-wec} \DIFaddend unit costs and prices in \Cref{eq:unit-cost}
(\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$C_{\text{fixed}}$,
 $C_{pto,\text{constant}}$, 
 $p_{P}$,
 $p_{F}$,
 $p_{s}$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-C}_{\text{fixed}}$,
 $\gls{sym-C}_{pto,\text{constant}}$, 
 $\gls{sym-p-P}$,
 $\gls{sym-p-F}$,
 $\gls{sym-p-s}$}\DIFaddend )
each decrease with the number of devices \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$N_{WEC}$ according to
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-N-WEC}$ according to
}\DIFaddend \begin{equation}\label{eq:cost-power-law}
    \gls{sym-C} = \gls{sym-C-infty} + (\gls{sym-C-1} \DIFadd{- }\gls{sym-C-infty} )
                    \cdot (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{N_{WEC}}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{sym-N-WEC}\DIFaddend )^{-\gls{sym-beta-expo}} ,
\end{equation}
where $\gls{sym-C-infty}$  is the asymptotic large-scale unit cost,
$\gls{sym-C-1}$  is the single-unit cost,
and $\gls{sym-beta-expo}$  is the rate of decrease.

The values of \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$C_\infty$, $C_1$, and $\beta$ for each CBS }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\gls{sym-C-infty}$, $\gls{sym-C-1}$, and $\gls{sym-beta-expo}$ for each }\gls{acr-cbs} \DIFaddend category
were obtained by curve-fitting \Cref{eq:cost-power-law} to the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{CBS estimates
for $N_{WEC} \in \{1, 10, 50, 100\}$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-cbs} \DIFadd{estimates
for $\gls{sym-N-WEC} \in \{1, 10, 50, 100\}$ }\DIFaddend \citep{neary_reference_2014},
and are tabulated in \Cref{tab:econ-model-values}.

\begin{table}[h]
    \centering
    \caption{Cost model values for each \gls{acr-cbs} category\DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{. Cost values are in 2012 USD.}\DIFaddendFL }
    \label{tab:econ-model-values}
    \DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \begin{tabular}{
%DIFDELCMD <         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.33\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.1\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\linewidth}
%DIFDELCMD <         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\linewidth}}
%DIFDELCMD <          %%%
\DIFdelFL{CBS }\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \expanded{
        \noexpand\tabular{\TableColumnSpecs{aor:econ-model-values}}
    }
         \gls{acr-cbs} \DIFaddendFL Category& \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Exponent $\beta$}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{Expo. $\gls{sym-beta-expo}$}\DIFaddendFL &Unit cost at scale \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{$C_{\infty}$}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \DIFaddFL{$\gls{sym-C-infty}$}\DIFaddendFL &Single-unit cost \newline $\gls{sym-C-1}$ \\\hline
         \textbf{1.1-1.3, 1.6-1.9} - Design-Indep. \gls{acr-capex} & 0.741& 1.24 \$M &13.92 \$M\\
         \textbf{1.4} - Structural \gls{acr-capex} & 0.481& 2.387 \$/kg&4.294 \$/kg\\
         \textbf{1.5} - \DIFdelbeginFL \DIFdelFL{Power Take Off CAPEX}\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \gls{acr-pto} \gls{acr-capex}\DIFaddendFL & 0.206& Constant: 92.59 \$k Power: 0.4454 \$/W Force: 0.0086 \$/N &Constant: 93.64 \$k Power: 1.355 \$/W Force: 0.0204 \$/N\\
 \textbf{2.1-2.6} - \gls{acr-opex} &0.557& 0&1.193 \$M\\
    \DIFdelbeginFL %DIFDELCMD < \end{tabular}
%DIFDELCMD <     %%%
\DIFdelendFL \DIFaddbeginFL \endtabular
    \DIFaddendFL \end{table}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\subsection{Linear \gls{acr-pto} Cost Scaling}
\label{sec:appendix-econ-pto-scaling}

Linear \gls{acr-pto} cost scaling is common for \gls{acr-wec} techno-economic analysis.
The \gls{acr-rm3} cost numbers \citep{RM3} which MDOcean uses as a baseline are themselves scaled from estimates for a smaller device: the reference model project applies linear scaling with peak power to an unpublished 2011 cost estimate by ReVision Consulting for a 100~kW peak hydraulic \gls{acr-pto} (except for the riser cable and control system, whose cost does not scale with power).
The \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{NREL System Advisory Model }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \gls{acr-nrel} \gls{acr-sam} \DIFaddend assumes that hydraulic \gls{acr-pto} cost scales slightly less than linearly with power (exponent 0.91), and one study uses regression to obtain separate linear models for the cost of commercial induction and permanent magnet generators as a function of torque capability \citep{nakhai_techno-economic_2022}.
%the cheaper of the induction and permanent magnet machines is chosen with a torque threshold:
% \begin{equation}
%DIF <      C_{gen} = \begin{cases}
%DIF <      80.86 \tau + 1032, & 0~\leq\tau <47~\text{Nm, ~~~~induction generator} \\
%DIF <      9.151 \tau + 4386, & 47\leq\tau \leq 4000~\text{Nm, permanent magnet generator}
%DIF >      \gls{sym-C}_{gen} = \begin{cases}
%DIF >      80.86 \gls{sym-tau} + 1032, & 0~\leq\gls{sym-tau} <47~\text{Nm, ~~~~induction generator} \\
%DIF >      9.151 \gls{sym-tau} + 4386, & 47\leq\gls{sym-tau} \leq 4000~\text{Nm, permanent magnet generator}
%     \end{cases}
% \end{equation}
